**Back:** *"Denisova is a cave in Siberia, a human group, not a dinosaur or an Indian cave."*

### Flashcard ANC-1079 — Reduction sequence and lithic terms
**Front:** Define core, flake, debitage, cortex, retouch and biface, and list the reduction sequence.
**Definition (learn):** Core: stone mass from which flakes are struck. Flake: detached blank. Debitage: waste. Cortex: natural outer surface. Retouch: secondary edge modification. Biface: tool worked on two faces (many handaxes).
**Breakdown:**
- Reduction: raw-material selection → core preparation → flake/blade production → retouch, backing or hafting → use and resharpening → discard with debitage and spatial context.
- Finished tools alone are insufficient evidence.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 04 §4)
**Links:** ANC-1080, ANC-1085, ANC-1083
**Trap:** Reading finished tools as the whole technology.
**Mains hook:** Use debitage and cores to argue for in-situ manufacture at Isampur (see ANC-1082).
**Back:** *"Core gives flakes, flakes leave debitage, debitage tells the plan."*

### Flashcard ANC-1080 — Acheulian: a technological assemblage, not a race
**Front:** What is the Acheulian and what does its label not mean?
**Definition (learn):** Acheulian is a technological assemblage label strongly associated with bifacial handaxes and cleavers, plus choppers and flakes. It is not an ethnicity or hominin species.
**Breakdown:**
- Handaxes, cleavers, choppers, flakes, quarrying and resharpening.
- Old "Soanian" vs "Madrasian" opposition should not be read as racial or sealed civilisations.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 04 §5)
**Links:** ANC-1079, ANC-1081
**Trap:** "Acheulian is a race." Or "handaxes are the only Lower Palaeolithic tools."
**Mains hook:** Argue that regional assemblages are more complex than two-tradition labels.
**Back:** *"Acheulian is a toolkit with handaxes, not a people with a name."*

### Flashcard ANC-1081 — Lower Palaeolithic regional sites
**Front:** Which sites represent Lower Palaeolithic evidence, and what is each's association?
**Definition (learn):** Soan/Potwar (terrace and pebble-core traditions); Didwana, Rajasthan (lower, middle and upper Palaeolithic in a palaeolake setting); Narmada valley (widespread lithics plus Hathnora); Bhimbetka (long occupation); Hunsgi–Baichbal/Isampur, Karnataka (Acheulian landscape); Attirampakkam, Tamil Nadu (deep Acheulian sequence).
**Breakdown:**
- Soan: fluvial context needs caution.
- Isampur: manufacture and habitation.
- Attirampakkam: chronology revision.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 04 §5)
**Links:** ANC-1082, ANC-1084, ANC-1077
**Trap:** Treating Bhimbetka as only a Mesolithic site (see ANC-1094).
**Mains hook:** Use a regional list to show that Lower Palaeolithic sites are distributed across regions with different chronologies.
**Back:** *"Soan, Didwana, Narmada, Bhimbetka, Isampur, Attirampakkam — six windows."*

### Flashcard ANC-1082 — Isampur as a manufacture-and-habitation site
**Front:** What evidence makes Isampur a manufacture site, and what supports habitation?
**Definition (learn):** Isampur preserves limestone slabs, cores, flakes, unfinished tools, debitage and hammerstones, demonstrating manufacture; use-wear indicates habitation.
**Breakdown:**
- Manufacture: unfinished tools and debitage in place.
- Habitation: use-wear and other remains.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Hunsgi–Baichbal/Isampur, Karnataka (✅ owner 04 §5; Advanced 04 §1)
**Links:** ANC-1079, ANC-1081
**Trap:** "Palaeolithic India lacks manufacturing evidence." Isampur shows production contexts.
**Mains hook:** Use Isampur to argue that technological process is evidence of planning.
**Back:** *"Isampur: the factory floor is still there."*

### Flashcard ANC-1083 — Levallois: a prepared-core strategy, not a tool type
**Front:** What is the Levallois technique and why is it not a finished tool?
**Definition (learn):** A prepared-core reduction strategy: shape core convexities → prepare striking platform → predetermine desired flake → detach planned blank.
**Breakdown:**
- Middle Palaeolithic flake emphasis.
- Attirampakkam, Nevasa/Chirki-Nevasa, Didwana/Thar, Son–Belan, Retlapalle are key sites.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 04 §6)
**Links:** ANC-1079, ANC-1084
**Trap:** "Levallois is a finished tool."
**Mains hook:** Explain how planned flaking indicates cognitive planning without naming a species.
**Back:** *"Plan the core, plan the flake: Levallois is a recipe, not a tool."*

### Flashcard ANC-1084 — Attirampakkam: 385 ± 64 ka and the 385–172 ka range
**Front:** What does luminescence dating at Attirampakkam show, and when and where was it published?
**Definition (learn):** Luminescence dating places the end of the Acheulian and beginning of Middle Palaeolithic technology at about 385 ± 64 thousand years ago. Akhilesh et al. published in Nature in 2018 with the broader range 385–172 ka, using post-infrared stimulated luminescence on about 7,200 artefacts.
**Breakdown:**
- Site-specific regional sequence with an error range.
- Does not date all India or identify the tool-maker.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 385 ± 64 ka (✅ owner 04 §6); 2018 Nature, vol. 554, pp. 97–101, 31 January 2018; pIR-IRSL on ~7,200 artefacts (✅ Wikipedia: Attirampakkam)
**Links:** ANC-1074, ANC-1083, ANC-1081
**Trap:** "One all-India Middle Palaeolithic date."
**Mains hook:** Argue that a site-specific chronology revises the textbook band without becoming a national date.
**Back:** *"Three-eight-five thousand, plus or minus sixty-four: the flake moment."*

### Flashcard ANC-1085 — Upper Palaeolithic: blades, burins, bone tools and symbolism
**Front:** What are the markers of the Upper Palaeolithic and their limits?
**Definition (learn):** Parallel-sided blades (standard long edges); burins (grooving/engraving); bone tools (organic diversification); ostrich-eggshell beads and engravings (ornament, symbol); ochre, cupules and special objects (marking, ritual possibilities).
**Breakdown:**
- Flake and core tools continue.
- Preservation of organic tools is selective.
- Symbolism challenges a purely utilitarian view but does not prove a formal religion or deity.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 35,000–10,000 BCE band (✅ owner 04 §1, §7)
**Links:** ANC-1086, ANC-1089
**Trap:** "Symbolism proves a religion."
**Mains hook:** Argue for cognitive complexity without overclaiming belief.
**Back:** *"Blades cut, burins engrave, beads adorn, ochre marks."*

### Flashcard ANC-1086 — Upper Palaeolithic sites: Kurnool, Patne, Bhimbetka, Baghor, Sanghao
**Front:** Which sites carry key Upper Palaeolithic evidence?
**Definition (learn):** Kurnool caves (bone tools and faunal evidence); Patne and Bhimbetka (ostrich-eggshell beads); Baghor sites in the Son valley (manufacture and possible cult context). Sanghao cave has Middle/Upper Palaeolithic tools, hearths, animal bones and possible burials.
**Breakdown:**
- Kurnool dates around 19,224 BP and 16,686 BP (Advanced owner).
- Baghor: Palaeolithic–Mesolithic overlap.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 04 §7; Advanced 04 §4)
**Links:** ANC-1085, ANC-1094
**Trap:** Treating Baghor as only one period.
**Mains hook:** Use Baghor to show period overlaps.
**Back:** *"Kurnool bones, Patne beads, Baghor cult-maybe, Sanghao burials-maybe."*

### Flashcard ANC-1087 — Mobility and site types in the Palaeolithic
**Front:** Which site types show planned mobility, and what caution does each carry?
**Definition (learn):** Quarry/factory (extraction and reduction, may also be habitation); short camp (brief occupation, thin deposits); recurrent habitation (diverse tools, hearths, palimpsest); butchery/processing area (fauna, cut marks); rock shelter (protection, over-representation by preservation); landscape cluster (linked sites).
**Breakdown:**
- Palaeolithic communities used settlement systems, not random wandering.
- Leadership or gender cannot be read from tool size alone.
- Absence of permanent houses ≠ no social organisation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 04 §8)
**Links:** ANC-1088, ANC-1096
**Trap:** "No houses = no social organisation."
**Mains hook:** Argue for planned mobility as knowledge of water, food, stone and season.
**Back:** *"Quarry, camp, home, butchery, shelter, cluster: planned mobility."*

### Flashcard ANC-1088 — Microliths: definition and composite use
**Front:** What is a microlith and what are its types and uses?
**Definition (learn):** A small stone element, generally under about 5 cm, often on fine-grained stone. Non-geometric: blades, points, scrapers, borers. Geometric: lunates (crescents), triangles, trapezes. Used singly or in series in wood or bone for arrows, spears, knives, sickles or harpoons.
**Breakdown:**
- Small size does not automatically give a Mesolithic date.
- Microliths occur in some late-Pleistocene contexts and persist into later food-producing cultures.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 04 §10)
**Links:** ANC-1089, ANC-1075
**Trap:** "Microliths always date to the Holocene Mesolithic."
**Mains hook:** Explain why microliths mark a composite technology more than a date.
**Back:** *"Under five centimetres, mounted in a series: the composite tool."*

### Flashcard ANC-1089 — Mesolithic core features
**Front:** What are the core features of the Mesolithic?
**Definition (learn):** Broad dependence on hunting, gathering, fishing and plant use; microlithic composite tools; occupation of wetlands, plains, coasts, forests and rock shelters; mobile to semi-sedentary communities; more visible cemeteries and rock art in some regions; pottery or managed animals at some sites (debated).
**Breakdown:**
- Mobile to semi-sedentary continuum.
- Art and burials are regional, not universal.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 9000–4000 BCE (✅ owner 04 §10, §19)
**Links:** ANC-1088, ANC-1090, ANC-1093
**Trap:** "Mesolithic = domesticated animals."
**Mains hook:** Present the Mesolithic as regional experimentation, not a simple transition.
**Back:** *"Hunt, gather, fish, microlith, cemetery, art: regionally."*

### Flashcard ANC-1090 — Bagor and Adamgarh: domestication claims reported but questioned
**Front:** What do Bagor and Adamgarh report, and what is the status of those claims?
**Definition (learn):** Bagor (Rajasthan, Kothari zone): reported domestic cattle and sheep/goat, but specialists disagree over identification and later-phase interpretation. Adamgarh (near Narmada, Madhya Pradesh): reported domestic cattle, sheep, goat, pig and dog, but dates and stratigraphy weaken the early claim.
**Breakdown:**
- Use "reported but questioned" unless species ID, context and date are secure.
- Bagor long microlithic sequence; Adamgarh Palaeolithic–Mesolithic sequence.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 04 §11, §13)
**Links:** ANC-1091, ANC-1070, ANC-1104
**Trap:** "Bagor and Adamgarh prove early domestication."
**Mains hook:** Use these claims to test the Mesolithic–Neolithic boundary.
**Back:** *"Reported, questioned, not yet proved."*

### Flashcard ANC-1091 — Langhnaj: north Gujarat microliths and burials
**Front:** What does Langhnaj show and why does it matter?
**Definition (learn):** Langhnaj (north Gujarat): microliths, burials, wild fauna and some pottery; an arid/semi-arid adaptation.
**Breakdown:**
- Microlithic sequence and burials.
- Arid adaptation example.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 04 §11)
**Links:** ANC-1090, ANC-1063
**Trap:** Treating Gujarat as only a later coastal culture.
**Mains hook:** Use Langhnaj to show western Mesolithic adaptation.
**Back:** *"Langhnaj: Gujarat's dry-country hunters."*

### Flashcard ANC-1092 — Middle-Ganga Mesolithic: Sarai Nahar Rai, Mahadaha and Damdama
**Front:** What do Sarai Nahar Rai, Mahadaha and Damdama show?
**Definition (learn):** Middle-Ganga habitation-burial sites with aquatic subsistence. Sarai Nahar Rai (Pratapgarh, UP; oxbow-lake setting; geometric microliths; 11 graves including a multiple burial and a skeleton with a projectile in the ribs). Mahadaha (UP wetland zone; burials within habitation; aquatic resources). Damdama (UP; hearths, microliths, 41 burials).
**Breakdown:**
- Sarai Nahar Rai is not a handaxe factory.
- Mahadaha and Damdama challenge the older purely seasonal-migration model.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 11 graves (Sarai Nahar Rai); 41 burials (Damdama) (✅ owner 04 §11–12)
**Links:** ANC-1093, ANC-1096, ANC-1097
**Trap:** "Sarai Nahar Rai is a handaxe factory."
**Mains hook:** Use these three sites to argue for year-round or repeated residence.
**Back:** *"Sarai, Mahadaha, Damdama: homes with graves."*

### Flashcard ANC-1093 — Mesolithic radiocarbon ranges: Sarai Nahar Rai and Bagor
**Front:** What radiocarbon ranges does the Advanced owner give for Sarai Nahar Rai and Bagor?
**Definition (learn):** Sarai Nahar Rai around 9958–9059 BCE; Bagor around 5418–4936 BCE and 4575–4344 BCE. Upper also lists Bhimbetka, Baghor, Paisra, Barkhera, Adamgarh and Damdama among dated sites.
**Breakdown:**
- Regional variation in chronology is the lesson.
- Ranges are sample-specific probability spans.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 04 Advanced §4)
**Links:** ANC-1045, ANC-1092, ANC-1090
**Trap:** Reading a range as exact calendar year.
**Mains hook:** Use these ranges to refute a single Mesolithic date for India.
**Back:** *"Two Bagor ranges, one Sarai range — none is a single year."*

### Flashcard ANC-1094 — Bhimbetka rock shelters and rock art
**Front:** What is Bhimbetka's discovery history, UNESCO status and art sequence?
**Definition (learn):** Bhimbetka (Raisen district, Madhya Pradesh) preserves shelters with long occupation and multi-period paintings. V.S. Wakankar first recognised its prehistoric importance, reporting shelters in 1957; UNESCO inscribed the site in 2003. Wakankar sorted the art into seven periods.
**Breakdown:**
- Over 750 rock shelters across seven hills (Wikipedia), of which about 400 have paintings.
- Phase labels (Wikipedia, Wakankar): Upper Palaeolithic, Mesolithic, Chalcolithic, early historic (two periods), medieval (two periods).
- Owner says occupation runs from the Lower Palaeolithic; Wakankar's first period is labelled Upper Palaeolithic ⚠️ (conflict to resolve).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Wakankar 1957; UNESCO 2003 (reference 925); 750+ shelters (✅ Wikipedia: Bhimbetka rock shelters). Owner statements (✅ owner 04 §14).
**Links:** ANC-1086, ANC-1095, ANC-1081
**Trap:** "All Bhimbetka paintings are Mesolithic." Wrong; multi-period.
**Mains hook:** Use Bhimbetka to connect cognition, art, heritage and sustainable tourism.
**Back:** *"Wakankar in fifty-seven, UNESCO in oh-three: many layers, many hands."*

### Flashcard ANC-1095 — Reading rock art: superimposition, style and dating limits
**Front:** What can rock art tell, and what must be controlled?
**Definition (learn):** Subject shows hunting, animals, gathering and dance; superimposition gives relative sequence but not calendar; style and technique group regionally but can be circular; pigment science dates a related deposit; archaeological context links art to occupation.
**Breakdown:**
- Depiction is selective, not a census.
- Painted shelter may not be a habitation area.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 04 §14)
**Links:** ANC-1094, ANC-1085
**Trap:** "Rock art reads like a text."
**Mains hook:** Argue that rock art is evidence of cognition, not a written record.
**Back:** *"Superimposed, styled, dated-next-door, painted-but-not-lived-in."*

### Flashcard ANC-1096 — Semi-sedentism and the challenge to purely seasonal migration
**Front:** What evidence supports repeated or year-round residence, and what does no single marker prove?
**Definition (learn):** Thick deposits, huts, heavy grinding equipment, commensal animals, seasonality evidence and cemeteries can support repeated or year-round residence. No single marker proves permanence.
**Breakdown:**
- Mahadaha and Damdama support occupation across seasons.
- Mobility continuum rather than binary.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 04 §12)
**Links:** ANC-1092, ANC-1087
**Trap:** "Hunter-gatherers were always mobile in one way."
**Mains hook:** Use the continuum to argue for regional variation in residence.
**Back:** *"Huts, hearths, graves, heavy stones: stay, not one-marker proof."*

### Flashcard ANC-1097 — Burials: possible inferences and limits
**Front:** What inferences can burials support, and what are their limits?
**Definition (learn):** Formal cemetery within habitation (repeated residence, memory, attachment to place); body orientation (shared convention); grave goods (identity, ritual or disposal custom); multiple burial (shared event); trauma (accident or violence); unequal goods (possible distinction).
**Breakdown:**
- Burial is an action by survivors, not a biography.
- Grave goods do not prove afterlife belief or hereditary hierarchy.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 04 §12)
**Links:** ANC-1092, ANC-1098
**Trap:** "Grave goods prove afterlife belief or hereditary hierarchy."
**Mains hook:** Argue for graded interpretation of mortuary evidence.
**Back:** *"The grave records the living's choices, not the dead's biography."*

### Flashcard ANC-1098 — Transition and coexistence: from forager to food producer
**Front:** What continuities and changes mark the Palaeolithic–Mesolithic–Neolithic transition?
**Definition (learn):** The transition is changing proportions, not total replacement: continuities include hunting, gathering, fishing, microliths, seasonal mobility and rock art; changes include more composite equipment, wider niches, longer residence, formal cemeteries and debated management.
**Breakdown:**
- Foragers, herders and cultivators could coexist and exchange.
- Thresholds are regionally uneven.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 04 §15)
**Links:** ANC-1099, ANC-1102, ANC-1073
**Trap:** "Transition = one revolution on one date."
**Mains hook:** Argue for a mosaic with overlapping practices.
**Back:** *"Changing proportions, not overnight replacement."*

### Flashcard ANC-1099 — Palaeolithic vs Mesolithic comparison axes
**Front:** How do the two periods compare on geology, technology, subsistence, mobility, habitation, symbolism and food production?
**Definition (learn):** Palaeolithic: mainly Pleistocene; core, flake, blade and organic tools; hunting, gathering, fishing; mobile with recurrent places; camps, factories and shelters; ornaments, ochre, cult objects; no established general farming. Mesolithic: early/mid-Holocene; microlithic composites; broad-spectrum foraging; mobile to semi-sedentary; longer deposits, huts and cemeteries at some sites; abundant rock art in some regions; disputed management.
**Breakdown:**
- Qualification column: microliths can pre-date Holocene; older forms continue; preservation bias.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 04 §19)
**Links:** ANC-1074, ANC-1089, ANC-1098
**Trap:** Using the comparison as a strict replacement table.
**Mains hook:** Use the seven axes as a 20-mark comparison skeleton.
**Back:** *"Pleistocene tools, Holocene composites, regional everything."*

### Flashcard ANC-1100 — Prehistoric chronology traps and the 20-mark route
**Front:** Which chronological and tool traps recur, and what is the 20-mark answer route?
**Definition (learn):** Traps: phases simultaneous; Acheulian as race; Levallois as tool; microliths always Holocene; handaxes only tools; Bhimbetka only Mesolithic; Hathnora settled; Bagor and Adamgarh proven domestication; Sarai Nahar Rai a handaxe factory; grave goods as proof. The route: reject linear ladder → Lower/Middle/Upper/Mesolithic → regional dated sequences → Narmada hominin caution → operational chain and site systems → subsistence, burials and art → limits → bounded transition to topic 05.
**Breakdown:**
- Official routing: 2019 Prelims Q92 (Denisovan) is the only routed PYQ; key unavailable locally.
- Paragraph formula: claim → site/assemblage → what it establishes → dating/context limit → regional comparison → verdict.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 04 §17–19)
**Links:** ANC-1078, ANC-1098, ANC-1099
**Trap:** Presenting a single-period claim as universal.
**Mains hook:** Reject the linear ladder and give a regional verdict.
**Back:** *"No ladder, many sequences, one honest limit per site."*

### GROUP — Neolithic and Chalcolithic Cultures (owner 05)

### Flashcard ANC-1101 — Concept: neolithisation as a regionally varied transition
**Front:** What is neolithisation, and why is it not one revolution?
**Definition (learn):** Neolithisation is the regionally varied process by which communities increased dependence on cultivated plants and managed or domesticated animals, altered settlement and storage, and developed new tool and craft systems.
**Breakdown:**
- Foraging → resource management → cultivation/herding experiments → mixed food production → regionally varied village systems.
- Not instantaneous, irreversible or uniform.
- Traits (domestication, cultivation, pastoralism, sedentism, polished stone, pottery, storage) need not appear together.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 05 §1)
**Links:** ANC-1102, ANC-1103, ANC-1123
**Trap:** "Neolithic = one package."
**Mains hook:** Argue for a mosaic of pathways rather than a revolution.
**Back:** *"Not a revolution, a mosaic: some traits, not all, at some sites."*

### Flashcard ANC-1102 — Why food production emerged: multi-causal hypotheses
**Front:** Which explanatory factors are proposed for food production, and what limits each?
**Definition (learn):** Environmental change (alters plants, animals, water); population or resource pressure (intensification); knowledge accumulation (selection and tending); risk management (diversified supply); social demand (feasting, status); contact and mobility (spread of domesticates and techniques).
**Breakdown:**
- Independent development, adoption, migration and interaction are hypotheses to test, not slogans.
- Crops, animals, pottery and tools can move by different routes and at different times.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 05 §2; Advanced 05 §1)
**Links:** ANC-1101, ANC-1103, ANC-1104
**Trap:** "Farming began because of one cause."
**Mains hook:** Explain the causes as interacting factors.
**Back:** *"Environment, numbers, knowledge, risk, status, contact: six hypotheses."*

### Flashcard ANC-1103 — Evidence for domestication and cultivation
**Front:** Which plant and animal evidence supports domestication, and what caution applies?
**Definition (learn):** Plants: seed morphology, rachis and spikelet bases, phytoliths and starch, weed ecology, impressions, grinding stones. Animals: morphological change, slaughter profile, pathology or traction, dung and corrals, isotopes and DNA.
**Breakdown:**
- Phytoliths and starch show use, not cultivation.
- Grinding stones can process wild foods.
- Domestication needs multiple lines of evidence, secure stratigraphy and direct or associated dates.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 05 §3)
**Links:** ANC-1102, ANC-1109, ANC-1090
**Trap:** "Presence of a grain proves cultivation."
**Mains hook:** List the lines of evidence needed for a domestication claim.
**Back:** *"Seeds, bones, stones, weeds: all of them, dated and in place."*

### Flashcard ANC-1104 — Mehrgarh: early food production in the north-west
**Front:** What is Mehrgarh, where is it, when was it first excavated, and what does its early sequence show?
**Definition (learn):** A north-western Neolithic site in the Kachi plain of Balochistan, now in Pakistan, near routes between the Indus plains and highlands. French mission under Jean-François and Catherine Jarrige found it in 1974 and excavated 1974–1986 and 1997–2000 (Wikipedia). Earliest settlement dated from c. 7000 BCE (Wikipedia gives 7000 BCE or 5250 BCE in different estimates).
**Breakdown:**
- Early levels show wheat and barley cultivation, sheep, goats and cattle.
- Aceramic and ceramic phases; flooding and rebuilding.
- Contributed to the north-west background of pre-Harappan cultures; not a universal diffusion template.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1974 (first excavation); 7th millennium BCE onward (✅ owner 05 §4–5; ✅ Wikipedia: Mehrgarh for 1974 and 1974–86)
**Links:** ANC-1105, ANC-1119, ANC-1123
**Trap:** "Mehrgarh lies in India." It is in Baluchistan, Pakistan; use "historical subcontinental frame" with present-border caution.
**Mains hook:** Present Mehrgarh as one regional sequence, not the template for every region.
**Back:** *"Mehrgarh: Kachi plain, seventh millennium, not a template."*

### Flashcard ANC-1105 — Mehrgarh crop emphasis: barley versus wheat
**Front:** What does Wikipedia report on the crops at Mehrgarh Period I, and how does it compare with the owner's wording?
**Definition (learn):** Period I crops included six-row barley, einkorn and emmer wheat; more than 90% of Neolithic crops were barley with a small amount of wheat (Gangal et al., cited on Wikipedia). Local barley domestication is suggested by evidence; wheat varieties are suggested to be of Near-Eastern origin.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner (basic 05 §5) lists "wheat/barley cultivation"; the fetched source emphasises barley dominance ✅ (Wikipedia: Mehrgarh).
- Cattle: Wikipedia cites zebu cattle domestication evidence and dates the earliest South Asian cattle herding to c. 7000 YBP.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** >90% barley (✅ Wikipedia: Mehrgarh, citing Gangal et al.)
**Links:** ANC-1104, ANC-1103
**Trap:** Writing "wheat-dominated" Mehrgarh.
**Mains hook:** Use the barley detail to show crop packages vary by ecology.
**Back:** *"Barley first, wheat second, zebu on the side."*

### Flashcard ANC-1106 — Burzahom: Kashmir Neolithic
**Front:** What is Burzahom, where is it, who excavated it and what are its key features?
**Definition (learn):** A Kashmir Neolithic settlement near Srinagar, about 16 km north-west of the city, at roughly 1,800 m. Known for pit dwellings, stone and bone tools and burials. It is not a rock-cut shrine site.
**Breakdown:**
- Pit dwellings (circular or oval pits, some with steps); post holes suggest birch-thatch roofs.
- Bone and stone tools: harpoons, needles, awls, antler tools.
- Burials (Period II): sitting-position burials under or near dwellings; dog burials with human skeletons (Wikipedia).
- Period III Megalithic, Period IV early historic (Wikipedia).
- Excavation: trial digs by Yale–Cambridge Expedition (1936 or 1939, sources conflict); ASI 1960–1971 under T.N. Khazanchi.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 3000–1000 BCE overall occupation; ASI 1960–71 (✅ Wikipedia: Burzahom; owner 05 §6)
**Links:** ANC-1107, ANC-1108
**Trap:** "Burzahom = rock-cut shrines." (PYQ 2021 statement.)
**Mains hook:** Use Burzahom to illustrate valley-specific Neolithic adaptation.
**Back:** *"Burzahom: pits, bones, dogs — no shrine."*

### Flashcard ANC-1107 — PYQ 2021 Prelims GS-I Q37: historical place and feature pairs
**Front:** Which pairs are correct in the 2021 Prelims Q37 demand (Burzahom–rock-cut shrines; Chandraketugarh–terracotta art; Ganeshwar–copper artefacts)?
**Definition (learn):** Burzahom–rock-cut shrines is incorrect. Chandraketugarh–terracotta art is correct. Ganeshwar–copper artefacts is correct. The inferred answer is pairs 2 and 3; the official key is unavailable locally, so no official answer is claimed.
**Breakdown:**
- Burzahom: Neolithic pit structures, stone and bone tools and burials.
- Ganeshwar-Jodhpura: copper craft, Khetri ore zone.
- Chandraketugarh: early historic, terracotta art (outside this owner's full history).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2021 Prelims GS-I Q37 (✅ owner 05 §20)
**Links:** ANC-1106, ANC-1117, ANC-1025
**Trap:** Calling the inferred answer the official key.
**Mains hook:** Use the matching exercise to show that place, period and feature must be tested independently.
**Back:** *"Shrine no, terracotta yes, copper yes — pairs two and three."*

### Flashcard ANC-1108 — Gufkral and the Kashmir sequence
**Front:** What does Gufkral add to the Kashmir Neolithic?
**Definition (learn):** Gufkral provides a regional sequence for comparing aceramic and ceramic phases and changing settlement, adding to Burzahom's pit structures and surface houses in later phases.
**Breakdown:**
- Later phases: surface houses; not an instant replacement.
- Crops and animals: mixed food-producing economy; wild resources continued.
- Human and animal burials: relationship not reducible to one belief.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 05 §6)
**Links:** ANC-1106, ANC-1104
**Trap:** "Dog burial reveals one belief." It does not.
**Mains hook:** Use Kashmir to argue valley-specific adaptation.
**Back:** *"Gufkral: aceramic then ceramic, pits then houses."*

### Flashcard ANC-1109 — Koldihwa and Mahagara: the rice question
**Front:** What is the debate at Koldihwa and Mahagara?
**Definition (learn):** Neolithic–microlithic settlements with rice remains; dates, stratigraphic association and wild-versus-domesticated identification are disputed.
**Breakdown:**
- Rice presence ≠ cultivation ≠ domestication ≠ single origin.
- Method: morphology, context, direct dates, processing or field evidence, comparison.
- Use "early rice cultivation evidence" where secure.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 05 §7; Advanced 05 §1 also names Pachoh and Indari)
**Links:** ANC-1110, ANC-1103
**Trap:** "Koldihwa rice automatically proves domesticated rice."
**Mains hook:** Argue that rice debates show the need for direct dating and morphology.
**Back:** *"Rice present is not rice domesticated."*

### Flashcard ANC-1110 — Lahuradewa: the middle-Ganga rice zone
**Front:** Why does Lahuradewa matter for the Ganga-valley debate?
**Definition (learn):** Lahuradewa demonstrates the middle Ganga valley as an early centre of rice-related food production, preventing a simple north-west-to-east diffusion model.
**Breakdown:**
- Does not prove one independent domestication event.
- Does not settle all rice taxonomy.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 05 §7)
**Links:** ANC-1109, ANC-1104
**Trap:** "Lahuradewa proves a single origin for rice."
**Mains hook:** Use Lahuradewa to argue against one-way diffusion.
**Back:** *"Lahuradewa: rice in the middle Ganga, not a one-way gift."*

### Flashcard ANC-1111 — Chirand and the Eastern Neolithic
**Front:** What does Chirand (Bihar) and the eastern Neolithic show?
**Definition (learn):** Chirand preserves a long Neolithic–Chalcolithic sequence with bone tools, rice, animal and fish remains and village evidence. Senuwar and related sites trace mixed farming; Pandu Rajar Dhibi (Bengal) shows regional pottery, rice-based subsistence and post-built houses.
**Breakdown:**
- Eastern evidence combines rice, fishing, hunting, herding and craft.
- Regional Neolithic–Chalcolithic overlap.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 05 §8, §13)
**Links:** ANC-1109, ANC-1119
**Trap:** "Eastern Neolithic = one cereal economy."
**Mains hook:** Argue for mixed eastern economies.
**Back:** *"Chirand: rice, fish, bone, village — no single crop."*

### Flashcard ANC-1112 — North-east Neolithic: Daojali Hading and Sarutaru
**Front:** What are the north-eastern Neolithic references, and what caution applies?
**Definition (learn):** Daojali Hading and Sarutaru are standard north-eastern Neolithic references with ground or shouldered stone tools and pottery linking hill-valley adaptation to eastern interaction.
**Breakdown:**
- Chronology less secure than western or Gangetic sequences.
- Tool similarity with East or Southeast Asia is a comparison, not migration evidence.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 05 §8)
**Links:** ANC-1111, ANC-1032
**Trap:** Reading tool similarity as migration or ethnicity.
**Mains hook:** Use the north-east to argue against a single diffusion line.
**Back:** *"Daojali and Sarutaru: hills, polished stone, uncertain dates."*

### Flashcard ANC-1113 — South Indian Neolithic: cattle, ground stone and mixed mobility
**Front:** What characterises the South Indian Neolithic?
**Definition (learn):** Cattle pastoralism, cultivation, ground stone tools, pottery, seasonal movement and more persistent settlements, forming a distinct pathway rather than a copy of Mehrgarh.
**Breakdown:**
- Key sites: Utnur (ash mound, cattle); Piklihal (cattle herding, camps and cowpens); Sanganakallu–Kupgal (settlement, quarrying, cattle, ritualised landscape); Budihal (ash mound with habitation and production); Tekkalakota, Hallur, Brahmagiri (regional sequences).
- Ash mounds are a distinctive trait; function is debated.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 05 §9)
**Links:** ANC-1114, ANC-1104
**Trap:** "South Indian Neolithic = Mehrgarh copy."
**Mains hook:** Argue for a distinct southern pathway.
**Back:** *"Cattle, ash, ground stone: the south keeps its own time."*

### Flashcard ANC-1114 — The ash-mound debate
**Front:** What explanations are proposed for ash mounds, and what caution applies?
**Definition (learn):** Ash mounds contain burnt cattle dung and settlement remains. Explanations: periodic burning of dung in cattle pens; sanitation, renewal or pest control; communal aggregation, feasting or ritual; repeated practical and symbolic actions.
**Breakdown:**
- No one function for every mound.
- Formation process, settlement and chronology must be studied site by site.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 05 §9)
**Links:** ANC-1113, ANC-1070
**Trap:** "All ash mounds had one ritual purpose."
**Mains hook:** Use the four explanations to show interpretive humility.
**Back:** *"Burn, clean, gather, ritualise — site by site."*

### Flashcard ANC-1115 — Chalcolithic: meaning and limits
**Front:** What does "Chalcolithic" mean, and what does it not guarantee?
**Definition (learn):** Chalcolithic means a copper-and-stone technological setting: copper appears alongside continued stone, bone and wooden tools.
**Breakdown:**
- Does mean: copper objects in village assemblages; farming and herding with pottery traditions; craft and exchange networks; some settlement hierarchy; overlap with Neolithic or Harappan.
- Does not guarantee: exclusive copper, iron, writing, planned cities, uniform urbanism, one ethnic community.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 05 §11)
**Links:** ANC-1116, ANC-1124
**Trap:** "Chalcolithic = urban copper civilisation."
**Mains hook:** Use the does/does-not table to define the period in one sentence.
**Back:** *"Copper and stone, not city and metal empire."*

### Flashcard ANC-1116 — Ahar-Banas: south-east Rajasthan copper culture
**Front:** What is the Ahar-Banas culture and its key sites?
**Definition (learn):** A south-east Rajasthan Chalcolithic culture with copper, black-and-red ware, farming and herding and settlement diversity. Key sites: Ahar, Gilund, Balathal.
**Breakdown:**
- Not a Harappan city culture.
- Link to Khetri and Harappan exchange.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 05 §12, §22)
**Links:** ANC-1117, ANC-1124
**Trap:** "Black-and-red ware = same people."
**Mains hook:** Use Ahar to show regional exchange without political absorption.
**Back:** *"Ahar, Gilund, Balathal: copper in the Aravalli south-east."*

### Flashcard ANC-1117 — Ganeshwar-Jodhpura: the Khetri copper-craft complex
**Front:** What is Ganeshwar-Jodhpura, and why is it not a Harappan city?
**Definition (learn):** A large copper assemblage and craft-production complex in the Khetri copper-belt zone of Rajasthan, with Harappan contact. Associated with copper artefacts (PYQ 2021 Q37 route).
**Breakdown:**
- Copper zone is distinct from uniform Harappan control.
- Should not be collapsed into OCP.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 05 §12, §17, §21)
**Links:** ANC-1107, ANC-1120
**Trap:** "Ganeshwar = Harappan city." Copper-craft complex with contacts.
**Mains hook:** Use Ganeshwar to show that exchange does not prove absorption.
**Back:** *"Ganeshwar: copper craft near Khetri, not a city."*

### Flashcard ANC-1118 — Kayatha, Malwa and Savalda: the broad textbook bands
**Front:** What are Kayatha, Malwa and Savalda, and what are their broad bands?
**Definition (learn):** Kayatha (western Madhya Pradesh; early Chalcolithic horizon; distinctive pottery and prestige copper), Malwa (Navdatoli, Eran, Nagda; painted pottery, farming and craft), Savalda (Tapi valley and northern Maharashtra; early western-Deccan phase). Broad bands from Sharma: Kayatha c. 2000–1500 BCE; Malwa c. 1700–1200 BCE.
**Breakdown:**
- Regional spread ≠ one polity.
- Savalda's relation to Malwa/Jorwe must be site-based.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 05 §12)
**Links:** ANC-1119, ANC-1121
**Trap:** "Ahar, Kayatha, Malwa and Jorwe are one culture."
**Mains hook:** Use the bands as scaffolding, not exact dates.
**Back:** *"Kayatha early, Malwa painted, Savalda Tapi-side."*

### Flashcard ANC-1119 — Jorwe: Maharashtra and Inamgaon/Daimabad
**Front:** What characterises the Jorwe culture and its key sites?
**Definition (learn):** Maharashtra Chalcolithic with painted pottery, farming, crafts and settlement hierarchy. Key sites: Jorwe, Nevasa, Inamgaon, Daimabad, Prakash. Jorwe c. 1400–700 BCE (Sharma band).
**Breakdown:**
- Inamgaon and Daimabad were unusually large within rural networks; not every Jorwe site is urban.
- Late Jorwe change requires multi-causal explanation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 1400–700 BCE (✅ owner 05 §12, §15)
**Links:** ANC-1122, ANC-1115
**Trap:** "Large Inamgaon makes all Jorwe sites urban."
**Mains hook:** Use Inamgaon to argue for varied site sizes and functions.
**Back:** *"Jorwe: painted, large, but mostly rural."*

### Flashcard ANC-1120 — OCP and copper hoards: association without ethnic labels
**Front:** What are OCP and copper hoards, and what safe distinctions apply?
**Definition (learn):** Ochre-Coloured Pottery (OCP) occurs in the upper Ganga region in late third and second millennium BCE contexts; copper hoards include distinctive copper artefacts over a wider distribution.
**Breakdown:**
- OCP settlements and copper hoards overlap partly but are not identical datasets.
- Hoards may be stray finds without habitation context.
- Copper hoards do not identify Aryans, a race or a migrating people.
- Association requires stratigraphy, metallurgy and settlement evidence.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 05 §17)
**Links:** ANC-1117, ANC-1166, ANC-1032
**Trap:** "Copper hoard = OCP = one ethnic group."
**Mains hook:** Argue that partial association is the honest position.
**Back:** *"Pottery partly meets copper; neither names a people."*

### Flashcard ANC-1121 — Kayatha affluence and child burials: differentiation without a class system
**Front:** What social evidence suggests differentiation, and what does it not prove?
**Definition (learn):** Unequal house size and location; storage concentration; differential grave goods; rare copper and ornaments; specialised craft areas; centrality within exchange networks. At Kayatha, concentrated copper bangles and axes and bead necklaces suggest affluent households. Child burials with unequal ornaments in western Maharashtra indicate difference, not a complete class system.
**Breakdown:**
- Differentiation can be inferred; hereditary caste, kingship or state need stronger evidence.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 05 §15)
**Links:** ANC-1118, ANC-1122
**Trap:** "Grave goods prove hereditary rank."
**Mains hook:** Argue for differentiation as a graded inference.
**Back:** *"Affluent households, unequal children, no proven caste."*

### Flashcard ANC-1122 — Late Jorwe and Chalcolithic decline: which variable declined?
**Front:** What changes mark the late Chalcolithic decline, and what must a "decline" specify?
**Definition (learn):** Possible changes: contraction or abandonment of large settlements; movement to smaller sites; pastoral mobility; altered crop and water strategy; trade and resource shifts; conflict, disease or reorganisation; environmental stress.
**Breakdown:**
- Aridity has been discussed at Inamgaon and in Late Jorwe, but no single cause.
- Specify the variable: site size, population, craft, exchange, storage or integration.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 05 §18)
**Links:** ANC-1119, ANC-1070, ANC-1151
**Trap:** "Decline = disappearance."
**Mains hook:** Read reorganisation as adaptation, not extinction.
**Back:** *"Name the variable that fell, then the cause that fits it."*

### Flashcard ANC-1123 — Relationship between Neolithic-Chalcolithic and Harappan cultures
**Front:** How should the relation between rural Neolithic-Chalcolithic cultures and Harappan cities be read?
**Definition (learn):** Pre-Harappan food-producing communities contributed regional backgrounds in the north-west; rural Neolithic-Chalcolithic communities existed alongside urban systems; exchange (Ganeshwar/Ahar) does not prove absorption; post-Harappan cultures followed urban decline but were not all descended from it; south and eastern cultures had independent trajectories.
**Breakdown:**
- Later Iron Age formations did not replace every copper-stone village at once.
- Neolithic → Chalcolithic → Harappan → Iron Age is not a universal ladder.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 05 §19)
**Links:** ANC-1101, ANC-1104, ANC-1117, ANC-1151
**Trap:** "Neolithic → Chalcolithic → Harappan → Iron Age is a ladder."
**Mains hook:** Argue for overlapping formations.
**Back:** *"Overlap, not a ladder: rural villages lived beside the cities."*

### Flashcard ANC-1124 — Chalcolithic traps and the 15-mark matrix
**Front:** Which Chalcolithic traps recur, and how should a comparative matrix be built?
**Definition (learn):** Traps: Ahar, Kayatha, Malwa and Jorwe are one culture; Chalcolithic = urban copper civilisation; same ware = same people; copper hoard = OCP = ethnic group; Inamgaon makes all Jorwe urban. Matrix axes: core zone, markers, link, limit for Ahar-Banas, Kayatha/Malwa, Jorwe and eastern complexes.
**Breakdown:**
- 15-mark route: reject single revolution or ladder → domestication evidence → regional Neolithic comparison → Chalcolithic matrix → production, craft and social consequences → source and chronology limits.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 05 §21–22)
**Links:** ANC-1115, ANC-1118, ANC-1120
**Trap:** Compressing all Chalcolithic cultures into one entry.
**Mains hook:** Write the matrix for two cultures in four lines each.
**Back:** *"Five traps, four axes, one verdict: mosaic."*

### GROUP — The Harappan Civilization (owners 06 basic and advanced)

### Flashcard ANC-1125 — Concept: the Harappan civilisation as an archaeological reconstruction
**Front:** Why is Harappan history reconstructed mainly from archaeology, and what is it strongest and weakest at?
**Definition (learn):** The Harappan (Indus) civilisation is known from material remains because its script is undeciphered. Material evidence is strongest for construction, production, diet, exchange and settlement; weakest for named rulers, language, law and personal belief.
**Breakdown:**
- "Harappan" is safer than "Indus Valley", since the distribution extended far beyond the Indus valley.
- Functional labels (granary, dockyard, citadel, priest-king) are interpretations.
- Advanced: every "fact" is an archaeological interpretation (owner Advanced 06 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 06 §1; Advanced 06 §1)
**Links:** ANC-1126, ANC-1148, ANC-1150
**Trap:** "Indus Valley civilisation" as the only name.
**Mains hook:** Argue that the undeciphered script shapes every claim about polity and religion.
**Back:** *"No script, so the trowel speaks: Harappan, not Indus-only."*

### Flashcard ANC-1126 — Discovery timeline and the 1853 seal dispute
**Front:** What is the early discovery sequence, and where do the sources disagree?
**Definition (learn):** Owner: Cunningham noticed a Harappan seal in 1853; Sahni began excavation at Harappa in 1921; Banerji worked at Mohenjo-daro in the 1920s and Marshall announced the civilisation.
**Breakdown:**
- Wikipedia (Indus Valley Civilisation): Cunningham visited Harappa in 1853 but published his seal interpretation in 1875; the seal-noticed-in-1853 claim is not confirmed there ⚠️ (owner-vs-source conflict).
- Wikipedia: Banerji visited Mohenjo-daro in 1919–20 and 1922–23; the discovery year is not stated.
- Wikipedia: Sahni's excavation year is not stated in the fetched text; the 1921 start is owner-only ⚠️.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1853 (owner) vs 1875 publication (✅ Wikipedia: Indus Valley Civilisation); 1921 (owner, unconfirmed)
**Links:** ANC-1127, ANC-1125
**Trap:** Stating "Cunningham identified the civilisation in 1853."
**Mains hook:** Show that early recognition was piecemeal and that dates should be cited with their source.
**Back:** *"Fifty-three visited, seventy-five published: check the seal date."*

### Flashcard ANC-1127 — Marshall's 1924 announcement and the Banerji–Sahni–Marshall triangle
**Front:** When and where did John Marshall announce the civilisation, and what roles did Banerji and Sahni play?
**Definition (learn):** Marshall announced the discovery on 24 September 1924 in the *Illustrated London News* (✅ Wikipedia). Rakhaldas (R.D.) Banerji recognised Mohenjo-daro's high antiquity; Marshall directed Sahni to excavate Harappa's two mounds (✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Owner says Marshall "announced" in the 1920s; the exact date is 24 September 1924.
- Mohenjo-daro's large-scale digs: Dikshit 1924–25; Marshall 1925–26 (✅ Wikipedia: Mohenjo-daro).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 24 September 1924 (✅ Wikipedia: Indus Valley Civilisation)
**Links:** ANC-1126, ANC-1128, ANC-1129
**Trap:** "Marshall discovered the civilisation alone." Banerji and Sahni did field work.
**Mains hook:** Credit the early excavators without making them the sole discoverers.
**Back:** *"Marshall spoke in 'twenty-four; Banerji and Sahni dug."*

### Flashcard ANC-1128 — Harappa: the type-site, Cemetery R-37 and the "granary"
**Front:** Why is Harappa the type-site, and what are its key facts?
**Definition (learn):** Harappa (Ravi region, Punjab, Pakistan) gave the name "Harappan" as the first excavated type-site. Features: multiple mounds, working platforms, cemetery and a 12-unit building. The "granary" is a conventional functional label.
**Breakdown:**
- Cemetery R-37 was discovered in 1937–38 by K.N. Sastri with H.K. Bose across four seasons ending 1940–41; roughly fifty Harappan burials, never fully published (✅ Wikipedia: Harappa, citing Possehl 1991).
- Harappa "granary" disputed; see ANC-1133.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Cemetery R-37 1937–38 (✅ Wikipedia: Harappa); 12-unit building (✅ owner 06 §16)
**Links:** ANC-1127, ANC-1133, ANC-1154
**Trap:** "Harappa was a granary city." Conventional label only.
**Mains hook:** Use Harappa's excavation history to show how a type-site shaped the name of a whole civilisation.
**Back:** *"Harappa names it; R-37 buries it; the granary is a guess."*

### Flashcard ANC-1129 — Mohenjo-daro: excavation history and key facts
**Front:** What are Mohenjo-daro's excavation dates and main architecture?
**Definition (learn):** Mohenjo-daro (Sindh, Pakistan) excavations: Dikshit 1924–25; Marshall 1925–26; Mackay (1927–31 account); Marshall, Dikshit and Mackay in the 1930s; Wheeler 1945; Dales 1964–65; excavation banned after 1965 (✅ Wikipedia: Mohenjo-daro).
**Breakdown:**
- Architecture: Great Bath, wells, drains, courtyard houses, public structures.
- Owner: Mohenjo-daro with Great Bath, wells, drains, bronze figurine and male bust.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1924–25 Dikshit; 1925–26 Marshall; 1945 Wheeler; 1964–65 Dales (✅ Wikipedia)
**Links:** ANC-1130, ANC-1131, ANC-1132
**Trap:** Treating "priest-king", "college" and "assembly hall" as settled names.
**Mains hook:** Use Mohenjo-daro as the test case for functional labels.
**Back:** *"Dikshit twenty-four, Marshall twenty-five, Wheeler forty-five, Dales sixty-four."*

### Flashcard ANC-1130 — The Dancing Girl: bronze, 1926, Mohenjo-daro
**Front:** What is the "Dancing Girl," what is it made of, and when was it found?
**Definition (learn):** A bronze statuette about 10.5 cm tall, found in 1926 in the "HR area" of Mohenjo-daro; held in the National Museum, New Delhi (✅ Wikipedia: Mohenjo-daro).
**Breakdown:**
- Material: bronze (PYQ 2025 Prelims Q72; official Set-A key available).
- Pose and name are modern description; not a portrait biography.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1926; 10.5 cm; bronze (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 06 §20 PYQ)
**Links:** ANC-1154, ANC-1131
**Trap:** Answering "stone" or "terracotta" for the 2025 statement.
**Mains hook:** Use the figurine to show the difference between material fact and interpretive name.
**Back:** *"Dancing Girl: bronze, twenty-six, ten centimetres."*

### Flashcard ANC-1131 — The "Priest-King": soapstone bust, 1927
**Front:** What is the "Priest-King" bust and when was it found?
**Definition (learn):** A seated male soapstone figure about 17.5 cm tall found in 1927 at Mohenjo-daro; in the National Museum, Karachi (✅ Wikipedia). Priest, king and identity are unknown.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: stone male bust; priest, king and identity unknown.
- Wikipedia: no evidence that priests or monarchs ruled the city.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1927; 17.5 cm; soapstone (✅ Wikipedia)
**Links:** ANC-1130, ANC-1149
**Trap:** "Priest-King identifies a ruler."
**Mains hook:** Use the bust to argue that kingship is not attested.
**Back:** *"Seated in twenty-seven, king in name only."*

### Flashcard ANC-1132 — The Great Bath: secure architecture
**Front:** What is secure about the Great Bath, and what function is only possible?
**Definition (learn):** A large public bath at Mohenjo-daro: about 12 m long, 7 m wide and 2.4 m deep; steps descend into a brick pool sealed with bitumen; baked brick and gypsum mortar (owner); possibly religious purification (Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Secure: waterproof public architecture.
- Possible: collective or ritual bathing.
- Not proven: temple or one religion.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 12 m × 7 m × 2.4 m; bitumen (✅ Wikipedia: Mohenjo-daro; baked brick and gypsum ✅ owner 06 §6)
**Links:** ANC-1145, ANC-1149
**Trap:** "Great Bath = temple."
**Mains hook:** Argue for waterproof collective architecture; keep ritual as hypothesis.
**Back:** *"Twelve by seven by two-point-four, bitumen-sealed, not yet a temple."*

### Flashcard ANC-1133 — The "Great Granary" label: Wheeler and Kenoyer
**Front:** Why is the "granary" label disputed?
**Definition (learn):** Wheeler named Mohenjo-daro's building the "Great Granary" in 1950; Kenoyer noted no evidence of grain there and suggested "Great Hall" of uncertain function (✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Harappa's granary is also a conventional label (owner).
- Functional labels need inscriptions or residues to be secure; neither exists.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Wheeler 1950 (✅ Wikipedia: Mohenjo-daro)
**Links:** ANC-1128, ANC-1129, ANC-1150
**Trap:** "Granaries are proven by inscriptions."
**Mains hook:** Use granary and dockyard labels to argue for qualified language.
**Back:** *"Wheeler said granary in 'fifty; Kenoyer asked, where's the grain?"*

### Flashcard ANC-1134 — Harappan chronology: Early, Mature and Late phases
**Front:** What are the three phases and their processes?
**Definition (learn):** Early Harappan c. 3300–2600 BCE (regionalisation and proto-urban growth). Mature Harappan c. 2600–1900 BCE (urban integration: cities, common sign system, seals, standard weights, brick norms, wide exchange). Late Harappan c. 1900–1300 BCE, varying regionally (localisation and deurbanisation).
**Breakdown:**
- Process phases, not three sealed peoples.
- Beginnings and endings vary by site.
- Advanced: Early (Kot Diji, Amri) regional and proto-urban.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 3300–2600; 2600–1900; 1900–1300 BCE (✅ owner 06 §2)
**Links:** ANC-1125, ANC-1151, ANC-1155
**Trap:** "Harappa ended abruptly around 1900 BCE."
**Mains hook:** Use regionalisation → integration → localisation as the spine for a 20-mark answer.
**Back:** *"Three-three to two-six, two-six to nineteen, nineteen to thirteen."*

### Flashcard ANC-1135 — Extent: the four cardinal extremes
**Front:** What are the four cardinal extremes of Harappan distribution?
**Definition (learn):** West Sutkagen-dor on the Makran coast; north Manda (Jammu region); east Alamgirpur (western Uttar Pradesh); south Malvan (southern Gujarat). Daimabad preserves southern Late-Harappan or contact evidence and should not be used as a Mature extreme.
**Breakdown:**
- Indus core: Harappa, Mohenjo-daro.
- Ghaggar-Hakra: Kalibangan, Banawali, Rakhigarhi.
- Kutch, Saurashtra, Gujarat: Dholavira, Lothal, Surkotada, Desalpur, Rangpur.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 06 §3)
**Links:** ANC-1136, ANC-1137
**Trap:** "Harappa = Indus valley only." Extent crosses basins, highlands and coasts.
**Mains hook:** Use the extent to argue for multi-ecological coordination.
**Back:** *"Sutkagen west, Manda north, Alamgirpur east, Malvan south."*

### Flashcard ANC-1136 — Shortugai: a north-west outpost near mineral routes
**Front:** What is Shortugai and what does it not prove?
**Definition (learn):** A Harappan outpost in Afghanistan near highland mineral routes; raw-material procurement and route control.
**Breakdown:**
- Does not prove political annexation.
- Example of outposts and resource nodes.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 06 §4, §16)
**Links:** ANC-1135, ANC-1137
**Trap:** "Outpost = colony."
**Mains hook:** Argue for procurement networks rather than annexation.
**Back:** *"Shortugai buys stone, not a kingdom."*

### Flashcard ANC-1137 — Settlement hierarchy: cities, regional centres, specialist towns, villages, outposts
**Front:** What are the five settlement roles in the urban-rural system?
**Definition (learn):** Large cities (Harappa, Mohenjo-daro, Dholavira, Rakhigarhi, Ganweriwala); regional centres (Kalibangan, Banawali); specialist centres (Chanhudaro, Nageshwar, Lothal); villages and farmsteads (Allahdino as a wealthy small example); outposts and resource nodes (Shortugai, coastal and quarry sites).
**Breakdown:**
- Urban civilisation does not mean every settlement was a city.
- Standardisation implies coordination, not necessarily a centralised empire.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 06 §4)
**Links:** ANC-1135, ANC-1136, ANC-1142
**Trap:** "Every Harappan settlement was a city."
**Mains hook:** Use the hierarchy to argue for a rural base supporting urban centres.
**Back:** *"Cities, regional towns, specialists, villages, outposts: five roles."*

### Flashcard ANC-1138 — Dholavira: excavation, UNESCO and the signboard
**Front:** Who excavated Dholavira, when was it inscribed on the World Heritage List, and what is its signboard?
**Definition (learn):** Dholavira (Khadir island, Kutch, Gujarat) excavated by the ASI under Ravindra Singh Bisht from 1990 (13 field seasons to 2005; Wikipedia). UNESCO inscription 27 July 2021 as "Dholavira: A Harappan City". Signboard of ten gypsum signs, about 37 cm high, over about 3 m long (Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Multi-part plan with stone construction and enclosed sectors.
- Corrects the universal two-part citadel/lower-town model.
- "Stadium" is interpretive (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1990–2005; 27 July 2021; ten signs (✅ Wikipedia: Dholavira)
**Links:** ANC-1139, ANC-1137, ANC-1154
**Trap:** Describing the signboard as deciphered.
**Mains hook:** Use Dholavira to argue against one template for Harappan towns.
**Back:** *"Bisht dug, UNESCO agreed in twenty-one, ten signs unread."*

### Flashcard ANC-1139 — Dholavira's reservoirs and the 2021 water-harvesting PYQ
**Front:** What water system does Dholavira show, and what PYQ does it answer?
**Definition (learn):** Dholavira's dams, channels, cisterns and reservoirs between seasonal streams; Wikipedia records "sixteen or more reservoirs" in Stage III and a rock-cut reservoir about 7 m deep and 79 m long. The 2021 Prelims Q32 asked for the town known for dams, channels and connected reservoirs; the inferred answer is Dholavira; official key unavailable locally.
**Breakdown:**
- Check dams on the Manhar and Mansar streams (Wikipedia).
- Stepwell excavation began in October 2014 (Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** "sixteen or more" reservoirs; 7 m deep, 79 m long (✅ Wikipedia: Dholavira)
**Links:** ANC-1138, ANC-1145, ANC-1154
**Trap:** Calling the inferred 2021 key official.
**Mains hook:** Use Dholavira as the example of civic water management.
**Back:** *"Sixteen reservoirs, check dams, one inferred key."*

### Flashcard ANC-1140 — Lothal: the dock controversy
**Front:** What is Lothal, who found and excavated it, and what is the dock debate?
**Definition (learn):** Lothal (Dholka taluka, Ahmedabad district, Gujarat, in the Bhal region): ASI found the site in 1954; digging 13 February 1955 to 19 May 1960; resumed 1961 (Wikipedia). A trapezoidal burnt-brick basin about 215 m north–south and 35 m east–west, with an inlet and sluice; interpreted as a dock by Rao and the ASI; as a tank by Leshnik (1968) and Yule (1982); marine traces by the National Institute of Oceanography (Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Harappan phase dated 2400–1900 BCE (Wikipedia).
- Floods c. 2350 BCE and c. 1900 BCE; river shift c. 2000 BCE.
- Owner: "dockyard" is leading but contested; Wadi al-Jarf harbour predates it (Wikipedia notes Khufu's older harbour).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1954 found; 1955–60 excavated; 215 × 35 m (✅ Wikipedia: Lothal)
**Links:** ANC-1133, ANC-1154
**Trap:** "Lothal was a proven dockyard."
**Mains hook:** Argue that the basin's function is an open question with evidence on both sides.
**Back:** *"Fifty-four found, fifty-five to sixty dug, dock or tank still argued."*

### Flashcard ANC-1141 — Kalibangan: fortified sectors, ploughed field and fire altars
**Front:** Who excavated Kalibangan, when, and what are its key finds?
**Definition (learn):** Kalibangan (Hanumangarh district, Rajasthan; on the Ghaggar's southern bank): ASI dig led by B.B. Lal with B.K. Thapar and others, 1960–61 to 1969–70; full report only in 2003 (Wikipedia). Early Harappan ploughed field c. 2800 BCE with grid furrows about 30 cm east–west and about 190 cm north–south (Wikipedia). Harappan-phase fire altars on the citadel, lower-town household altars and a separate structure.
**Breakdown:**
- Drainage differed from Mohenjo-daro (owner).
- Fire does not prove Vedic ritual.
- Wikipedia's claim that Kalibangan lacks mother-goddess worship is not in the owner ⚠️ (not verified here).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1960–70 excavation; c. 2800 BC ploughed field (✅ Wikipedia: Kalibangan)
**Links:** ANC-1137, ANC-1145, ANC-1149
**Trap:** "Fire altars = Vedic altars."
**Mains hook:** Use the ploughed field to show early agriculture in the Ghaggar zone.
**Back:** *"Ploughed field before the fort, fire but not Vedic."*

### Flashcard ANC-1142 — Chanhudaro: a bead and seal craft town without a citadel
**Front:** Who excavated Chanhudaro, when, and what made it a specialist centre?
**Definition (learn):** Chanhudaro (Sindh, about 130 km south of Mohenjo-daro): N.G. Majumdar investigated it in 1931; Ernest Mackay led the American School team in 1935–36 (Wikipedia). Bead-making workshop with a furnace; shell bangles; steatite seal making; copper tools (Mackay's "Sheffield of the British Empire").
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: unfortified specialist craft town; no conventional citadel.
- Wikipedia silent on fortification ⚠️ (only the layout comparison is noted).
- French mission under Aurore Didier since 2015 (Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1931 (Majumdar); 1935–36 (Mackay) (✅ Wikipedia: Chanhu-daro)
**Links:** ANC-1137, ANC-1154
**Trap:** "Chanhudaro had a citadel."
**Mains hook:** Use Chanhudaro to argue that urban production existed without a citadel.
**Back:** *"Thirty-one found, Mackay's bead factory, no citadel."*

### Flashcard ANC-1143 — Rakhigarhi: the large inland centre and its excavators
**Front:** Where is Rakhigarhi, who excavated it, and what is the caution for size rankings?
**Definition (learn):** Rakhigarhi (Hansi district, Haryana): ASI began digging in 1963; Kurukshetra University (Suraj Bhan) in 1969; ASI under Amarendra Nath 1997–2000; Deccan College under Vasant Shinde 2011–16; ASI resumed 2021 (✅ Wikipedia: Rakhigarhi).
**Breakdown:**
- Large inland centre with craft, drains and cemetery (owner).
- Size rankings and genetic generalisations need caution.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1963; 1997–2000; 2011–16; 2021 onward (✅ Wikipedia)
**Links:** ANC-1153, ANC-1135
**Trap:** "Rakhigarhi = all Harappans."
**Mains hook:** Use the excavation chain to show how a site's data accumulate across decades.
**Back:** *"Sixty-three dug, Shinde twenty-eleven, twenty-one resumed."*

### Flashcard ANC-1144 — Surkotada and the disputed horse
**Front:** What is the status of the Surkotada equid (horse) claim?
**Definition (learn):** Surkotada (Kutch, Gujarat) is a fortified settlement with reported equid bones. Species, context and wider significance are disputed; the horse was not securely central to the Harappan economy.
**Breakdown:**
- "Horse present" ≠ "horse economically central."
- Desalpur (Kutch) is a fortified Harappan site; distinguish from non-Harappan Sohgaura.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 06 §7, §16)
**Links:** ANC-1154, ANC-1166
**Trap:** "Surkotada proves a horse-centred civilisation."
**Mains hook:** Use the Surkotada horse to show the difference between presence and centrality.
**Back:** *"Equid bones, horse claims, not a horse civilisation."*

### Flashcard ANC-1145 — Drainage, water and civic maintenance
**Front:** What secure evidence shows Harappan water management, and what does it not reveal?
**Definition (learn):** House bathing floors linked to street drains at several sites; covered drains, soak jars and inspection arrangements; public, private and neighbourhood wells (especially Mohenjo-daro); Great Bath waterproofing; Dholavira reservoirs; site-specific flood control.
**Breakdown:**
- Drains require cleaning and collective norms; architecture supports organised civic maintenance.
- Does not reveal the constitutional form of government.
- Kalibangan, Dholavira, Lothal and Mohenjo-daro used different solutions.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 06 §6)
**Links:** ANC-1132, ANC-1139, ANC-1141
**Trap:** "Sophisticated drainage proves a uniform state."
**Mains hook:** Argue for organised maintenance without inferring a constitution.
**Back:** *"Bathing floors meet drains; drains need hands, not a king."*

### Flashcard ANC-1146 — Weights, measures and the absence of coinage
**Front:** What do standard weights and measures show, and why is coinage not proved?
**Definition (learn):** Carefully graded stone weights supported exchange; shell and ivory scales with measurement markings show linear measurement; mature brick ratios (1:2:4) and seal forms show shared technical norms. No Harappan metal coinage is securely known.
**Breakdown:**
- Do not reduce the system to one "multiple of 16" rule (owner).
- Standard weights ≠ coinage.
- Advanced: uniform weights, bricks and seals across about 1 million sq km imply shared norms (Advanced 06 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Brick ratio 1:2:4 (✅ owner 06 §9); about 1 million sq km (✅ owner Advanced 06 §1)
**Links:** ANC-1134, ANC-1147, ANC-1150
**Trap:** "Standard weights prove coins."
**Mains hook:** Argue for coordination of exchange without coinage or a named empire.
**Back:** *"Weights weigh, bricks obey, seals agree; no coin yet."*

### Flashcard ANC-1147 — Trade: internal network and Mesopotamian Meluhha, Dilmun and Magan
**Front:** Which resources moved within the Harappan network, and which Mesopotamian terms describe long-distance links?
**Definition (learn):** Internal network: copper, tin, metals, lapis, carnelian, shell, timber, food, beads, pottery, seals. Mesopotamian terms: Meluhha (probably the Indus region or wider eastern zone); Dilmun (Gulf intermediary, commonly Bahrain); Magan/Makan (Oman or Makran-linked zone).
**Breakdown:**
- Advanced: lapis from Afghanistan, tin and copper; procurement-driven economy.
- Imported objects prove contact, not colonies or trade volume.
- Lothal's basin supports maritime orientation but the dock label stays qualified.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 06 §10; Advanced 06 §1)
**Links:** ANC-1140, ANC-1146, ANC-1166
**Trap:** "Imports prove a Harappan colony."
**Mains hook:** Use Meluhha to argue that contact and procurement are the safe claims.
**Back:** *"Meluhha, Dilmun, Magan: contact, not conquest."*

### Flashcard ANC-1148 — Script: undeciphered, short and seal-bound
**Front:** What is secure about the Harappan script, and which numbers differ between sources?
**Definition (learn):** The sign system remains undeciphered; inscriptions are short, mostly on seals, sealings, pottery and tablets; language and sign values are unknown.
**Breakdown:**
- Basic owner: "several hundred" signs, depending on variant counting; inscriptions generally run right-to-left, with some other layouts.
- Advanced owner: about 400–450 basic signs; average about 5 signs; only some multi-line texts use boustrophedon.
- The two owner counts differ; flag the difference in revision ⚠️.
- "Logo-syllabic" is a proposal, not a decipherment.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 06 §11; Advanced 06 §1)
**Links:** ANC-1125, ANC-1154
**Trap:** "Harappan script language is known." Or "the whole script is boustrophedon."
**Mains hook:** Argue that writing is undeciphered, so polity and religion remain interpretations.
**Back:** *"Short, sealed, unread: count the signs, don't name the language."*

### Flashcard ANC-1149 — Art, religion and the contested labels
**Front:** Which contested labels attach to Harappan art and religion, and what is secure?
**Definition (learn):** "Dancing Girl" (bronze, Mohenjo-daro; pose and name modern); "Priest-King" (stone bust; identity unknown); female figurines (not one automatic Mother Goddess cult); seals with animals, composite beings and signs; the horned seated figure labelled "Pashupati" or proto-Shiva (contested); pipal and animal motifs; fire installations; possible linga-like stones (later identification uncertain).
**Breakdown:**
- No monumental temple or unified state religion securely identified.
- Burials give mortuary custom but no single afterlife doctrine.
- Advanced: "Pashupati" seal contested between Marshall's proto-Shiva and a horned deity or mother-goddess reading.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 06 §12, §15; Advanced 06 §2)
**Links:** ANC-1130, ANC-1131, ANC-1132
**Trap:** "Pashupati seal is definitely Shiva." Or "female figurines prove a Mother Goddess."
**Mains hook:** Argue for interpretation without back-projection.
**Back:** *"Horned seal, female figure, Great Bath: visions, not creeds."*

### Flashcard ANC-1150 — Political organisation: three models without invented kings
**Front:** What three political models are proposed, and what is the safe claim?
**Definition (learn):** No securely identified royal tombs, king lists, palaces, monumental temples or named dynasties. Models: centralised state or empire (standardisation, planning, seals); several regional states or city-states (regional centres, layout variation); corporate or decentralised elites (limited royal display, collective standards). Safe claim: strong coordination across urban and exchange systems, with debated political form.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: "ruling elite" inferred from citadel, granaries, standardisation and hoarded valuables (Advanced 06 §1).
- Egypt and Mesopotamia contrast: no royal tombs.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 06 §14; Advanced 06 §1)
**Links:** ANC-1146, ANC-1151, ANC-1133
**Trap:** "Harappan state was a centralised monarchy like Egypt."
**Mains hook:** Argue that coordination is safer than invented monarchy.
**Back:** *"Empire, city-states, or corporate elites: coordination yes, king no."*

### Flashcard ANC-1151 — Decline: deurbanisation, not extinction
**Front:** What changed after c. 1900 BCE, and what causes were proposed?
**Definition (learn):** After c. 1900 BCE major urban traits declined unevenly: cities contracted or were abandoned; script, seals, standards and crafts diminished; settlements shifted; regional pottery grew; agriculture diversified; rural communities continued. Cause families: river change, aridity, trade contraction, social reorganisation, disease, invasion or incursion.
**Breakdown:**
- Each cause alone fails: sites differed in hydrology; internal production continued; no evidence for one invasion destroying the civilisation.
- Advanced: decline linked to Ghaggar-Hakra and Indus shifts, ecological stress, breakdown of Mesopotamian trade and internal reorganisation (Advanced 06 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 1900 BCE (✅ owner 06 §17)
**Links:** ANC-1134, ANC-1152, ANC-1154
**Trap:** "The civilisation ended abruptly in 1900 BCE."
**Mains hook:** Argue for multi-causal deurbanisation and localisation.
**Back:** *"Six causes, none alone: deurbanise, localise, continue."*

### Flashcard ANC-1152 — Continuity and discontinuity in the Harappan legacy
**Front:** What continuities and discontinuities does Harappan evidence show?
**Definition (learn):** Continuities: crops, livestock and rural settlement; some pottery and craft; regional population continuity; selected symbols or practices. Discontinuities: urban scale, integrated networks, script, seal system and standardisation, long-distance exchange and specialised production.
**Breakdown:**
- Advanced: Late Harappan diversification and continuity in pottery, beliefs and agriculture complicate any clean Harappan/Vedic break (Advanced 06 §1).
- Topic 06's safe conclusion: continuity plus major institutional discontinuity.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 06 §18; Advanced 06 §1)
**Links:** ANC-1151, ANC-1155, ANC-1178
**Trap:** "A similar motif proves an unchanged tradition."
**Mains hook:** Use the continuity–discontinuity pair to answer both "was there a legacy?" and "was it the same?"
**Back:** *"Fields and villages stay; cities, script and seals go."*

### Flashcard ANC-1153 — The 2019 Rakhigarhi ancient-DNA study (Cell)
**Front:** What did the 2019 Rakhigarhi genome show, and what can it not establish?
**Definition (learn):** Published in Cell (vol. 179, issue 3), titled "An Ancient Harappan Genome Lacks Ancestry from Steppe Pastoralists or Iranian Farmers"; with Vasant Shinde and Vagheesh M. Narasimhan among the listed authors (Wikipedia). Based on one individual; little or no immediate Steppe-related contribution; Iranian-related component from an older hunter-gatherer lineage, not recent Iranian farmers.
**Breakdown:**
- One individual cannot represent all Harappans or identify language, caste, religion or polity.
- Advanced: consistent with later Indo-Aryan-speaking pastoral migration after the mature cities (⚠️ scholarly reading in owner).
- Announced later analyses are not completed findings.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Cell 2019; one individual (✅ Wikipedia: Rakhigarhi; ✅ owner 06 §19)
**Links:** ANC-1046, ANC-1170, ANC-1171
**Trap:** "One Rakhigarhi individual = all Harappans."
**Mains hook:** Argue that genetics reframes the Aryan question without answering it.
**Back:** *"One genome, no Steppe, Iranian-related and older: sample, not census."*

### Flashcard ANC-1154 — Routed PYQs for Harappan architecture, sites, script and inference
**Front:** Which PYQs route to this owner, and what is the status of each key?
**Definition (learn):** 2019 Prelims Q7 (identify non-Harappan site among Chanhudaro, Kot Diji, Sohgaura, Desalpur): inferred answer Sohgaura; official key unavailable. 2021 Prelims Q32 (town with dams, channels and reservoirs): inferred Dholavira; key unavailable. 2025 Prelims Q72 (Dancing Girl material): bronze; official key available. 2025 Mains Q1 (salient features of Harappan architecture; 10 marks, 150 words; spine: planning → construction → drainage → public works → regional variation → functional-label caution). 2026 Prelims Q8 (household spindle whorls, weights, courtyard houses): provisional key present locally.
**Breakdown:**
- Sohgaura is associated with a later copper-plate inscription in UP; not Harappan.
- 2026 Q8: spindle whorls support household spinning; weights support measurement; courtyards with wells do not establish a common-property system.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 06 §20; §23)
**Links:** ANC-1138, ANC-1139, ANC-1149
**Trap:** Presenting an inferred or provisional key as official.
**Mains hook:** Use the 2025 architecture spine for a 10-mark answer.
**Back:** *"Sohgaura out, Dholavira water, bronze girl, architecture spine, household inference."*

### Flashcard ANC-1155 — Advanced: Late Harappan diversification and cultural continuity
**Front:** What does the Advanced owner add about the Late Harappan phase?
**Definition (learn):** Decline of urban features and diversification of agriculture; cultural continuities in pottery, beliefs and agriculture link Harappans to later cultures; a clean "end" is a myth.
**Breakdown:**
- Subsistence diversity: wheat, barley, millets, pulses and, regionally, rice.
- Late Harappan rural cultures persisted.
- Regional pottery and settlement shifts.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner Advanced 06 §1)
**Links:** ANC-1134, ANC-1151, ANC-1152
**Trap:** "Late Harappan = dead civilisation."
**Mains hook:** Argue for localisation with continuity.
**Back:** *"The cities fade; the farms diversify."*

### Flashcard ANC-1156 — No iron, crafts and metallurgy in the Mature Harappan system
**Front:** Which crafts and materials defined the Mature Harappan technology, and is iron part of it?
**Definition (learn):** Bead-making (Chanhudaro, Lothal), shell (Nageshwar), faience (controlled high-temperature production), seal-cutting in steatite, metallurgy (copper or bronze tools, vessels, mirrors, figurines; gold, silver, lead objects), textiles (spindle whorls), wheel-made red ware with black designs. No iron defines the Mature Harappan system; stone, bone, shell, wood and copper or bronze coexisted.
**Breakdown:**
- Craft concentration suggests workshops and specialisation, not guilds, factories or hereditary castes.
- Do not infer iron from later sites.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 06 §8)
**Links:** ANC-1140, ANC-1142, ANC-1072
**Trap:** "Harappans used iron."
**Mains hook:** Use the crafts list to argue for specialisation without guild or caste inference.
**Back:** *"Beads, shell, faience, seals, bronze: no iron defines the city."*

### GROUP — The Aryan Problem & Identity of Aryan Culture (owner 07)

### Flashcard ANC-1157 — Category firewall: language, race, genes, pottery, ethnicity, civilisation, polity
**Front:** Why must the Aryan debate separate language, race, genes, pottery, ethnicity, civilisation and polity?
**Definition (learn):** Each category measures something different; none alone establishes another. Language is learned speech; archaeological culture is a recurring assemblage; race is an outdated typology when treated as fixed hierarchy; biological ancestry is statistical among sampled individuals; ethnicity is a historically produced boundary; civilisation is an analytical formation; polity is a political order.
**Breakdown:**
- The owner's firewall: LANGUAGE ≠ RACE ≠ GENES ≠ POTTERY ≠ ETHNICITY ≠ CIVILISATION ≠ POLITY.
- Use "Indo-Aryan-speaking groups", "early Vedic textual culture", or "Steppe-related ancestry" only for the category supported.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 07 §1)
**Links:** ANC-1158, ANC-1164, ANC-1178
**Trap:** Using one category to prove another.
**Mains hook:** Open the answer with the firewall to set the terms.
**Back:** *"Seven words, seven separate things."*

### Flashcard ANC-1158 — Ārya: a contextual self-description, not a race
**Front:** What does ārya mean in Vedic usage, and why is it not a biological race?
**Definition (learn):** A contextual ancient self-description: insider, ally, respectable person, social status, or participant in a ritual-political order. Its meaning varies by passage and period.
**Breakdown:**
- Not synonymous with every Indo-European speaker.
- Vedic texts record conflicts among those called ārya as well as with dāsa or dasyu.
- Later noble or respectable usage does not prove an unchanged ethnic meaning.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 07 §2)
**Links:** ANC-1159, ANC-1157
**Trap:** "Aryan = race."
**Mains hook:** Read the term by hymn, speaker, opponent, genre and context.
**Back:** *"Ārya names a status in the hymn, not a skull."*

### Flashcard ANC-1159 — Dāsa and dasyu: conflict terms, not one race
**Front:** How should dāsa and dasyu be read?
**Definition (learn):** Terms for opponents and outsiders with shifting meanings: political enemy, rival ritual group, linguistic or cultural outsider, subordinate or captive, mythic adversary.
**Breakdown:**
- Cannot be mapped mechanically onto one biological race, indigenous population or archaeological culture.
- Non-Indo-Aryan names and loanwords demonstrate interaction, not a sealed world.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 07 §2; owner 08 §11)
**Links:** ANC-1158, ANC-1174, ANC-1194
**Trap:** "Dāsa = dark-skinned indigenous race."
**Mains hook:** Use the range of meanings to argue against a racial reading.
**Back:** *"Enemy, rival, outsider, captive — never a census."*

### Flashcard ANC-1160 — Why the Aryan problem persists
**Front:** Which features make the Aryan problem hard to settle?
**Definition (learn):** Vedic Sanskrit has strong Indo-European and Indo-Iranian links; the Rig Veda is layered, religious-poetic and orally transmitted; Harappan writing is undeciphered; material cultures do not reveal language; South Asian ancient-DNA sampling is uneven; language spread, population movement and cultural change can run at different speeds.
**Breakdown:**
- Question-by-question residual uncertainty: language family (route), geography (dates), movement (scale, gender, route), Harappan–Vedic relationship (Harappan language unknown), material culture (no secure Aryan correlate).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 07 §3)
**Links:** ANC-1161, ANC-1177
**Trap:** "The Aryan debate is settled."
**Mains hook:** Use the five-row table to show what each evidence stream can and cannot settle.
**Back:** *"Language, text, script, pots, genes: all moving at different speeds."*

### Flashcard ANC-1161 — Indo-European, Indo-Iranian and the comparative method
**Front:** What is the family tree from Proto-Indo-European to Vedic Sanskrit, and what makes comparison valid?
**Definition (learn):** Proto-Indo-European → Proto-Indo-Iranian → Indo-Aryan, Iranian and Nuristani; Indo-Aryan → Vedic Sanskrit and later Indo-Aryan languages. Comparative method: regular sound correspondences; shared morphology and grammar; inherited vocabulary; shared subgroup innovations; loanwords and areal features identified separately.
**Breakdown:**
- Accidental word resemblance is not evidence.
- Linguistic relatedness is the most secure core of the debate.
- Comparative linguistics alone cannot identify homeland, route, conquest or ancestry.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 07 §4)
**Links:** ANC-1162, ANC-1163
**Trap:** "One cognate proves a migration."
**Mains hook:** Argue that the family tree shows relationship, not route.
**Back:** *"Regular sounds make kin; routes need more witnesses."*

### Flashcard ANC-1162 — Indo-Iranian shared vocabulary: Vedic soma and Avestan haoma
**Front:** What do Vedic soma and Avestan haoma indicate, and what do shared divine or horse vocabularies not indicate?
**Definition (learn):** Vedic Sanskrit and Avestan share inherited linguistic and ritual vocabulary; soma and haoma indicate relationship and later divergence. Shared divine or horse-chariot terms do not mean identical religion or one archaeological culture.
**Breakdown:**
