**Back:** *"Twenty-fourth in the line, not the first: Mahavira refines the vows."*

### Flashcard ANC-2027 — The Buddha: Śākya biography, sacred places and epithets
**Front:** What is the standard sacred biography of Siddhārtha Gautama, and which places are tested?
**Definition (learn):** Siddhārtha Gautama is remembered as a Śākya from the Himalayan foothill zone; the standard sacred biography covers renunciation, search among teachers, rejection of both indulgence and extreme self-mortification, awakening at Bodh Gayā, first sermon at Sārnāth (near Benares), and death/parinirvāṇa at Kuśinagara. (✅ owner 10 basic §1 and advanced §1; ✅ owner 10 §11.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Lumbini: Ashoka's Lumbini pillar inscription confirms the early Buddha association (✅ owner 10)
- Awakening: Bodh Gayā (✅ owner 10)
- First sermon: Sārnāth/deer park near Benares, remembered as dhammachakka-pavattana (✅ owner 10)
- Death: parinirvāṇa at Kuśinagara (✅ owner 10)
- Sangha founded; teaching for over four decades (✅ owner 10 advanced)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Buddha c. 6th–5th century BC (⚠️ owner 10); exact chronology debated; long and short chronologies exist (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** ANC-2025, ANC-2028, ANC-2029, ANC-2039
**Trap:** Do not convert one traditional birth/death pair into uncontested history. The Lumbini inscription confirms the place association, not every later episode. (✅ owner 10)
**Mains hook:** Use the biography as a historical core surrounded by later elaboration. Qualify with source criticism.
**Back:** *"Lumbini born, Bodh Gaya awakened, Sarnath spoke, Kushinagara closed."*

### Flashcard ANC-2028 — Epithets trap: Śākyamuni and Tathāgata versus Nayaputta/Nātaputta
**Front:** Which name is an epithet of the Buddha, and which name refers to Mahāvīra?
**Definition (learn):** Śākyamuni and Tathāgata are recognised Buddhist epithets of Gautama Buddha. Nigaṇṭha Nātaputta/Nayaputta refers to Mahāvīra in Pali sources. (✅ owner 10 basic §4, §5; ✅ owner 10 §11.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Buddha: Śākyamuni; Tathāgata (✅ owner 10)
- Mahāvīra: Nigaṇṭha Nātaputta; Nayaputta/Nātaputta (✅ owner 10)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PYQ 2024 GS-I Q54 tested the Buddha's epithets (Nayaputta, Shakyamuni, Tathagata); official Set-A key available locally, answer not recorded here (✅ owner 10 2024–2025 integration).
**Links:** ANC-2026, ANC-2027
**Trap:** "Nayaputta is an epithet of the Buddha" is false; it refers to Mahāvīra. (✅ owner 10)
**Mains hook:** Not a Mains item; a Prelims identity trap that also tests source literacy.
**Back:** *"Shakyamuni, Tathagata: Buddha. Nayaputta: Mahavira."*

### Flashcard ANC-2029 — Jain five vows and the Triratna (Jain path, not Buddhist)
**Front:** What are the Jain five vows and the Triratna, and to which tradition does the Triratna belong?
**Definition (learn):** The Jain five vows are non-violence (ahiṃsā), truth (satya), non-stealing (asteya), celibacy (brahmacharya) and non-possession/non-hoarding (aparigraha). The Triratna (three jewels) are right knowledge, right faith and right action/conduct; liberation is through them. (✅ owner 10 basic §1; ✅ owner 10 advanced §4; ✅ owner 10 §12.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Earlier four vows linked to previous teachers; the fifth, celibacy, added by Mahāvīra (✅ owner 10 basic §1)
- Ascetic "great vows" are stricter than lay observances (✅ owner 10 §12.1)
- Triratna: right faith, right knowledge, right conduct (✅ owner 10)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Jain Triratna is a Jain formulation; Buddhism's path is the Eightfold Path (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** ANC-2030, ANC-2032, ANC-2037
**Trap:** "Triratna belongs to Buddhism" is wrong in this context; it is Jain. Do not confuse it with the Buddhist Triratna of Buddha–Dhamma–Sangha. (✅ owner 10 basic §5; ⚠️ the Buddhist Triratna is standard knowledge)
**Mains hook:** Use the vows as the ethical bridge between Jain and Buddhist discipline; qualify with lay versus monastic scaling.
**Back:** *"Five vows, three jewels: knowledge, faith, action; ahimsa leads."*

### Flashcard ANC-2030 — Jain ontology: jīva/ajīva, anekāntavāda, syādvāda, nayavāda and the four gatis
**Front:** What doctrines are needed to explain Jain ascetic ethics, and which four forms of existence are tested?
**Definition (learn):** Living and non-living reality (jīva/ajīva): living beings are ensouled and karma is binding matter, which explains rigorous asceticism and non-injury. Anekāntavāda (many-sidedness), syādvāda (conditioned predication) and nayavāda (standpoints) were strategies against one-sided claims; their systematised forms are later. The four forms of existence (gati) are deva, manuṣya, nāraki and tiryañca; yakṣa is not one of them. (✅ owner 10 §12.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Jīva: ensouled living beings; karma as matter (✅ owner 10)
- Karma/liberation: stop karmic influx; shed karma through knowledge, conduct, austerity (✅ owner 10)
- Anekāntavāda / syādvāda / nayavāda (later systematised; not verbatim Mahāvīra) (✅ owner 10)
- Four gatis: deva, manuṣya, nāraki, tiryañca; yakṣa excluded (✅ owner 10 basic §4; ✅ owner 10 §12.1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PYQ 2026 GS-I Q3 asks the four principal forms of existence; the 2026 Set-A key is provisional and no answer letter is recorded here (✅ owner 10 2026 integration).
**Links:** ANC-2029, ANC-2038 (contrast with Buddhist anātman), ANC-2034
**Trap:** Including yakṣa among the four gatis is wrong. Do not treat the jīva ontology as modern biology; the 2023 PYQ on souls in rocks and water tests ancient ontology linked to ahiṃsā (see ANC-2052). (✅ owner 10)
**Mains hook:** Explain how ontology drives asceticism and non-injury; qualify that syādvāda's full form is later.
**Back:** *"Deva, manushya, naraki, tiryancha: four gatis; yaksha is a guest, not a member."*

### Flashcard ANC-2031 — Jain sects: Śvetāmbara and Digambara (regional predominance and differences)
**Front:** What separates Śvetāmbara from Digambara Jainism, and where did each predominate?
**Definition (learn):** The Jain split into Śvetāmbara and Digambara traditions, formed gradually over centuries. Differences concern clothing and "sky-clad" asceticism, scriptures, images/ritual and women's capacity for liberation. Śvetāmbaras predominated in western India; Digambaras in the south. (✅ owner 10 basic §4; ✅ owner 10 advanced §4; ✅ owner 10 §12.3)
**Breakdown:**
- Clothing: Digambara "sky-clad" asceticism (✅ owner 10 §12.3)
- Scriptures: Śvetāmbaras accept the Agamas; Digambaras hold that the original canon was lost (✅ owner 10 §17)
- Women's liberation: Digambara authorities generally held a woman could not attain liberation in a female body; Śvetāmbara accepted it (✅ owner 10 §12.2)
- Regions: Śvetāmbara in west India; Digambara in south (✅ owner 10 advanced)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Division gradual; not one council, famine or date (✅ owner 10 §12.3).
**Links:** ANC-2032, ANC-2033, ANC-2035
**Trap:** "Jain split during one famine at one council" is wrong; the famine narrative is later explanatory tradition. (✅ owner 10 §19)
**Mains hook:** Use sect formation to show that Jain institutions adapted to regions and patronage; qualify with later sectarian memory.
**Back:** *"Shvetambara white-clad in the west, Digambara sky-clad in the south: gradual, not a single split."*

### Flashcard ANC-2032 — Sthānakavāsī: a later Śvetāmbara reform (PYQ 2018 Q71)
**Front:** What is the Sthānakavāsī tradition, and how should it be dated in an answer?
**Definition (learn):** Sthānakavāsī is a non-image-worship reform tradition within the Śvetāmbara family, a much later development. Standard reference knowledge links its roots to Lonkā Shāh (15th century) and later formal organisation. (✅ owner 10 §12.3 and §21 PYQ route; ⚠️ Lonkā Shāh (15th c.) is standard reference knowledge in owner 10 §21)
**Breakdown:**
- Family: Śvetāmbara (✅ owner 10)
- Feature: non-image worship (✅ owner 10)
- Date: much later than Mahāvīra (✅ owner 10)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PYQ 2018 Q71 tested the Sthānakavāsī affiliation (✅ owner 10 2018–2023 integration; official key unavailable locally).
**Links:** ANC-2031, ANC-2026
**Trap:** Do not project Sthānakavāsī into Mahāvīra's age; it is a later reform. (✅ owner 10)
**Mains hook:** Not a central Mains item; use only to show later Jain reform.
**Back:** *"No images, later reform: Sthanakavasi is Shvetambara but late."*

### Flashcard ANC-2033 — Jain councils and canon: Pāṭaliputra, Valabhī and the lost-canon dispute
**Front:** What do Jain tradition and Śvetāmbara tradition say about the Pāṭaliputra and Valabhī councils, and what is the Digambara position?
**Definition (learn):** Jain tradition links an early effort to preserve teachings during or after famine and migration at Pāṭaliputra with Sthūlabhadra. Śvetāmbara tradition associates Valabhī, around the mid-first millennium CE, with the final redaction of its canon. Digambaras do not accept the Śvetāmbara Āgamas as the preserved original canon. (✅ owner 10 §12.3)
**Breakdown:**
- Pāṭaliputra: Sthūlabhadra; famine and migration; later sectarian source (✅ owner 10)
- Valabhī: mid-first-millennium CE final redaction (Śvetāmbara) (✅ owner 10)
- Digambara: canon lost; Āgamas not accepted (✅ owner 10)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Valabhī "around the mid-first millennium CE" (owner wording; exact year not given in owner, so no year asserted here). ⚠️
**Links:** ANC-2031, ANC-2056
**Trap:** Do not present Pāṭaliputra as the origin of the Digambara–Śvetāmbara split; it is an early canon-preservation account. (✅ owner 10)
**Mains hook:** Canon formation as a tool for regional continuity; qualify with later redaction.
**Back:** *"Patna for preservation, Valabhi for redaction: canon, not schism."*

### Flashcard ANC-2034 — Jain community, laity and women's religious agency
**Front:** How was the Jain community organised, and what must be said about women?
**Definition (learn):** The fourfold Jain community comprised monks, nuns, laymen and laywomen. Renunciation depended on household donors. Women entered renunciant communities in large numbers in some traditions. Jain ethics attracted urban and mercantile groups, but this is a tendency, not proof of a merchant-only religion. (✅ owner 10 §12.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Fourfold community: monks, nuns, laymen, laywomen (✅ owner 10)
- Lay ethics: scaled vows; lay giving linked with commercial networks (✅ owner 10 §12.2, §4 advanced)
- Women: liberation controversy (Digambara vs Śvetāmbara) (✅ owner 10)
- Language: Ardhamāgadhī and other Prākrits; regional Sanskrit, Kannaḍa, Tamil and Apabhraṃśa literatures (✅ owner 10)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Jainism had a detailed lay ethic (✅ owner 10 advanced §1).
**Links:** ANC-2031, ANC-2038, ANC-2042
**Trap:** "Jainism had no lay dimension" is wrong; texts regulate laity. "Jainism was only a merchant religion" is also wrong. (✅ owner 10 advanced §5)
**Mains hook:** Use lay-monastic interdependence to explain Jain survival; qualify that mercantile support was one stream.
**Back:** *"Four limbs of the community: monks, nuns, laymen, laywomen; donors hold it up."*

### Flashcard ANC-2035 — Jain persistence and the Jain historical minimum
**Front:** Why did Jainism persist in India, and what regions and monuments anchor its history?
**Definition (learn):** Jainism persisted through durable ascetic-lay networks, adaptable lay ethics, regional communities, mercantile and elite support, literary production and institutional continuity; persistence did not mean absence of change. Strong centres: Gangetic region, Mathura, western India, Kalinga, parts of the Deccan and south India. (✅ owner 10 §12.4, §16.4)
**Breakdown:**
- Ascetic-lay organisation; localised communities (✅ owner 10 §16.4)
- Less dependence on very large monastic establishments (✅ owner 10 §16.4)
- Anchors: Udayagiri-Khaṇḍagiri; Mathura votive tablets and inscriptions; later Śravaṇa Beḷgoḷa traditions (✅ owner 10 §12.4)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Each anchor belongs to a specific period (✅ owner 10 §12.4).
**Links:** ANC-2031, ANC-2057
**Trap:** "Jainism survived because it never changed" is wrong. (✅ owner 10 §19)
**Mains hook:** Use comparative persistence (Jain vs Buddhist) to build an evaluative answer; qualify it as a comparative explanation.
**Back:** *"Networks, lay ethics, merchants, literature: Jainism lasts by adapting."*

### Flashcard ANC-2036 — Four Noble Truths and the Middle Path (both extremes)
**Front:** What are the Four Noble Truths, and how does the Middle Path (majjhimā paṭipadā) connect to them?
**Definition (learn):** The Four Noble Truths are: suffering/unsatisfactoriness; its cause; its cessation; and the path to cessation. The Middle Path explicitly rejects both sensual indulgence and self-mortification and is taught through the Noble Eightfold Path. Buddhism explains suffering through desire (craving) and ignorance. (✅ owner 10 basic §1, §2; ✅ owner 10 §13.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Truth 1: suffering/unsatisfactoriness (✅ owner 10 §13.1)
- Truth 2: cause, craving and ignorance (✅ owner 10 §2)
- Truth 3: cessation (✅ owner 10 §13.1)
- Truth 4: path to cessation (Eightfold Path) (✅ owner 10 §13.1)
- Middle Path: rejects indulgence and self-mortification (✅ owner 10 §13.1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Nirvāṇa as liberation from craving (✅ owner 10 basic §1).
**Links:** ANC-2037, ANC-2038, ANC-2027, ANC-2029 (Jain asceticism contrast)
**Trap:** The Middle Path is not "moderation in everything" as a mere slogan; it is structured through the Eightfold Path. (⚠️ standard interpretation)
**Mains hook:** Use the Middle Path to show Buddhism's practical rejection of extreme asceticism, distinct from Jain radical asceticism.
**Back:** *"Suffer, cause, cease, path: four truths; neither luxury nor self-torture."*

### Flashcard ANC-2037 — Noble Eightfold Path (grouped into wisdom, morality, mental discipline)
**Front:** What are the eight limbs of the Noble Eightfold Path, and how are they grouped?
**Definition (learn):** Right view, intention, speech, action, livelihood, effort, mindfulness and concentration. They are often grouped as wisdom (view, intention), morality (speech, action, livelihood) and mental discipline (effort, mindfulness, concentration). (✅ owner 10 §13.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Wisdom: right view; right intention (✅ owner 10)
- Morality: right speech; right action; right livelihood (✅ owner 10)
- Mental discipline: right effort; right mindfulness; right concentration (✅ owner 10)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Grouping as stated in owner 10 §13.1 (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** ANC-2036, ANC-2029, ANC-2039
**Trap:** Do not confuse the Buddhist eight-limb path with Jain Triratna (three jewels) or the Jain five vows. (✅ owner 10)
**Mains hook:** The Eightfold Path as an ethical bridge for GS-IV; qualify that it is a practice, not a creed.
**Back:** *"View, intent, speech, action, livelihood, effort, mindfulness, concentration: wisdom, morals, mind."*

### Flashcard ANC-2038 — Dependent origination, anitya and anātman versus Jain jīva
**Front:** How do dependent origination, impermanence and non-self differ from the Jain soul doctrine?
**Definition (learn):** Dependent origination (pratītyasamutpāda/paṭiccasamuppāda): phenomena arise through conditions, not an uncaused first beginning. Impermanence (anitya/anicca) and non-self (anātman/anattā) undermine attachment to a permanent, independent self. Jain doctrine affirms jīva; Buddhism rejects a permanent self. (✅ owner 10 §13.1; ✅ owner 10 §17)
**Breakdown:**
- Dependent origination: conditioned arising (✅ owner 10)
- Anitya/anicca: impermanence (✅ owner 10)
- Anātman/anattā: non-self; not Jain soul doctrine (✅ owner 10)
- Karma in Buddhism: intentional action sustains rebirth; liberation ends greed, hatred and delusion (✅ owner 10 §13.1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Comparison table: Jain jīva vs Buddhist anātman (✅ owner 10 §17).
**Links:** ANC-2030, ANC-2036, ANC-2056
**Trap:** "Buddhism is Jain soul doctrine" is wrong; Buddhism must not be described as Jain soul doctrine. (✅ owner 10)
**Mains hook:** Use the self-doctrine contrast to show doctrinal difference despite shared ethics; qualify that the contrast is about metaphysics, not ethics.
**Back:** *"Jain keeps the jiva; Buddhist keeps the cause, drops the self."*

### Flashcard ANC-2039 — Sangha, Vinaya and the Buddhist lay community
**Front:** How was the Buddhist sangha organised, and what did monks, nuns and lay followers supply?
**Definition (learn):** The community is conventionally organised around Buddha, Dhamma and Sangha. Monks (bhikkhu) and nuns (bhikkhunī) followed disciplinary rules (Vinaya). Lay followers (upāsaka/upāsikā) supplied food, robes, medicine and residence and sought merit and instruction. The sangha was a portable institution. (✅ owner 10 §13.2; ✅ owner 10 advanced §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Triad: Buddha, Dhamma, Sangha (✅ owner 10)
- Monks/nuns: bhikkhu, bhikkhunī under Vinaya (✅ owner 10)
- Laity: upāsaka/upāsikā; food, robes, medicine, residence (✅ owner 10)
- Monasteries tied to routes, towns, craft groups and donors (✅ owner 10)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sangha founded by the Buddha; teaching over four decades (✅ owner 10 advanced §1).
**Links:** ANC-2027, ANC-2040, ANC-2041, ANC-2055
**Trap:** "Sangha was only a spiritual idea" is wrong; it was an organisation of discipline and transmission. Entry did not instantly abolish caste. (✅ owner 10)
**Mains hook:** Use the sangha to explain how Buddhism travelled across regions.
**Back:** *"Buddha, Dhamma, Sangha; Vinaya rules; lay donors feed the order."*

### Flashcard ANC-2040 — Mahāprajāpatī Gotamī and the eight heavy rules (garudhammas)
**Front:** What does tradition say about Mahāprajāpatī Gotamī, and what must be said about the eight heavy rules?
**Definition (learn):** Tradition associates Mahāprajāpatī Gotamī with the creation of the order of nuns after initial resistance. The accounts of the eight heavy rules (garudhammas) are textually layered and historically debated, but they reflect institutional subordination in received Vinaya traditions. Nuns were teachers and donors as well as subjects of regulation. (✅ owner 10 §13.3)
**Breakdown:**
- Tradition: Gotamī and the nuns' order (✅ owner 10)
- Garudhammas: textually layered; historically debated (✅ owner 10)
- Agency: nuns as teachers and donors (✅ owner 10)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No date asserted in owner; ⚠️.
**Links:** ANC-2034 (Jain women), ANC-2039, owner 13 (women in the Buddha-age society)
**Trap:** Avoid both "Buddhism created full gender equality" and "women had no religious agency". (✅ owner 10 §13.3)
**Mains hook:** Use the nuns' order to show constrained agency; qualify with Vinaya layers.
**Back:** *"Gotami opened the order; eight heavy rules bound it: agency and limit."*

### Flashcard ANC-2041 — Language: Pāli, Middle Indo-Aryan and canonical versus verbatim speech
**Front:** What language did early Buddhist teaching use, and what does Pāli signify?
**Definition (learn):** Early Buddhist teaching circulated orally in Middle Indo-Aryan speech forms. Pāli is the canonical language of Theravāda, not a proven verbatim record of the Buddha's personal dialect. Other schools preserved texts in Sanskritised or Prākrit forms; many survive through Chinese and Tibetan translations. (✅ owner 10 §13.4)
**Breakdown:**
- Middle Indo-Aryan oral teaching (✅ owner 10)
- Pāli: Theravāda canon (✅ owner 10)
- Sanskritised/Prākrit school canons (✅ owner 10)
- Chinese and Tibetan translations preserve lost Indic materials (✅ owner 10 §14.1)
- Jain canonical language: Ardhamāgadhī (✅ owner 10 §12.2)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Accessible language aided expansion but did not explain conversion alone (✅ owner 10 §13.4).
**Links:** ANC-2039, ANC-2054, ANC-2056
**Trap:** "Pāli was the language the Buddha spoke; Mahāvīra spoke only Ardhamāgadhī" is an unsafe claim (✅ owner 10 §19).
**Mains hook:** Use language as one of several transmission factors; qualify with canon versus speech.
**Back:** *"Middle Indo-Aryan first, Pali for Theravada, Chinese and Tibetan keep the lost."*

### Flashcard ANC-2042 — First Buddhist Council: Rājagṛha (Mahākāśyapa, Ajātaśatru)
**Front:** Where, when, who presided and under which patron was the First Buddhist Council held?
**Definition (learn):** Recitation of the teaching and discipline soon after the Buddha's parinirvāṇa, in a cave near Rājagṛha. Mahākāśyapa presided; Ānanda recited the suttas and Upāli the Vinaya. Patron: Ajātaśatru. (✅ owner 10 §14.1; ✅ Wikipedia "Buddhist councils")
**Breakdown:**
- Place: cave near Rājagṛha (Rājgir) (✅ Wikipedia)
- President: Mahākāśyapa (✅ Wikipedia)
- Recitation: Ānanda (suttas); Upāli (Vinaya) (✅ Wikipedia)
- Patron: Ajātaśatru (✅ Wikipedia)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Year not given by owner or Wikipedia; traditional dating only; Wikipedia states "just after the Buddha's parinirvāṇa". Many scholars consider it improbable (✅ Wikipedia, historicity note).
**Links:** ANC-2043, ANC-2044, ANC-2045, ANC-2046
**Trap:** Councils are transmitted through school-specific memories; do not treat the First Council as a securely dated fact. (✅ owner 10 §14.1)
**Mains hook:** Use councils as evidence of institutional memory, not neutral chronology.
**Back:** *"Rajagriha, Mahakassapa, Ajatashatru: memory before schism."*

### Flashcard ANC-2043 — Second Buddhist Council: Vaiśālī, Yaśa and the Mahādeva "five points"
**Front:** Where and when was the Second Buddhist Council, who led it, and what was the cause of the first schism?
**Definition (learn):** Held at Vālukārāma, Vaiśālī, about 100 years after the parinirvāṇa (traditional, no year given). Yaśa Kākaṇḍakaputta raised the dispute over monastic discipline; Soreyya-Revata led a 700-monk recital; Sabbakāmi was also consulted; patron Kālāśoka. Sthavira sources instead place Mahādeva's five points at Pāṭaliputra about 35 years later. (✅ Wikipedia "Buddhist councils")
**Breakdown:**
- Place: Vālukārāma, Vaiśālī (✅ Wikipedia)
- Leaders: Yaśa Kākaṇḍakaputta (raised issue); Soreyya-Revata (700-monk recital) (✅ Wikipedia)
- Patron: Kālāśoka (✅ Wikipedia)
- Schism causes: Theravāda blames Vaiśālī monks' ten points; Mahāsāṃghikas say Sthaviras added rules; Sthavira sources blame Mahādeva's five points (✅ Wikipedia)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** "About a century later" (✅ owner 10 §14.1); "100 years after" (✅ Wikipedia). No year asserted. 
**Links:** ANC-2042, ANC-2044, ANC-2045
**Trap:** Do not make every later school division the immediate result of one dispute; most scholars see Mahādeva's episode as later and some place the schism after the council. (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 10 §14.1)
**Mains hook:** Councils show disputes over discipline; qualify with school-specific transmission.
**Back:** *"Vaishali ten points, Mahadeva's five at Patna: discipline splits the order."*

### Flashcard ANC-2044 — Third Buddhist Council: Pāṭaliputra, Aśoka and Moggaliputta Tissa
**Front:** Where and under which ruler was the Third Buddhist Council held, who presided, and what is the source caution?
**Definition (learn):** In the reign of Aśoka at Pāṭaliputra; Moggaliputta Tissa presided with 1,000 monks (Theravāda chronicles); purification and missions. (✅ Wikipedia "Buddhist councils"; ✅ owner 10 §14.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Place: Pāṭaliputra (✅ Wikipedia)
- President: Moggaliputta Tissa; 1,000 monks (✅ Wikipedia)
- Patron: Aśoka (✅ Wikipedia)
- Theravāda chronicles link it to missions; account is school-specific (✅ owner 10 §14.1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Year not stated by owner or Wikipedia excerpt; reign of Aśoka (3rd century BCE, ⚠️ standard).
**Links:** ANC-2043, ANC-2045, owner 14 (Aśoka's dhamma)
**Trap:** Ashoka did not found Buddhism or exclusively export it; expansion relied on many actors and routes (✅ owner 10 §19). The Third Council's split link to Sarvāstivāda–Vibhajjavāda is contested (✅ Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** Use the council to explain Aśokan patronage without monocausal credit.
**Back:** *"Ashoka's Pataliputra, Tissa presides: purification with missions."*

### Flashcard ANC-2045 — "Fourth" councils: Kashmir under Kaniṣka and Sri Lanka
**Front:** What are the two competing "fourth" council traditions, who presided, and how is each recognised?
**Definition (learn):** Sarvāstivāda tradition: a council at Kuṇḍalavana, Kashmir, under Kaniṣka I, with Vasumitra as president, Aśvaghoṣa as vice-president and 500 monks. Sinhalese Theravāda: a council at Aluvihāra (Alu Vihāra) in Sri Lanka in the 1st century BCE under Vaṭṭagāmani Abhaya (Valagamba), with the canon written down (Dīpavaṃsa places it 29–17 BCE). Theravādins do not recognise the Kashmir council as authoritative. (✅ Wikipedia "Buddhist councils"; ✅ owner 10 §14.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Kashmir: Kaniṣka I; Vasumitra (president); Aśvaghoṣa (vice); 500 monks (✅ Wikipedia)
- Sri Lanka: Aluvihāra; Vaṭṭagāmani Abhaya; 1st c. BCE (✅ Wikipedia)
- Pārśva advised Kaniṣka (✅ Wikipedia)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Dīpavaṃsa 29–17 BCE for the Sri Lanka canon-writing; Valagamba's death given as 77 BCE in some sources (⚠️ Wikipedia notes a discrepancy).
**Links:** ANC-2044, ANC-2046, ANC-2048, ANC-2049
**Trap:** "There is a single pan-Buddhist fourth council" is wrong. Kaniṣka's council is not a Theravāda council. (✅ owner 10 §14.1)
**Mains hook:** Use competing councils to show school-specific canon formation; qualify with Kushan-era Sarvāstivāda.
**Back:** *"Kashmir for Kanishka, Alu Vihara for Theravada: no single fourth council."*

### Flashcard ANC-2046 — Modern councils: 1871 Mandalay and 1954–56 Yangon ("Fifth" and "Sixth")
**Front:** What are the Burmese Fifth and Sixth Buddhist councils, and why must they be kept separate from ancient councils?
**Definition (learn):** The Fifth council, Mandalay 1871, under King Mindon, was attended by about 2,400 monks; three elders (Jagarābhivaṃsa, Narindābhidhaja, Sumaṅgalasāmi) led it. The Sixth council, Kaba Aye, Yangon, opened 17 May 1954 and closed 24 May 1956 under the Burmese government of PM U Nu; Mahāsi Sayadaw asked the questions and Mingun Sayadaw answered, with about 2,500 monks. (✅ Wikipedia "Buddhist councils")
**Breakdown:**
- Fifth: Mandalay, 1871; Mindon; three elders; ~2,400 monks (✅ Wikipedia)
- Sixth: Kaba Aye, Yangon; 1954–1956; U Nu; Mahāsi Sayadaw and Mingun Sayadaw (✅ Wikipedia)
- Numbering: Burmese count is Theravāda-only (✅ Wikipedia)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As above; modern councils are 19th–20th century (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** ANC-2042 to ANC-2045
**Trap:** Do not confuse modern "fifth" and "sixth" councils with the ancient Fifth. The numbering is Burmese Theravāda only. (✅ Wikipedia)
**Mains hook:** Modern councils show Buddhist revival in colonial and post-colonial Burma; not an Ancient History question.
**Back:** *"Mandalay 1871, Yangon 1954–56: Burma's count, not the ancient one."*

### Flashcard ANC-2047 — Council chronology ladder (Buddhist)
**Front:** What is the sequence of Buddhist councils with confidence labels?
**Definition (learn):** Ladder: Parinirvāṇa → First (Rājagṛha, c. after death) → Second (Vaiśālī, c. 100 years later) → Mahādeva's five points (Pāṭaliputra, c. 35 years after the second, per Sthavira sources) → Third (Pāṭaliputra, Aśoka's reign) → Sri Lanka canon-writing (1st c. BCE) → Kaniṣka's Kashmir council (Kushan era) → Burmese 1871 and 1954–56. (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 10 §14.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Confidence: First council improbable in many scholars' view; Second has traditional dating only (✅ Wikipedia)
- Third: Aśoka's reign (⚠️ no year in source)
- Fourth: competing traditions (✅ Wikipedia)
- Fifth/Sixth: modern (✅ Wikipedia)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As stated; no uncertain year asserted.
**Links:** ANC-2042 to ANC-2046, ANC-2048
**Trap:** Do not treat council dates as securely fixed facts. (✅ owner 10 §19)
**Mains hook:** Use the ladder to demonstrate source-label discipline in an answer.
**Back:** *"First, Second, Third, Kashmir, Burma: each council speaks for its own school."*

### Flashcard ANC-2048 — Early schools and later Buddhist developments (Sthavira, Mahāsāṃghika, Sarvāstivāda, Mahāyāna)
**Front:** How should the early Buddhist schools and the rise of Mahāyāna be described?
**Definition (learn):** The Sthavira and Mahāsāṃghika lineages belong to complex post-Buddha history; Theravāda derives through a Sthavira lineage but is not a synonym for all Sthaviravāda schools. Lokottaravāda is associated with the Mahāsāṃghika family. Sarvāstivāda became influential in north-west India, Kashmir and Central Asia. Mahāyāna grew through many communities and texts around the early centuries BCE/CE. (✅ owner 10 §14.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Sthavira / Mahāsāṃghika: early lineages (✅ owner 10)
- Lokottaravāda: Mahāsāṃghika-related (✅ owner 10)
- Sarvāstivāda: north-west India, Kashmir, Central Asia; Sanghabhūti's late-fourth-century commentary on its Vinaya (✅ owner 10 §14.2)
- Mahāyāna: gradual emergence; no single birth date (✅ owner 10)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mahāyāna emergence early centuries BCE/CE (✅ owner 10 §14.2 and §18).
**Links:** ANC-2045, ANC-2049, ANC-2050
**Trap:** "Mahāyāna began at the fourth council and invented images" is wrong (✅ owner 10 §19). "Theravāda is Hīnayāna" is wrong (✅ owner 10).
**Mains hook:** Use school diversity to show that "Buddhism" was a plural field. Qualify with regional sequences.
**Back:** *"Sthavira, Mahasamghika, Sarvastivada: schools before Mahayana, none a single birthday."*

### Flashcard ANC-2049 — Mahāyāna features: Bodhisattva ideal, pāramitās, Maitreya
**Front:** What is the textbook triad of Mahāyāna features, and which tested items must be distinguished?
**Definition (learn):** The textbook triad: expanded Buddha status, Bodhisattva path, image and ritual. Pāramitās (perfections) structure the Bodhisattva path. Maitreya is the future Buddha in Buddhist tradition and is not exclusively Mahāyāna. Avalokiteśvara, Lokeśvara and Padmapāṇi are Bodhisattva names/forms. (✅ owner 10 §21 PYQ routes)
**Breakdown:**
- Expanded Buddha/Bodhisattva cults and new sūtras (✅ owner 10 §14.2)
- Six pāramitās (⚠️ standard reference knowledge per owner 10 §21): dāna (generosity), śīla (morality), kṣānti (patience), vīrya (effort), dhyāna (meditation), prajñā (wisdom)
- Image worship not exclusive to Mahāyāna (✅ owner 10 §14.2)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PYQ 2018 Q74 (Maitreya); PYQ 2019 Q9 (Mahāyāna features); PYQ 2020 Q31 (pāramitās) (✅ owner 10 PYQ ledger). Keys not recorded here.
**Links:** ANC-2048, ANC-2050, ANC-2052 (Dhanyakaṭaka)
**Trap:** Pāramitās are perfections on the Bodhisattva path, not Dharmasūtras or merchant guilds (2020 Q31 route). Maitreya is not exclusively Mahāyāna. (✅ owner 10 §21)
**Mains hook:** Mahāyāna as a diverse development; qualify image worship as not unique.
**Back:** *"Buddha as cosmic, Bodhisattva as path, perfections as steps: Maitreya is shared."*

### Flashcard ANC-2050 — Vajrayāna and Sanghabhūti (PYQ 2024 Q64)
**Front:** Which later Buddhist developments must not be projected into the Buddha's age, and whom does PYQ 2024 Q64 test?
**Definition (learn):** Vajrayāna is a later tantric development. PYQ 2024 Q64 tests Sanghabhūti, a late-fourth-century Indian Buddhist monk who travelled to China and was associated with a commentary on the Sarvāstivāda Vinaya; Sanghabhadra is a different, later Vaibhāṣika Abhidharma scholar. (✅ owner 10 §14.2 and 2024–2025 integration; audited official UPSC route)
**Breakdown:**
- Vajrayāna: tantric; later (✅ owner 10 §14.2)
- Sanghabhūti: Sarvāstivāda Vinaya commentary; China (✅ owner 10 2024–2025 integration)
- Sanghabhadra: different scholar (✅ owner 10)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sarvāstivāda: north-west India, Kashmir, Central Asia. Official 2024 Set-A key present locally (answer not recorded here).
**Links:** ANC-2048, ANC-2049
**Trap:** Do not equate Sanghabhūti with Sanghabhadra; do not equate Sarvāstivāda with Theravāda. (✅ owner 10)
**Mains hook:** Not a Mains item; use only as a Prelims identity trap.
**Back:** *"Sanghabhuti commented on Sarvastivada Vinaya; Sanghabhadra is someone else."*

### Flashcard ANC-2051 — Dhanyakaṭaka and Mahāsāṃghika geography (PYQ 2023 Q41)
**Front:** Where is Dhanyakaṭaka, and what Buddhist tradition is associated with it?
**Definition (learn):** Dhanyakaṭaka/Dharaṇīkoṭa in the lower Kṛṣṇā–Āndhra region is associated with the Amarāvatī Buddhist zone and Mahāsāṃghika traditions. It must not be confused with Gandhāra, Kaliṅga or Magadha. (✅ owner 10 §15.2; ✅ owner 10 2018–2023 integration)
**Breakdown:**
- Region: lower Kṛṣṇā–Āndhra (✅ owner 10)
- Associated with Amarāvatī zone (✅ owner 10)
- Tradition: Mahāsāṃghika (✅ owner 10)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PYQ 2023 Q41 (official key unavailable locally).
**Links:** ANC-2048, ANC-2055 (Amarāvatī), owner 17 Satavahanas (outside this fragment)
**Trap:** Dhanyakaṭaka is not Gandhāra (north-west), Kaliṅga (Odisha), or Magadha (Bihar). (✅ owner 10)
**Mains hook:** Not a central Mains item; use for regional mapping of Buddhist centres.
**Back:** *"Dhanyakataka: lower Krishna, Amaravati, Mahasamghika."*

### Flashcard ANC-2052 — Jain ontology of souls in natural objects (PYQ 2023 Q47)
**Front:** What does Jain ontology say about living beings in rocks, running water and natural objects, and how should the answer be framed?
**Definition (learn):** Jain classification treats living beings as ensouled, including in rocks, water and other natural objects; this is an ancient ontology connected to ahiṃsā, not modern biology or environmental science. (✅ owner 10 §12.1 and 2018–2023 integration, PYQ 2023 Q47)
**Breakdown:**
- Jīva: ensouled living beings (✅ owner 10)
- Ahiṃsā: non-injury as ethical consequence (✅ owner 10)
- Not modern biology (✅ owner 10)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PYQ 2023 Q47 (official key unavailable locally).
**Links:** ANC-2030, ANC-2029
**Trap:** Do not answer as a modern scientific claim about plant or mineral life; answer as Jain doctrine. (✅ owner 10)
**Mains hook:** Use to show ethical ontology underpinning non-injury; qualify with doctrinal framing.
**Back:** *"Jiva fills the rock and river in Jain thought: ahimsa from ontology, not biology."*

### Flashcard ANC-2053 — Parivrājaka, śramaṇa and upāsaka (PYQ 2020 Q71)
**Front:** What do the terms parivrājaka, śramaṇa and upāsaka denote in ancient India?
**Definition (learn):** A parivrājaka is a wanderer/renouncer; a śramaṇa is a striving renouncer, not a high-status priest; an upāsaka is a Buddhist lay follower (male; upāsikā female). (✅ owner 10 §21 PYQ route)
**Breakdown:**
- Parivrājaka: wanderer renouncer (✅ owner 10)
- Śramaṇa: striving renouncer; broad field including Jain, Buddhist, Ājīvika, materialist (✅ owner 10 §10)
- Upāsaka: lay follower (✅ owner 10)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PYQ 2020 Q71 (official key unavailable locally).
**Links:** ANC-2025, ANC-2039
**Trap:** Śramaṇa is not a priest; upāsaka is not a monk. (✅ owner 10)
**Mains hook:** Use the śramaṇa field to explain renunciant ferment.
**Back:** *"Parivrajaka wanders, shramana strives, upasaka lays hands on donations."*

### Flashcard ANC-2054 — Canonical texts: Jain and Buddhist titles (PYQ 2022 Q55)
**Front:** Which literary works are Jain and which are Buddhist in the 2022 identification route?
**Definition (learn):** Pariśiṣṭaparvan and Triṣaṣṭiśalākāpuruṣacarita (Triṣaṣṭilakṣaṇa Mahāpurāṇa in the owner's wording) are Jain. Nettipakaraṇa and Avadānaśataka are Buddhist. (✅ owner 10 §21 PYQ route)
**Breakdown:**
- Jain: Pariśiṣṭaparvan; Triṣaṣṭilakṣaṇa Mahāpurāṇa (✅ owner 10)
- Buddhist: Nettipakaraṇa; Avadānaśataka (✅ owner 10)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PYQ 2022 Q55 (official key unavailable locally).
**Links:** ANC-2041, ANC-2039
**Trap:** Do not mix Jain and Buddhist titles; the Triṣaṣṭi title is a Jain text (not a Buddhist one). (✅ owner 10)
**Mains hook:** Not a Mains item; a source-literacy Prelims trap.
**Back:** *"Parishishta and Trishashti are Jain; Netti and Avadana are Buddhist."*

### Flashcard ANC-2055 — Patronage: Aśoka, merchants, householders and the material anchors
**Front:** How did patronage and material remains sustain Buddhism, and what are the limits?
**Definition (learn):** Early traditions remember support from Magadhan and Kosalan rulers and wealthy householders (Anāthapiṇḍika, Visākhā). Aśoka's inscriptions give unusually early evidence for royal support, pilgrimage and concern with the sangha, though his dhamma policy was not identical to Buddhist doctrine. Post-Mauryan patrons, local rulers, merchants and donors sustained institutions. Anchors: Sāñcī, Bharhut, Amarāvatī/Dhānyakaṭaka; stupas, caves, monasteries. (✅ owner 10 §15.1, §15.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Early patrons: Magadha and Kosala rulers; Anāthapiṇḍika; Visākhā (✅ owner 10)
- Aśoka: inscriptions on support, pilgrimage, sangha (✅ owner 10)
- Post-Mauryan: local rulers, merchants, donors (✅ owner 10)
- Kaniṣka: important but filtered through later tradition (✅ owner 10)
- Material: stupas, relic deposits; caves; inscriptions; images (✅ owner 10 §15.2)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Stupa form had funerary antecedents; not every stupa began as a relic repository (✅ owner 10 §15.2).
**Links:** ANC-2044, ANC-2051, owner 14 (Aśokan edicts), owner 16 (Kushān, outside this fragment)
**Trap:** "Ashoka converted India and exported Buddhism everywhere" is wrong (✅ owner 10 §19). Donor identity does not reveal the whole population's religion.
**Mains hook:** Use donative inscriptions and monuments to show plural patronage; qualify with silence-not-proof.
**Back:** *"Ashoka's edicts, merchants' gifts, stupa stones: patronage plural, proof partial."*

### Flashcard ANC-2056 — Buddhist expansion beyond India: routes and phases
**Front:** Through which routes and phases did Buddhism spread beyond India?
**Definition (learn):** Expansion ran through overlapping monastic, mercantile, diplomatic, translation and pilgrimage networks: (1) Sri Lanka, with Theravāda chronicles linking missions to Aśoka; (2) north-west India and Central Asia along transregional routes; (3) China via translators and travelling monks, then Korea and Japan; (4) mainland and maritime Southeast Asia; (5) Himalayan and Tibetan regions through later Sanskritic and tantric transmissions. (✅ owner 10 §15.3)
**Breakdown:**
- Sri Lanka: Theravāda chronicles; Aśoka-linked missions (✅ owner 10)
- Central Asia; China; Korea; Japan (✅ owner 10)
- SE Asia; Himalaya and Tibet (✅ owner 10)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No single route, ruler or language explains expansion (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** ANC-2041, ANC-2044, ANC-2057
**Trap:** "Ashoka exported Buddhism everywhere" is wrong; expansion relied on many earlier and later actors. (✅ owner 10 §19)
**Mains hook:** Use the routes to show trans-Asian spread (advanced: a trans-Asian civilisational network); qualify with regional outcomes.
**Back:** *"Lanka, Central Asia, China, Southeast Asia, Tibet: five routes, no single key."*

### Flashcard ANC-2057 — Buddhism's decline in India: regional, gradual and multicausal
**Front:** What were the causes of the weakening of Buddhist institutions in India, and why is "disappearance" the wrong framing?
**Definition (learn):** Decline was regional, gradual and multicausal: changing royal and landed patronage; competition and accommodation with developing Brahmanical/Purāṇic and devotional institutions; absorption of practices, deities and sacred places; economic and settlement changes affecting large monasteries; institutional concentration; regional political violence, with later raids aggravating rather than originating decline. Buddhism remained important in Himalayan and eastern zones and flourished abroad. (✅ owner 10 §16.3; ✅ owner 10 basic §4: declined in India by the twelfth century AD)
**Breakdown:**
- Six causal strands (✅ owner 10 §16.3)
- Persistence: Himalayan, eastern zones; overseas; modern revivals (✅ owner 10)
- Decline in India "by the twelfth century AD" (✅ owner 10 basic §4, Prelims)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 12th century AD (✅ owner 10 basic §4).
**Links:** ANC-2035, ANC-2056, ANC-2058
**Trap:** Do not use Turkish raids, Brahmanical hostility, tantric "degeneration" or loss of patronage as a single cause. "Buddhism vanished because of Muslim invasions" is wrong. (✅ owner 10 §16.3, §19)
**Mains hook:** "Why did Buddhism spread widely yet decline in India while Jainism survived?" Qualify: comparative, not deterministic.
**Back:** *"Six strands, one slow fade: patronage, absorption, economy, concentration, violence, aggravation."*

### Flashcard ANC-2058 — Hostile-search trap register (Jain and Buddhist)
**Front:** Which unsafe claims about Jainism and Buddhism must be corrected?
**Definition (learn):** A trap list the owner calls the "hostile-search trap register": (1) they arose only because traders opposed Brāhmaṇism; (2) both denied caste and created equality; (3) Pāli was the Buddha's language and Mahāvīra spoke only Ardhamāgadhī; (4) Aśoka converted India and exported Buddhism; (5) all councils are securely dated; (6) Theravāda is Hīnayāna; (7) Mahāyāna began at the fourth council and invented images; (8) Digambara and Śvetāmbara split in one famine; (9) Buddhism vanished because of Muslim invasions; (10) Jainism survived because it never changed. (✅ owner 10 §19)
**Breakdown:** Each unsafe claim has a marks-safe correction in the owner (✅ owner 10 §19).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed; no new facts.
**Links:** ANC-2025, ANC-2041, ANC-2043, ANC-2048, ANC-2057
**Trap:** Each trap is a single-cause or universal claim. Look for "only", "all", "invented", "exported".
**Mains hook:** Use these corrections to qualify any evaluation answer.
**Back:** *"Only, all, invented, exported: the four words that signal a false universal."*

### Flashcard ANC-2059 — Jain vs Buddhist comparison pairs (controlled)
**Front:** How do Jainism and Buddhism compare on self, discipline, karma, community, canon and trajectory?
**Definition (learn):** Controlled comparison from owner §17: self/living being (Jain affirms jīva; Buddhist rejects a permanent self); discipline (Jain rigorous asceticism and radical ahiṃsā; Buddhist Middle Path and Eightfold Path); karma (Jain subtle material bondage; Buddhist intentional action without a permanent self); community (both: monks, nuns, laymen, laywomen); canon (Jain Śvetāmbara Āgamas accepted, Digambara holds canon lost; Buddhist multiple school canons, Pāli Tipiṭaka is Theravāda); trajectory (Jain long regional continuity; Buddhist wider trans-Asian expansion and relative institutional decline in India). (✅ owner 10 §17)
**Breakdown:** Six parallel axes (✅ owner 10 §17).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Pali Tipiṭaka: Theravāda canon, not the only Buddhist canon (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** ANC-2030, ANC-2036, ANC-2038, ANC-2057
**Trap:** Similar ethical vocabulary does not make the two doctrines identical. (✅ owner 10 §9)
**Mains hook:** "Compare Jain and Buddhist institutions: discipline, laity, mobility and patronage." Use parallel axes, not two mini-essays.
**Back:** *"Jiva keeps the self, Buddha drops it; Jains stay local, Buddhists go global."*

### Flashcard ANC-2060 — Source-evidence ladder for Jain and Buddhist history
**Front:** Which evidence classes can establish what, and what caution attaches to each?
**Definition (learn):** Canonical traditions (doctrine, discipline, remembered biography) with oral transmission and redaction; later biographies and commentaries (sacred memory, pilgrimage geography); inscriptions (donors, establishments, royal support, some sect names; silence is not absence); archaeology and art (stupas, caves, images; material form does not reveal doctrine); foreign-language canons and travel accounts (transmission and translation vocabulary may alter Indian concepts). (✅ owner 10 §9.1)
**Breakdown:** Five evidence classes with explicit limits (✅ owner 10 §9.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Six source-critical rules (separate traditional chronology; biography core; identify school; test texts with inscriptions; distinguish founder's teaching from later systems; reconstruct expansion through combined evidence) (✅ owner 10 §9.2).
**Links:** ANC-2025, ANC-2047, ANC-2055
**Trap:** Mature Jain logic and Buddhist philosophy cannot be projected into the 6th–5th century BCE. (✅ owner 10 §9.2)
**Mains hook:** Use the ladder in any answer on origins to show source discipline.
**Back:** *"Canon teaches, inscription donates, stupa stands: each proves its own part."*

### Flashcard ANC-2061 — Jain and Buddhist chronology with confidence labels
**Front:** What is the broad chronology of Jain and Buddhist developments, with confidence rules?
**Definition (learn):** Sixth–fifth centuries BCE: setting of Mahāvīra, Buddha and śramaṇa teachers (founder dates debated). Centuries after the Buddha: monastic consolidation and council memories. Third century BCE: Aśokan inscriptional and monumental patronage. Late BCE to early CE: stupas, caves and gradual Mahāyāna emergence. Early CE onward: image, scholastic and transregional traditions. Mid-first millennium CE: Valabhī tradition and mature sectarian histories. (✅ owner 10 §18)
**Breakdown:** Six periods with confidence rules (✅ owner 10 §18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sixth–fifth c. BCE; third c. BCE (Aśoka); mid-first millennium CE (Valabhī) (✅ owner 10 §18).
**Links:** ANC-2047, ANC-2033, ANC-2045
**Trap:** Do not project mature systems back onto founders. (✅ owner 10 §18)
**Mains hook:** A timeline anchor for a 20-mark answer on origin and spread.
**Back:** *"Sixth-fifth founders, third-century Ashoka, mid-millennium Valabhi: mature later."*

### Flashcard ANC-2062 — Mains answer architecture for Jain and Buddhist questions
**Front:** What answer skeletons does the owner give for 10-, 15- and 20-mark questions on Jainism and Buddhism?
**Definition (learn):** 10 marks/150 words: define the problem; three dimensions with named source/site/institution; one caveat; qualified two-line judgement. 15 marks/250 words: context → doctrine needed for mechanism → institution/social base → patronage and spread → limitation → verdict. 20 marks/300 words: source problem → chronology → social-economic setting → monastic/lay institutions → patronage and routes → regional/sectarian variation → consequences → multicausal conclusion. (✅ owner 10 §20)
**Breakdown:**
- Directive-aware thesis options: Discuss; Critically examine; Compare/evaluate (✅ owner 10 §8)
- Five evidence units per 15-mark answer (✅ owner 10 §20)
- Paragraph claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification (repository AGENTS.md answer pattern)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Word limits as stated (✅ owner 10).
**Links:** ANC-2025, ANC-2059, ANC-2058
**Trap:** Do not reduce a Discuss question to a single cause; for evaluate questions, state the verdict and its limits.
**Mains hook:** "Discuss the social and economic setting of Jainism and Buddhism and evaluate their long-term impact." Qualify each claim with a limit.
**Back:** *"Thesis, evidence, institution, spread, limit, verdict: six beats for a Jain-Buddhist answer."*

### GROUP — Mahājanapadas and the Rise of Magadha (basic owner 11 + advanced owner 11)

### Flashcard ANC-2063 — Concept: the Mahājanapada age as a connected early historic system
**Front:** What is the core idea of the Mahājanapada age, and what is its correct boundary?
**Definition (learn):** The period c. 600–200 BCE in which large territorial polities, ganas/saṅghas, forest tribes, trade, money, new elites and renunciant movements formed one connected early historic world; political conflict was a struggle for people, land and resources. The age is the context of the second urbanisation in the Gangetic basin. (✅ owner 11 basic §1; ✅ owner 11 advanced §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Large states: mahājanapadas, a transmitted classification (✅ owner 11)
- Gaṇas/saṅghas coexisted with monarchies (✅ owner 11)
- Urban/monetary base: NBPW, towns, trade routes, punch-marked coins (✅ owner 11 §13)
- Stops at the Mauryan threshold (Chandragupta's conquest is outside this owner) (✅ owner 11 §9, §18)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 600 BCE used as a convenient threshold, not a switch (✅ owner 11 §10). Chronology tables give c. 1000–600 BCE background and c. 322/321 BCE Mauryan threshold (✅ owner 11 §10).
**Links:** ANC-2064, ANC-2065, ANC-2075, ANC-2086, ANC-2094
**Trap:** "Sixteen fixed states existed in 600 BCE" is wrong; sixteen is a conventional textual list. (✅ owner 11 §19)
**Mains hook:** "The rise of the Mahājanapadas reflects the second urbanisation of north India, where surplus, warfare and territorial consolidation combined." Qualify with uneven regional development.
**Back:** *"Cities, kings, renouncers: one system, not a list of sixteen."*

### Flashcard ANC-2064 — The Aṅguttara Nikāya list of sixteen mahājanapadas and its variants
**Front:** Name the sixteen mahājanapadas of the Aṅguttara Nikāya list, and state what the list is not.
**Definition (learn):** The Aṅguttara Nikāya preserves the conventional list of sixteen large states associated with the Buddha's age: Kāśi, Kosala, Aṅga, Magadha, Vajji, Malla, Chedi, Vatsa, Kuru, Pañchāla, Matsya, Śūrasena, Aśmaka, Avanti, Gandhāra and Kamboja. It is a transmitted classification, not an archaeological count of sixteen simultaneous polities with fixed borders. (✅ owner 11 basic §1; ✅ owner 11 advanced §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Mahāvastu: similar list with substitutions such as Shibi and Dasharna (✅ owner 11 §9.2)
- Jain Bhagavatī Sūtra: a different eastern/southern political geography (✅ owner 11 §9.2)
- Puranic dynastic lists: contradictions and telescoping (✅ owner 11 §9.2)
- Omission does not prove that smaller polities did not exist (✅ owner 11 §11.1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sixteen states; Buddhist, Jain, Puranic traditions do not supply an identical map or chronology (✅ owner 11 basic §4).
**Links:** ANC-2063, ANC-2065, ANC-2066 to ANC-2074 (individual states), ANC-2098
**Trap:** "All lists and dynasties agree" is wrong; the variant lists are a classic trap. (✅ owner 11 §19)
**Mains hook:** Use the list to show that early state formation was textually conventional and regionally diverse.
**Back:** *"Kashi, Kosala, Anga, Magadha, Vajji, Malla, Chedi, Vatsa, Kuru, Panchala, Matsya, Surasena, Ashmaka, Avanti, Gandhara, Kamboja."*

### Flashcard ANC-2065 — The four powerful states and the political clusters
**Front:** Which four states were especially powerful, and how do the other mahājanapadas cluster politically?
**Definition (learn):** Magadha, Kosala, Vatsa and Avanti were the especially powerful states. Clusters: powerful monarchies (Magadha, Kosala, Vatsa, Avanti); eastern rivals (Kāśi, Aṅga, Vajji, Malla); older upper-plain polities (Kuru, Pañchāla); central/western connectors (Matsya, Śūrasena, Chedi, Avanti); north-western corridor (Gandhāra, Kamboja); trans-Vindhyan exception (Aśmaka). (✅ owner 11 basic §1, §11.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Monarchies: Magadha, Kosala, Vatsa, Avanti (✅ owner 11)
- Eastern: Kāśi, Aṅga, Vajji, Malla (✅ owner 11)
- Upper plain: Kuru, Pañchāla (✅ owner 11)
- Connectors: Matsya, Śūrasena, Chedi, Avanti (✅ owner 11)
- North-west: Gandhāra, Kamboja (✅ owner 11)
- Trans-Vindhyan: Aśmaka (✅ owner 11)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Most lay north of the Vindhyas, from the north-west frontier to Bihar (✅ owner 11 basic §4).
**Links:** ANC-2064, ANC-2066, ANC-2071, ANC-2073
**Trap:** Avanti is both a "powerful monarchy" and a "connector"; do not place Avanti in the Mahānadi basin. (✅ owner 11 §21)
**Mains hook:** Use clusters to argue for a regional map rather than a single hierarchy.
**Back:** *"Four powerful: Magadha, Kosala, Vatsa, Avanti. Clusters are eastern, upper-plain, connector, north-west, trans-Vindhyan."*

### Flashcard ANC-2066 — Aṅga and the Champā corridor
**Front:** What is the historical significance of Aṅga, and how is its annexation remembered?
**Definition (learn):** Aṅga (eastern Bihar; Champā–Ganga corridor; capital Champā) was annexed by Magadha under Bimbisāra in tradition. Its annexation removed an eastern rival and opened the Champā corridor; motives are tradition-mediated. Ajātaśatru's association with Champā is also recorded. (✅ owner 11 §11, §14)
**Breakdown:**
- Zone: eastern Bihar (✅ owner 11 §11)
- Capital: Champā (✅ owner 11 §11)
- Monarchy annexed by Bimbisāra (✅ owner 11 §11)
- Champā association with Ajātaśatru (✅ owner 11 §14)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Bimbisāra c. 545–493 BCE (Upinder Singh) or 544–492 BCE (R.S. Sharma textbook convention) (✅ owner 11).
**Links:** ANC-2065, ANC-2079, ANC-2080
**Trap:** The annexation is a tradition; do not present motives as verified. (✅ owner 11 §14)
**Mains hook:** Use Aṅga as the first step in Magadhan expansion; qualify it as traditional.
**Back:** *"Anga falls to Bimbisara; Champa opens the road east."*

### Flashcard ANC-2067 — Kāśi and Kosala: absorption, Sarayu and Shrāvastī
**Front:** How did Kāśi and Kosala relate, and what were Kosala's core features?
**Definition (learn):** Kāśi (middle Ganga, around Varanasi) was absorbed by Kosala; Kosala (Awadh/Sarayu basin; capital Śrāvastī, with Sāketa/Ayodhya also important) remained a major monarchy. "Kosala-Sarayu" is the secure pair for the 2025 PYQ. Kosala had a fertile Sarayu basin, Śrāvastī, and prior control of Kāśi. (✅ owner 11 §11, §14, §17.1, §21)
**Breakdown:**
- Kāśi: Varanasi; later absorbed by Kosala (✅ owner 11 §11)
- Kosala: Śrāvastī; Sāketa/Ayodhya (✅ owner 11 §11)
- Kosala–Magadha: Bimbisāra's Kosalan marriage with a Kāśi dowry tradition (✅ owner 11 §14)
- Kosala-Sarayu matched correctly (2025 Q17) (✅ owner 11 §21)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025 Prelims GS-I Set A Q17 pairs (see ANC-2095).
**Links:** ANC-2065, ANC-2080, ANC-2095
**Trap:** Kāśi did not remain an independent expansion rival after absorption; Magadha did not begin as the only expansionist power. (✅ owner 11 §14)
**Mains hook:** Use Kosala to show that Magadhan supremacy was contested by a strong northern/eastern monarchy.
**Back:** *"Kashi falls to Kosala; Kosala holds the Sarayu and Sravasti."*

### Flashcard ANC-2068 — Vajji/Vṛji: confederated clan oligarchy, not "the first democracy"
**Front:** What was Vajji's political form, and why is "first democracy" an unsafe claim?
**Definition (learn):** Vajji/Vṛji (north Bihar, north of the Ganga; capital Vaiśālī) was a confederated clan oligarchy, prominently the Licchhavi; it could mobilise a confederated north-Ganga coalition. Its political membership was far narrower than the total population; it was oligarchic, not a modern democracy. (✅ owner 11 §11, §12, §17.1, §19)
**Breakdown:**
- Zone: north Bihar (✅ owner 11 §11)
- Capital: Vaiśālī (✅ owner 11 §11)
- Form: confederated clan oligarchy; Licchhavi prominent (✅ owner 11 §11)
- Membership: elite male clan assembly; women, slaves, labourers, artisans, clients and non-clan residents excluded (✅ owner 11 §12)
- Conflict: Ajātaśatru's prolonged war, Pāṭaligrāma fortification, Vassakāra dissension story (✅ owner 11 §14)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Texts mention assembly hall (santhāgāra) and quorum/voting token (śalākā) terms (✅ owner 11 §12).
**Links:** ANC-2011, ANC-2069, ANC-2080, ANC-2093
**Trap:** "Vajji was the world's first democracy" is a hostile-search trap; describe a restricted clan oligarchy/confederacy (✅ owner 11 §19). Magadha's defeat of Vajji does not prove monarchy is always superior (✅ owner 11 §12).
**Mains hook:** Use Vajji to show political diversity in early states; qualify with the oligarchic social base.
**Back:** *"Vajji: clans in council, not citizens in a modern republic."*

### Flashcard ANC-2069 — Malla: gaṇa with more than one centre
**Front:** What is known about the Malla mahājanapada and its centres?
**Definition (learn):** The Malla mahājanapada (eastern Uttar Pradesh zone) had Kusinārā and Pāvā as centres; it is a gaṇa/confederated tradition with more than one centre. Shakyas are cited as a smaller adjacent example of a gaṇa. (✅ owner 11 §11, §12)
**Breakdown:**
- Zone: eastern UP zone (✅ owner 11 §11)
- Centres: Kusinārā and Pāvā (✅ owner 11 §11)
- Form: gaṇa/confederated (✅ owner 11 §11)
- Adjacent example: Shākyas (✅ owner 11 §12)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Buddha's death at Kuśinagara (✅ owner 10 cross-reference).
**Links:** ANC-2027, ANC-2065, ANC-2068
**Trap:** Do not confuse Kusinārā (Malla) with another city or the Shākya capital at Kapilavastu (⚠️ standard knowledge).
**Mains hook:** Use Malla to show gaṇa diversity beyond Vajji.
**Back:** *"Malla: Kusinara and Pava, a gana with two centres."*

### Flashcard ANC-2070 — Vatsa: Kauśāmbī, Udayana and the political-commercial node
**Front:** What do we know about Vatsa, and what caution applies to its political history?
**Definition (learn):** Vatsa (lower Yamuna and Ganga–Yamuna junction routes; capital Kauśāmbī) was a monarchy and a major political-commercial centre. Its political history is shaped by Udayana–Pradyota marriage and rivalry traditions, which are strongly literary and romantic. (✅ owner 11 §11, §14, §17.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Zone and capital: Kauśāmbī; Yamuna-Ganga route (✅ owner 11)
- Form: monarchy (✅ owner 11)
- Udayana–Pradyota marriage/rivalry traditions (✅ owner 11 §14)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Vatsa is one of the four powerful states (✅ owner 11).
**Links:** ANC-2065, ANC-2071
**Trap:** Literary romance is not a verified treaty archive; do not treat Udayana's marriage as a dated fact. (✅ owner 11 §14)
**Mains hook:** Use Vatsa to show how route and commerce, not only conquest, created power.
**Back:** *"Vatsa: Kaushambi on the river, romance in the tradition, route in the fact."*

### Flashcard ANC-2071 — Avanti: western alternative to Magadha (Malwa, Ujjain–Māhiṣmatī, Shiśunāga)
**Front:** What made Avanti a strong western alternative, and how was it absorbed?
**Definition (learn):** Avanti (Malwa and the Narmadā-linked corridor; capitals Ujjayinī and Māhiṣmatī) commanded routes and iron-bearing hinterlands. Its Pradyota rivalry with Magadha and Vatsa ended in defeat or absorption, credited in tradition to Śiśunāga. Avanti also had access to iron, so iron cannot be a Magadhan monopoly. (✅ owner 11 §11, §14, §17, §17.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Zone: Malwa; Narmadā corridor (✅ owner 11)
- Capitals: Ujjayinī and Māhiṣmatī (✅ owner 11 §11)
- Rivalry with Magadha: Pradyota; absorption credited to Śiśunāga (✅ owner 11 §14)
- Iron: Avanti also accessed iron (✅ owner 11 §1, §17)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Often northern/southern divisions (✅ owner 11 §11).
**Links:** ANC-2073, ANC-2082, ANC-2078, ANC-2095
**Trap:** Avanti–Mahānadi is incorrect (2025 Q17); Avanti centres on Malwa and the Ujjain–Māhiṣmatī/Narmadā corridor (✅ owner 11 §21). "Magadha alone had iron" is wrong (✅ owner 11 §19).
**Mains hook:** Use Avanti to test the iron-monopoly thesis.
**Back:** *"Avanti: Malwa and Narmada, Ujjain and Mahishmati, beaten by Shishunaga's Magadha."*

### Flashcard ANC-2072 — Kamboja: north-western frontier polity with uncertain limits
**Front:** Why is Kamboja hard to define, and why is "Kamboja–Vipāśā" incorrect?
**Definition (learn):** Kamboja is a north-western highland/frontier polity beyond Gandhāra; capital Rājapura is given in some later traditions only; political form and exact limits are uncertain. It is not secure to define it by the Vipāśā river. (✅ owner 11 §11, §21)
**Breakdown:**
- Zone: north-western highland/frontier (✅ owner 11 §11)
- Capital: Rājapura, not secure (✅ owner 11 §11)
- Political form and limits uncertain (✅ owner 11 §11)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025 Prelims GS-I Set A Q17: pair (II) Kamboja–Vipāśā is incorrect per the owner-recorded route (✅ owner 11 §21).
**Links:** ANC-2074, ANC-2095
**Trap:** Do not match Kamboja to a famous river by association. (✅ owner 11 §21 execution method)
**Mains hook:** Use Kamboja as an example of frontier uncertainty.
**Back:** *"Kamboja: north-west frontier, not a river pair."*

### Flashcard ANC-2073 — Aśmaka/Assaka: the trans-Vindhyan exception on the Godavarī
**Front:** Where was Aśmaka, and why is it an exception?
**Definition (learn):** Aśmaka/Assaka lay in the Godavarī corridor, south of the Vindhyas in the Buddhist list; its centre is Potana/Podana. It was a monarchy. Its name and geography must remain source-specific. (✅ owner 11 §11, §11.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Zone: Godavarī corridor (✅ owner 11 §11)
- Centre: Potana/Podana (✅ owner 11 §11)
- Form: monarchy (✅ owner 11 §11)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025 Prelims GS-I Set A Q17: Aśmaka–Godavarī is correct (✅ owner 11 §21).
**Links:** ANC-2064, ANC-2065, ANC-2095
**Trap:** Trans-Vindhyan does not mean Deccan Satavahana; keep the Mahājanapada context. (✅ owner 11 §11)
**Mains hook:** Use Aśmaka to show that the classic list extended southward.
**Back:** *"Ashmaka: Godavari corridor, the one trans-Vindhyan name on the sixteen."*

### Flashcard ANC-2074 — Gandhāra: Taxila and the north-western routes
**Front:** What was the core of Gandhāra and why does it matter?
**Definition (learn):** Gandhāra (Taxila; Peshawar–Rawalpindi and north-western routes) was a monarchy in major early traditions, connected to transregional routes. Its capital was Taxila. Gandhāra and Aśmaka show distinct north-western and trans-Vindhyan trajectories. (✅ owner 11 §11, §17.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Zone: Peshawar–Rawalpindi; Taxila (✅ owner 11 §11)
- Form: monarchy (✅ owner 11 §11)
- Connection: transregional routes (✅ owner 11 §11)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Taxila as capital (✅ owner 11 §11).
**Links:** ANC-2072, ANC-2056 (Buddhist north-west), owner 12 (Achaemenid and Greek contact)
**Trap:** "Gandhāra was part of Magadha" is not supported by the owner. Keep Gandhāra's north-west trajectory separate.
**Mains hook:** Use Gandhāra to link state formation with the north-west frontier (later Achaemenid/Greek contexts).
**Back:** *"Gandhara: Taxila and the north-west road."*

### Flashcard ANC-2075 — Monarchy versus gaṇa-saṅgha: axes of comparison
**Front:** How do monarchy and gaṇa-saṅgha differ on authority, decision-making, mobilisation and social base?
**Definition (learn):** Monarchy: the king, dynastic household and court; final command concentrated; mobilisation potentially faster; governance through officials and local elites. Gaṇa/saṅgha: an elite male clan assembly with a chief or smaller executive; decisions collective among political members; shared elite stake but coalition and faction problems; membership narrower than total population. (✅ owner 11 §12)
**Breakdown:**
- Authority: king vs assembly with chief (✅ owner 11 §12)
- Decision: concentrated command vs collective decision (✅ owner 11 §12)
- Mobilisation: faster revenue concentration vs coalition problems (✅ owner 11 §12)
- Examples: Magadha, Kosala, Vatsa, Avanti vs Vajji, Malla (✅ owner 11 §12)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Texts mention santhāgāra, śalākā (✅ owner 11 §12).
**Links:** ANC-2011, ANC-2068, ANC-2069, ANC-2093
**Trap:** Do not convert santhāgāra or śalākā into a modern parliament. Do not present gaṇas as universal citizenship or popular sovereignty. (✅ owner 11 §9.1, §12)
**Mains hook:** Compare the two axes on authority, membership, mobilisation, succession and limits for a 15-mark answer.
**Back:** *"King commands, clan assembly deliberates: both are elite, neither is modern."*

### Flashcard ANC-2076 — Rājagṛha versus Pāṭaliputra: two capitals, two logics
**Front:** How do Rājagṛha (Rajgir) and Pāṭaliputra (Pāṭaligrāma) differ in setting, role and evidence?
**Definition (learn):** Rājagṛha/Girivraja was the earliest capital, a hill-enclosed basin, defended by five hills and massive stone wall circuits. Pāṭaligrāma/Pāṭaliputra rose later on a riverine plain near Ganga-system approaches; it was a communication point, frontier fort and enlarged territorial command. (✅ owner 11 basic §1; ✅ owner 11 §16.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Rājagṛha: five hills; stone walls; Bimbisāra/Ajātaśatru centre (✅ owner 11 §1, §16.1)
- Pāṭaliputra: Ajātaśatru's fortification; Udayin's capital tradition (✅ owner 11 §16.1)
- Archaeology: Rājagṛha's outer walls are real but their earliest dates debated; NBPW at Kumrahar/Bulandibagh (✅ owner 11 §16.1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Ganga–Son junction is a useful shorthand; river channels changed (✅ owner 11 §16.1).
**Links:** ANC-2077, ANC-2080, ANC-2081
**Trap:** "Rajgir and Pāṭaliputra were the same capital" is wrong; Rājagṛha was earlier, Pāṭaliputra rose later. Mauryan remains at Kumrahar must not be back-projected to the Haryanka phase. (✅ owner 11 §4, §5, §16.1)
**Mains hook:** Use the two capitals to show how capitals encode geography, defence and ambition (advanced §7).
**Back:** *"Rajgir: five hills first. Pataliputra: river second. Not the same capital."*

### Flashcard ANC-2077 — Pāṭaliputra's archaeology: NBPW at Kumrahar and Bulandibagh
**Front:** What do Kumrahar and Bulandibagh show, and what must not be assumed?
**Definition (learn):** NBPW at Kumrahar and Bulandibagh supports early historic settlement at Pāṭaliputra; the earliest plan remains poorly known. Famous Mauryan remains at Kumrahar must not be back-projected into the Haryanka phase. (✅ owner 11 basic §4; ✅ owner 11 advanced §4; ✅ owner 11 §16.1)
**Breakdown:**
- NBPW: early historic horizon (✅ owner 11 advanced §1)
- Earliest plan: poorly known (✅ owner 11 §16.1)
- Mauryan Kumrahar: later (✅ owner 11 §16.1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Pāṭaliputra's greatness linked to Ganga–Son routes (✅ owner 11 advanced §4).
**Links:** ANC-2076, ANC-2087, ANC-2088
**Trap:** "Pāṭaliputra was important from the Rig Vedic age" is wrong; it belongs to the early historic / Mahājanapada–Mauryan trajectory (✅ owner 11 advanced §5).
**Mains hook:** Use archaeology to test a text-based claim about Pāṭaliputra.
**Back:** *"Kumrahar: NBPW shows early settlement; the Mauryan hall stays Mauryan."*

### Flashcard ANC-2078 — Why Magadha rose: multicausal, comparative and contingent
**Front:** What factors made Magadha dominant, and what is the qualified verdict?
**Definition (learn):** Factors: floodplain and rivers (fertile zones, transport, crossings); southern uplands and resources (forests, timber, elephants, ore-bearing zones); iron (support for tools, crafts and weapons); capitals (Rājagṛha's hill defence; Pāṭaliputra's riverine command); agrarian/urban base (surplus, towns, trade, tolls); political agency (Bimbisāra, Ajātaśatru, Śiśunāga, Nanda consolidation); administration and army; rival sequence. Verdict: conjunctural, cumulative and contingent. (✅ owner 11 §17, §17.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Geography, iron, rivers, capitals, economy, agency, administration, sequence (✅ owner 11 §17)
- Textbook factors also include elephants and forest resources (✅ owner 11 basic §1)
- Rivals with similar advantages: Kosala, Vatsa, Aṅga, Avanti (✅ owner 11 §17)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Upinder Singh accepts iron as a factor, not a monopoly (✅ owner 11 basic §1).
**Links:** ANC-2079 to ANC-2084, ANC-2089, ANC-2090, ANC-2099
**Trap:** "Magadha rose only because of one great ruler" and "Iron monopoly alone explains Magadha" are both wrong. (✅ owner 11 §5, §19)
**Mains hook:** "Explain Magadha's rise as a convergence of geography, resources and statecraft." Qualify: contingent, not inevitable.
**Back:** *"Rivers, hills, iron, rulers, sequence: convergence, not one magic key."*

### Flashcard ANC-2079 — Bimbisāra: Aṅga, marriages and textbook dating
**Front:** What did Bimbisāra achieve, and how is his chronology stated?
**Definition (learn):** Bimbisāra's base was Rājagṛha; he annexed Aṅga and built marriage and diplomatic networks: a Kosalan marriage with a Kāśi dowry tradition, a Licchhavi/Videha connection and a Madra princess. These are diplomatic traditions indicating strategy and prestige, not a verified treaty archive. Textbook dates: c. 544–492 BCE (R.S. Sharma convention); c. 545–493 BCE (Upinder Singh). (✅ owner 11 §10, §14, §15; ✅ owner 11 advanced mini-timeline)
**Breakdown:**
- Aṅga annexation (✅ owner 11)
- Kosalan marriage with Kāśi dowry tradition (✅ owner 11 §14)
- Licchhavi/Videha and Madra connections (✅ owner 11 §14)
- Relations with Avanti traditions (✅ owner 11 §15)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 544–492 BCE is "not an uncontested absolute chronology" (✅ owner 11 basic §4, ⚠️ owner flag).
**Links:** ANC-2066, ANC-2078, ANC-2080
**Trap:** Do not treat textbook dates as exact; attribute and retain contradictory successions. (✅ owner 11 §19)
**Mains hook:** Use Bimbisāra's networks to show that diplomacy and conquest worked together.
**Back:** *"Anga taken, Kosala married, Rajagriha ruled: 544–492 by textbook convention."*

### Flashcard ANC-2080 — Ajātaśatru: Kosala, Vajji, Pāṭaligrāma and the Vassakāra story
**Front:** What wars and fortifications are attributed to Ajātaśatru, and what caution applies?
**Definition (learn):** Ajātaśatru's conflicts with Kosala (Prasenajit, followed by settlement) and Vajji; the Pāṭaligrāma fortification; expansion north of the Ganga; the Vassakāra dissension story (a moralised narrative). Patricide, weapons and Vassakāra details are narrative traditions. (✅ owner 11 §14, §15)
**Breakdown:**
- Kosala: Ajātaśatru–Prasenajit conflict and settlement (✅ owner 11 §14)
- Vajji: prolonged conflict; coalition, intelligence and fortification (✅ owner 11 §14)
- Pāṭaligrāma fortification (✅ owner 11 §14, §15)
- Ajātaśatru associated with Champā (✅ owner 11 §14)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 5th century BCE (⚠️ owner mini-timeline); "New Rājagṛha walls" linked to his time (✅ owner advanced §4).
**Links:** ANC-2068, ANC-2076, ANC-2079, ANC-2081
**Trap:** Patricide is narrative tradition, not a verified fact. (✅ owner 11 §15)
**Mains hook:** Use the Vajji war to show coalition, fortification and intelligence in state-making; qualify the moralised narrative.
**Back:** *"Ajatashatru: Kosala, Vajji, Pataligrama, New Rajagriha walls, and a tale of dissension."*

### Flashcard ANC-2081 — Udayin/Udayibhadda: Pāṭaliputra's founding tradition
**Front:** What is Udayin (Udayibhadda) associated with?
**Definition (learn):** Udayin/Udayibhadda is associated with founding or establishing Pāṭaliputra as the capital; founder, successor sequence and chronology vary. (✅ owner 11 §15, §16.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Capital tradition: Pāṭaliputra (✅ owner 11 §16.1)
- Source limit: sequence and dates vary (✅ owner 11 §15)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated in owner.
**Links:** ANC-2076, ANC-2077
**Trap:** Do not treat Udayin's foundation as one securely dated event. (✅ owner 11 §15)
**Mains hook:** Not a central Mains item; use for Pāṭaliputra's capital shift.
**Back:** *"Udayin: Pataliputra's founding tradition."*

### Flashcard ANC-2082 — Śiśunāga: from amātya to victor over Avanti
**Front:** What is Śiśunāga's place in the Haryanka–Śiśunāga–Nanda sequence?
**Definition (learn):** Śiśunāga is traditionally an official/amātya elevated after the Haryankas and credited with ending Avanti's independent power. Election, ancestry, capital and annexation details are not uniform. (✅ owner 11 §15)
**Breakdown:**
- Origin: amātya elevated (✅ owner 11 §15)
- Achievement: ended Avanti's independent power (✅ owner 11 §14, §15)
- Caveat: sources differ on ancestry and capital (✅ owner 11 §15)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 5th–4th century BCE sequence (⚠️ owner §10: Shishunaga consolidation in the fifth–fourth centuries).
**Links:** ANC-2071, ANC-2085
**Trap:** "Shishunaga destroyed Avanti in one battle" is not stated. Keep to "credited with ending independent power". (✅ owner 11 §14)
**Mains hook:** Use Śiśunāga's Avanti victory in the rival-sequence argument.
**Back:** *"Shishunaga: amatya rises, Avanti falls."*

### Flashcard ANC-2083 — Kālāśoka/Kākavarṇa: Pāṭaliputra and the second council link
**Front:** What do traditions link Kālāśoka/Kākavarṇa with?
**Definition (learn):** Kālāśoka/Kākavarṇa is linked in traditions with Pāṭaliputra/Vaiśālī and with the second Buddhist council (patron Kālāśoka per Wikipedia). Names and sequence vary. (✅ owner 11 §15; ✅ Wikipedia "Buddhist councils")
**Breakdown:**
- Patron of Second Council: Kālāśoka (✅ Wikipedia)
- Pāṭaliputra/Vaiśālī link: tradition (✅ owner 11 §15)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No year asserted.
**Links:** ANC-2043, ANC-2085
**Trap:** Do not treat Kālāśoka as securely dated in the Haryanka sequence. (✅ owner 11 §15)
**Mains hook:** Use to show that councils and royal sequences overlap in tradition.
**Back:** *"Kalashoka: Vaishali council patron, Pataliputra tradition."*

### Flashcard ANC-2084 — Nandas: Mahāpadma, Dhana Nanda and the fiscal-military reputation
**Front:** What do the Nanda traditions say, and what caution applies to army figures?
**Definition (learn):** Mahāpadma Nanda (Ugraseṇa traditions) was the founder of the Nanda line with wide conquest and fiscal concentration in differing traditions; founder names and "low origin" stories conflict and are socially polemical. Dhana Nanda (last Nanda) was wealthy and militarily formidable at the Mauryan threshold. Indian and Greek names do not align neatly; army figures are rhetorical, not census data. (✅ owner 11 §15)
**Breakdown:**
- Mahāpadma Nanda: wide conquest and fiscal concentration (✅ owner 11 §15)
- Dhana Nanda: wealth and military strength (✅ owner 11 §15)
- Greek accounts: outsider, rhetorical (✅ owner 11 §9.2)
- Nanda fourth century BCE (✅ owner 11 §10)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Fourth-century BCE Nandas; overthrow c. 322/321 BCE (✅ owner 11 §10).
**Links:** ANC-2078, ANC-2085, ANC-2086, owner 12 (Greek contacts), owner 14 (Mauryan threshold)
**Trap:** "Nandas had precisely reported huge armies" is wrong; treat figures as rhetoric. (✅ owner 11 §19)
**Mains hook:** Use Nanda fiscal-military concentration as the immediate base for the Mauryas.
**Back:** *"Mahapadma founds, Dhana wealth-hoards: army numbers are rhetoric, not census."*

### Flashcard ANC-2085 — Dynastic ladder: Haryanka → Śiśunāga → Nanda
**Front:** What is the conventional Magadhan dynastic sequence, and what is contested?
**Definition (learn):** The conventional sequence is Haryanka → Śiśunāga → Nanda; Buddhist, Jain and Puranic sources disagree on names, reigns, succession, patricide stories and dates. Haryanka rulers: Bimbisāra, Ajātaśatru, Udayin/Udayibhadda. Śiśunāga: credited with ending Avanti. Nanda: Mahāpadma and Dhana Nanda traditions. (✅ owner 11 §15)
**Breakdown:**
- Haryanka: Bimbisāra; Ajātaśatru; Udayin (✅ owner 11 §15)
- Śiśunāga (✅ owner 11 §15)
- Nanda: Mahāpadma; Dhana (✅ owner 11 §15)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Fifth–fourth centuries BCE (✅ owner 11 §10).
**Links:** ANC-2079, ANC-2080, ANC-2081, ANC-2082, ANC-2084, ANC-2086
**Trap:** The ladder is conventional; do not present it as a secure king list. (✅ owner 11 §15)
**Mains hook:** Use the ladder as a skeleton, with the caution that it is retrospective.
**Back:** *"Haryanka, Shishunaga, Nanda: the ladder is conventional, not verified."*

### Flashcard ANC-2086 — Chronology ladder: Mahājanapada to Mauryan threshold
**Front:** What is the chronological sequence from Later Vedic territoriality to the Nanda overthrow?
**Definition (learn):** c. 1000–600 BCE: Later Vedic territoriality (background). c. seventh–sixth centuries BCE onward: early NBPW and clearer large-polity traditions. Sixth–fifth centuries BCE: competition among Magadha, Kosala, Vatsa and Avanti. Fifth–fourth: Haryanka expansion, Vajji conflict, Śiśunāga consolidation. Fourth century: Nanda concentration. c. 322/321 BCE: Nanda overthrow and transition to Mauryan unification. (✅ owner 11 §10)
**Breakdown:** Six phases with caution column (✅ owner 11 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As stated; ceramic chronologies vary by site (✅ owner 11 §10).
**Links:** ANC-2063, ANC-2085, ANC-2094
**Trap:** Do not treat c. 600 BCE as a switch that created sixteen states, cities, coinage and NBPW simultaneously. (✅ owner 11 §10)
**Mains hook:** A ladder for the chronology part of a 20-mark answer.
**Back:** *"1000-600 background, 600 threshold, 322 Mauryan door: never a switch."*

### Flashcard ANC-2087 — Second urbanisation: an urban identification bundle
**Front:** What criteria combine to identify a city in the second urbanisation, and what can no single indicator prove?
**Definition (learn):** Second urbanisation denotes renewed town growth after the Harappan decline, especially in the Gangetic plains and connected corridors. An urban identification should combine settlement size/density, fortification, specialised crafts, exchange and coins, administrative/religious functions, and a supporting hinterland. (✅ owner 11 §13.1)
**Breakdown:**
- NBPW: fine-ware horizon; cannot prove a city, ethnicity or ruler (✅ owner 11 §13.1)
- Ramparts and walls: organised labour and defence; not centralised monarchy (✅ owner 11 §13.1)
- Craft debris: specialised production; not guild scale (✅ owner 11 §13.1)
- Punch-marked coins: monetised exchange; not end of barter (✅ owner 11 §13.1)
- Literary "great city" labels: remembered importance (✅ owner 11 §13.1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Harappa was the first urbanisation (✅ owner 11 basic §5).
**Links:** ANC-2063, ANC-2088, ANC-2089, ANC-2093
**Trap:** "Second urbanisation began with Harappa" is wrong. (✅ owner 11 basic §5)
**Mains hook:** Use the bundle approach to show that urbanisation is a multi-indicator claim.
**Back:** *"Size, walls, crafts, coins, rites, hinterland: a bundle, not a single sign."*

### Flashcard ANC-2088 — Northern Black Polished Ware (NBPW): chronology and limits
**Front:** What is NBPW, what is its broad chronology, and what can it not prove?
**Definition (learn):** Northern Black Polished Ware (NBPW) is a fine-ware production and circulation horizon, broadly placed from the seventh century BCE to the second/first centuries BCE in one standard chronology, with regional and site variation. It is not always literally black, exclusively northern, or evidence of one polishing method. (✅ owner 11 §13.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Fine-ware production/circulation (✅ owner 11 §13.1)
- Chronology: c. 7th c. to 2nd/1st c. BCE (✅ owner 11 §13.1)
- Not a city, ethnicity, named ruler or exact boundary (✅ owner 11 §13.1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** NBPW at Kumrahar/Bulandibagh (✅ owner 11 advanced §1).
**Links:** ANC-2077, ANC-2087, ANC-2086
**Trap:** "NBPW proves a city or Magadhan rule" is wrong. (✅ owner 11 §19)
**Mains hook:** Use NBPW as one material marker within a multi-indicator bundle.
**Back:** *"NBPW: fine-ware horizon, seventh to second centuries, not a city-certificate."*

### Flashcard ANC-2089 — Crafts, corporate terms, routes and the gahapati–seṭṭhi distinction
**Front:** What do śreṇi, pūga, nigama, gahapati and seṭṭhi mean, and what are Uttarāpatha and Dakṣiṇāpatha?
**Definition (learn):** Śreṇi, pūga and nigama suggest organised groups, but later Jātaka guild detail cannot be projected into the sixth century BCE. Gahapati denotes a substantial property-owning producer/householder; seṭṭhi more often denotes an urban business and credit elite. Uttarāpatha (northern route) and Dakṣiṇāpatha (southern route) are corridors linking changing nodes, not uniformly paved imperial highways. (✅ owner 11 §13.3)
**Breakdown:**
- Corporate terms: śreṇi, pūga, nigama (✅ owner 11 §13.3)
- Gahapati: producer/householder (✅ owner 11 §13.3)
- Seṭṭhi: urban business/credit elite (✅ owner 11 §13.3)
- Routes: Uttarāpatha; Dakṣiṇāpatha (✅ owner 11 §13.3)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Guilds not fully developed corporations by 600 BCE (✅ owner 11 §19).
**Links:** ANC-2087, ANC-2090, 
**Trap:** Gahapati and seṭṭhi are not interchangeable merchant names. (✅ owner 11 §13.3)
**Mains hook:** Use the terms to qualify claims about merchant power.
**Back:** *"Gahapati holds land, settha lends money; Uttarapatha and Dakshinapatha are roads, not highways."*

### Flashcard ANC-2090 — Iron causal firewall: resource → access → labour → institution → capacity
