**Front:** Why is iron not a mechanical cause of agriculture, surplus, cities or states?
**Definition (learn):** Iron artefacts increased in many first-millennium BCE contexts, aiding clearance, cultivation, crafts and weapons. But rice cultivation, rainfall, alluvium, labour, land control and knowledge also shaped production; surplus became state capacity only through extraction, transport, officials, coercion and legitimacy. The firewall: resource → access → labour/skill → institution → capacity. (✅ owner 11 §13.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Iron: clearance, cultivation, crafts, weapons (✅ owner 11 §13.2)
- Surplus: extraction, transport, officials, coercion, legitimacy (✅ owner 11 §13.2)
- Textual fractions (e.g., one-sixth) are not a verified uniform tax rate (✅ owner 11 §13.2)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Texts mention produce shares, tribute, tolls; one-sixth is not a verified rate (✅ owner 11 §13.2).
**Links:** ANC-2005 (Later Vedic iron), ANC-2078, ANC-2087
**Trap:** "Iron caused second urbanisation and states" is wrong. (✅ owner 11 §19)
**Mains hook:** Use the firewall to write a causal paragraph with a limit.
**Back:** *"Resource, access, labour, institution, capacity: iron is one link, not the chain."*

### Flashcard ANC-2091 — Coinage: punch-marked coins, hoards and uneven monetisation
**Front:** What do punch-marked coins and coin terms show, and what can they not show?
**Definition (learn):** Silver punch-marked coins, hoards and textual coin vocabulary indicate uneven monetisation and valuation. Deposition date differs from minting date; issuers can be uncertain; exchange in kind continued. Most early punch-marked coins do not identify one king. (✅ owner 11 §9.2, §13.1, §13.3)
**Breakdown:**
- Monetised valuation and possible fiscal use (✅ owner 11 §13.1)
- Hoards: deposition date ≠ minting date (✅ owner 11 §13.3)
- Issuers uncertain; series vary (✅ owner 11 §19)
- Exchange in kind continued (✅ owner 11 §13.3)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No single issuing Magadhan monopoly (✅ owner 11 §19).
**Links:** ANC-2087, ANC-2089, ANC-2093
**Trap:** "All punch-marked coins were issued by Magadha" and "end of barter" are both wrong. (✅ owner 11 §13.1, §19)
**Mains hook:** Use coins to show monetisation is uneven and regional.
**Back:** *"Punch-marked: silver, hoarded, issuer unknown, barter lived on."*

### Flashcard ANC-2092 — Interstate competition: Kāśi–Kosala, Kosala–Magadha, Avanti, Vatsa (sequence)
**Front:** What were the main pre-Magadhan supremacy contests, and what is the lesson?
**Definition (learn):** Aṅga (Bimbisāra's annexation); Kāśi–Kosala (Kāśi absorbed by Kosala); Kosala–Magadha (marriage/dowry traditions followed by Ajātaśatru–Prasenajit conflict and settlement); Vajji (prolonged conflict and fortification); Avanti (Pradyota rivalry with Magadha and Vatsa; defeat or absorption credited to Śiśunāga); Vatsa (Kauśāmbī and Udayana–Pradyota traditions). Marriage was strategic but did not permanently prevent war. (✅ owner 11 §14)
**Breakdown:** Six rival relations (✅ owner 11 §14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Vassakāra narrative is moralised/retrospective (✅ owner 11 §14).
**Links:** ANC-2066 to ANC-2071, ANC-2078
**Trap:** Magadha did not begin as the only expansionist power. (✅ owner 11 §14)
**Mains hook:** Use the sequence to show that Magadha's supremacy was contingent.
**Back:** *"Anga, Kashi, Kosala, Vajji, Avanti, Vatsa: a sequence of defeats and marriages."*

### Flashcard ANC-2093 — Vajji: "first democracy" trap and the oligarchy limit
**Front:** Why must the "Vajji was the world's first democracy" claim be corrected?
**Definition (learn):** The owner's correction: describe a restricted clan oligarchy/confederacy and identify the evidence limit. Membership excluded women, slaves, dependent labourers, many artisans, clients and non-clan residents. Republics were oligarchic, not modern democracies. (✅ owner 11 §12, §19; ✅ owner 11 §3 table row 3)
**Breakdown:**
- Oligarchy: elite male clans (✅ owner 11 §12)
- Excluded groups (✅ owner 11 §12)
- Oligarchic republics, not democracy (✅ owner 11 advanced: republics were oligarchic)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated; prolonged war with Magadha (✅ owner 11 §14).
**Links:** ANC-2068, ANC-2075
**Trap:** Avoid "republic" without clarifying its oligarchic base. (✅ owner 11 §8)
**Mains hook:** Use Vajji to show the diversity of early polity, with a limit.
**Back:** *"Clan council, not citizen vote: the republic was an oligarchy."*

### Flashcard ANC-2094 — Mauryan threshold: what Topic 11 owns and does not own
**Front:** Where does the Mahājanapada owner stop, and what does it hand to the Mauryan owner?
**Definition (learn):** The owner stops at the Mauryan threshold: Nanda consolidation created the immediate territorial, fiscal and military base inherited by the Mauryas; the Nanda overthrow and transition c. 322/321 BCE. Chandragupta's campaigns, Kauṭilyan state theory, Mauryan administration, Aśoka and imperial decline belong to the next owner. (✅ owner 11 §9, §10, §18)
**Breakdown:**
- Owned here: Nanda base; threshold c. 322/321 BCE (✅ owner 11 §18)
- Not owned here: Chandragupta; Arthaśāstra categories; Mauryan administration; Aśoka (✅ owner 11 §18)
- Arthaśāstra categories cannot be projected into Bimbisāra's administration (✅ owner 11 §16.2)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 322/321 BCE (✅ owner 11 §10).
**Links:** ANC-2086, ANC-2085, owner 14 (Mauryan Empire)
**Trap:** "Mauryan institutions already existed unchanged in Bimbisāra's time" is wrong. (✅ owner 11 §19)
**Mains hook:** Use the threshold to bridge into Mauryan administration without projecting back.
**Back:** *"Nandas leave the base; the Mauryas take the door at 322."*

### Flashcard ANC-2095 — PYQ 2025 GS-I Q17: region–river pairs (Aśmaka, Kamboja, Avanti, Kosala)
**Front:** Which of the four pairs are correctly matched in PYQ 2025 Q17, and what is the official key?
**Definition (learn):** Pairs: (I) Aśmaka–Godāvarī: correct; (II) Kamboja–Vipāśā: incorrect; (III) Avanti–Mahānadī: incorrect (Avanti centres on Malwa and the Ujjain–Māhiṣmatī/Narmadā corridor); (IV) Kosala–Sarayū: correct. The owner records the official local Set-A key as B (only two pairs correct). (✅ owner 11 §21; key as recorded in the owner's PYQ integration)
**Breakdown:**
- Method: political core → river basin/corridor → source-specific variation → count (✅ owner 11 §21)
- Each pair assessed separately (✅ owner 11 §21)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025 Prelims GS-I Set A Q17; local key recorded as B in owner 11 §21. ⚠️ Key is taken from the owner; not independently verified.
**Links:** ANC-2065, ANC-2071, ANC-2072, ANC-2073, ANC-2067
**Trap:** Do not match a polity to a famous river by association alone. (✅ owner 11 §21)
**Mains hook:** Not a Mains item; a Prelims pair-matching trap.
**Back:** *"Ashmaka-Godavari, Kosala-Sarayu: right; Kamboja-Vipasha and Avanti-Mahanadi: wrong."*

### Flashcard ANC-2096 — Source ladder for Mahājanapada evidence
**Front:** Which source classes can establish which claims, and what is each one's caution?
**Definition (learn):** Aṅguttara Nikāya: conventional list of sixteen; composite transmitted text. Mahāvastu: similar list with substitutions. Jain Bhagavatī Sūtra: different eastern/southern geography; later. Puranic lists: contradictions and retrospective legitimation. Buddhist/Jain narratives: courts, wars and patronage shaped by sectarian purpose. Greek accounts of the Nanda horizon: names and army numbers later, outsider and often rhetorical. Archaeology: NBPW, fortifications, crafts and stratigraphy; a layer does not name a ruler. Numismatics: punch-marked coins rarely name a king. (✅ owner 11 §9.2)
**Breakdown:** Eight evidence classes with mandatory cautions (✅ owner 11 §9.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Source rule: list, ruler story, excavated layer and coin distribution are independent classes (✅ owner 11 §9.2).
**Links:** ANC-2064, ANC-2077, ANC-2084, ANC-2091
**Trap:** Do not use one class as automatic proof of another. (✅ owner 11 §9.2)
**Mains hook:** A source-critical line for "critically examine" questions.
**Back:** *"List, legend, layer, coin: four independent witnesses, none proves the others."*

### Flashcard ANC-2097 — Mahājanapada hostile-search trap register
**Front:** Which unsafe claims about the Mahājanapada and Magadha must be corrected?
**Definition (learn):** The owner's trap register includes: sixteen fixed states in 600 BCE; all lists and dynasties agree; Vajji was the world's first democracy; NBPW proves a city or Magadhan rule; iron caused second urbanisation; all punch-marked coins were Magadhan; guilds were fully developed by 600 BCE; Magadha alone had fertile rivers, iron or towns; Bimbisāra/Ajātaśatru chronology is exact; Nandas had precise armies; one great king created supremacy; Mauryan institutions existed unchanged. (✅ owner 11 §19)
**Breakdown:** Each has a marks-safe correction in the owner (✅ owner 11 §19).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** ANC-2063, ANC-2068, ANC-2078, ANC-2084, ANC-2087, ANC-2088, ANC-2091, ANC-2094
**Trap:** "One great king", "exactly", "all", "alone" are the telltale words.
**Mains hook:** Use corrections to qualify any causal answer.
**Back:** *"Exactly, all, alone, one king: four words that flag a false universal."*

### Flashcard ANC-2098 — Mahājanapada answer architecture (10, 15, 20 marks)
**Front:** What answer skeletons does the owner give for Mahājanapada and Magadha questions?
**Definition (learn):** 10 marks/150 words: define; three named evidence units; one comparison or source limit; ranked causal judgement. 15 marks/250 words: chronology/source → map/polity → agrarian-urban mechanism → institutions and social groups → comparison → verdict; monarchy versus gaṇa compares authority, membership, mobilisation, succession and limits. 20 marks/300 words: geography → resources → capitals → economy/revenue → political agency → administration/army → rival sequence → counterpoints → contingent synthesis. Causal paragraph: factor → named evidence → mechanism → rival comparison → limit. (✅ owner 11 §20)
**Breakdown:** Three word limits and two paragraph formulae (✅ owner 11 §20).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Word limits as stated (✅ owner 11 §20).
**Links:** ANC-2078, ANC-2075, ANC-2096
**Trap:** A "discuss" answer reduced to a single cause loses marks. (✅ owner 11 §19)
**Mains hook:** "Critically examine Magadha's rise beyond the iron-ore thesis." Qualify with contingency.
**Back:** *"Factor, evidence, mechanism, rival, limit: the Magadha paragraph."*

### Flashcard ANC-2099 — Regional alternatives to Magadha: Kosala, Vatsa, Avanti, Vajji, Gandhāra, Aśmaka
**Front:** What regional advantages did rival states possess, and what does the comparison teach?
**Definition (learn):** Kosala: fertile Sarayū basin, Śrāvastī, prior control of Kāśi. Vatsa: Kauśāmbī and Yamunā–Gaṅgā route advantages. Avanti: Malwa, Ujjain–Māhiṣmatī routes, iron-bearing hinterlands. Vajji: confederated north-Gaṅgā coalition. Gandhāra and Aśmaka: distinct north-western and trans-Vindhyan trajectories. (✅ owner 11 §17.1)
**Breakdown:** Five regional alternatives (✅ owner 11 §17.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated in owner.
**Links:** ANC-2078, ANC-2067, ANC-2070, ANC-2071, ANC-2068, ANC-2073, ANC-2074
**Trap:** "Magadha alone had fertile rivers, iron or towns" is wrong. (✅ owner 11 §19)
**Mains hook:** Use alternatives to test whether Magadha's rise was inevitable.
**Back:** *"Kosala's Sarayu, Vatsa's Yamuna, Avanti's iron, Vajji's coalition: Magadha was not alone."*

### GROUP — Iranian and Macedonian Invasions (basic owner 12 + advanced owner 12)

### Flashcard ANC-2100 — Concept: the north-west as a connected frontier (limited conquest, durable contact)
**Front:** What is the core idea of the Achaemenid and Macedonian contacts with the north-west, and what is the verdict formula?
**Definition (learn):** The north-west was exposed because it was connected, not because it was an empty gateway. Achaemenid incorporation placed Gandhāra and parts of the Indus frontier in an imperial zone for a longer period; Alexander's campaign was a rapid military passage that crossed the north-western rim and stopped at the Beas. The owner's verdict formula: "limited conquest, durable contact." (✅ owner 12 basic §1, §10; ✅ owner 12 advanced §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Achaemenid: longer imperial frontier, tributary and satrapal, local elites (✅ owner 12 §21)
- Macedonian: rapid conquest campaign, negotiated arrangements, garrisons (✅ owner 12 §21)
- Legacy: Aramaic/Kharoṣṭhī context; Greek settlements; route knowledge (✅ owner 12 §20)
- Neither mapped as uniform rule over India; neither directly explains Mauryan unification (✅ owner 12 §21)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Achaemenid contact from c. 6th century BCE; Darius c. 518/516 BCE convention; Alexander 327–325 BCE (✅ owner 12 mini-timeline).
**Links:** ANC-2101, ANC-2110, ANC-2118, ANC-2121, ANC-2125
**Trap:** "These were India's first foreign invasions" erases earlier mobility and contact (✅ owner 12 §22).
**Mains hook:** "Discuss: Achaemenid incorporation and Alexander's campaign were geographically bounded but placed the north-west within wider imperial, military and communication networks." Qualify with local agency.
**Back:** *"Limited conquest, durable contact: the north-west was connected, not empty."*

### Flashcard ANC-2101 — Achaemenid expansion chronology: Cyrus, Cambyses and Darius I
**Front:** What is the graded evidence for Achaemenid control of Gandhāra and Hindush, from Cyrus to Darius I?
**Definition (learn):** Cyrus: Gandhāra may have entered the Achaemenid sphere under Cyrus, but direct evidence for a securely mapped Indian conquest is insufficient. Cambyses: no equally secure subcontinental conquest sequence. Darius I: the Behistun inscription places Gandhāra within Darius's imperial geography; later lists (Naqsh-i Rustam tradition) include Hindush. The c. 518/516 BCE date is a textbook convention. (✅ owner 12 §12.1, §12.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Cyrus: possible antecedent (✅ owner 12 §12.1)
- Cambyses: no secure sequence (✅ owner 12 §12.1)
- Darius I: Behistun (Gandhāra); Naqsh-i Rustam (Hindush) (✅ owner 12 §12.2)
- c. 518/516 BCE: useful textbook date, not an exact conquest chronology (✅ owner 12 §12.2)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Basic and advanced differ in tone. Basic: 516 BCE is "a conventional reconstruction, not a self-verifying exact annexation date". Advanced mini-timeline: "516 BC Darius annexes Punjab and Sindh (RS chronology)". **Owner conflict:** the advanced file states an annexation of Punjab and Sindh, which the basic file does not support; the basic version is the safer answer. ⚠️ flagged.
**Links:** ANC-2100, ANC-2102, ANC-2103
**Trap:** Do not state that Darius conquered all Punjab and Sindh "precisely in 516 BCE" (✅ owner 12 §22). Cyrus is a "possible antecedent", not settled annexation.
**Mains hook:** Use graded evidence (attested royal claim, probable control, campaign passage, tributary arrangement) to avoid maximalist claims.
**Back:** *"Cyrus maybe, Cambyses unclear, Darius claims Gandhara: 516 is a convention."*

### Flashcard ANC-2102 — Gandhāra and Hindush: royal geography, not "all India"
**Front:** What do the terms Gandhāra and Hindush denote in Achaemenid royal sources, and what must not be assumed?
**Definition (learn):** Gandhāra appears in Darius's Behistun geography and in Xerxes' royal lists; Hindush appears among subject lands in later Darius lists. "Hindush" must not be expanded automatically to all India. "India" in royal and classical sources is a shifting geographical category, not the modern Republic. (✅ owner 12 §9, §12.2, §12.3)
**Breakdown:**
- Gandhāra: Behistun; Xerxes' royal lists (✅ owner 12 §12.2, §12.3)
- Hindush: later Darius lists; Naqsh-i Rustam tradition (✅ owner 12 §12.2)
- "India": shifting category (✅ owner 12 §9)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gandhāra and parts of the Indus/lower-Indus world within Achaemenid claims and varying control (✅ owner 12 §13.1).
**Links:** ANC-2101, ANC-2106, ANC-2127
**Trap:** Royal list is not proof of uniform direct rule. Do not draw a maximalist Achaemenid border across all Punjab, Sindh and the Gangā plain (✅ owner 12 §13.1).
**Mains hook:** Use Gandhāra and Hindush to show the claim–control gap in imperial sources.
**Back:** *"Gandhara in Behistun, Hindush in later lists: claims, not a map of India."*

### Flashcard ANC-2103 — Scylax voyage and Darius's interest in the Indus route
**Front:** What does the Scylax voyage in Herodotus show, and what is its limit?
**Definition (learn):** Herodotus reports that Darius commissioned exploration from the Indus toward the sea (the Scylax voyage). It supports Achaemenid interest in route and fiscal knowledge, but it is a literary report, not a surviving expedition log. (✅ owner 12 §12.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Darius commissioned exploration from the Indus (✅ owner 12 §12.2)
- Interest in route and fiscal knowledge (✅ owner 12 §12.2)
- Literary report; no log survives (✅ owner 12 §12.2)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Darius era (✅ owner 12 §12.2).
**Links:** ANC-2101, ANC-2115, ANC-2127
**Trap:** Do not treat Scylax as proof of a completed Achaemenid conquest of the Indus delta. (✅ owner 12 §12.2)
**Mains hook:** Use the route evidence to argue that knowledge preceded conquest.
**Back:** *"Scylax sailed for Darius: a report of route interest, not a log."*

### Flashcard ANC-2104 — Xerxes and the 480 BCE contingents
**Front:** What does Herodotus say about Indian and Gandhāran contingents under Xerxes, and what does it prove?
**Definition (learn):** Herodotus describes Indian and Gandhāran contingents in Xerxes' campaign against Greece in 480 BCE, showing military incorporation or obligation. Such participation does not prove identical administration in every north-western locality. Service may have involved contingents supplied through local elites, not a uniform standing army. (✅ owner 12 §12.3, §13.3)
**Breakdown:**
- 480 BCE: Indian and Gandhāran troops in Xerxes' army (✅ owner 12 §12.3)
- Clothing and weapons are ethnographic representations (✅ owner 12 §13.3)
- Local-elite supplied contingents (✅ owner 12 §13.3)
- Achaemenid authority in the east weakened before Alexander (✅ owner 12 §12.3)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 480 BCE (✅ owner 12 §12.3).
**Links:** ANC-2101, ANC-2105, ANC-2106
**Trap:** Clothing and weapon descriptions are not full equipment inventories. Military participation is not identical administration. (✅ owner 12 §13.3)
**Mains hook:** Use Xerxes' contingents to show military incorporation with local mediation.
**Back:** *"Xerxes' Indians at 480 BCE: obligation, not a uniform army."*

### Flashcard ANC-2105 — Herodotean tribute: "twentieth district" and 360 talents of gold dust
**Front:** What does Herodotus report about the Indian tribute, and why must it not be treated as a modern statistic?
**Definition (learn):** Herodotus places "Indians" in the twentieth tribute district and reports 360 talents of gold dust, described as an enormous share of Asian tribute. It shows that Greek tradition perceived the Indian frontier as rich and fiscally important. (✅ owner 12 §13.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Twentieth tribute district (✅ owner 12 §13.2)
- 360 talents of gold dust (✅ owner 12 §13.2)
- Greek perception of wealth (✅ owner 12 §13.2)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** The figure is taken from the owner's citation of Herodotus; not independently re-checked in this build. ⚠️ (✅ owner 12 §13.2)
**Links:** ANC-2104, ANC-2106
**Trap:** Do not treat 360 talents as an audited revenue; do not equate the district with a royal-inscription satrapy; do not treat gold-dust stories as proof of one extraction mechanism (✅ owner 12 §13.2).
**Mains hook:** Use the figure to illustrate outsider perception, with source criticism.
**Back:** *"Twentieth district, 360 talents: a Greek estimate of richness, not a receipt."*

### Flashcard ANC-2106 — Achaemenid administration: royal claim, tribute, local elites and limits
**Front:** What mechanisms likely governed the Achaemenid north-west, and what is the limit of "uniform satrapal administration"?
**Definition (learn):** Likely mechanisms: satrapal or provincial supervision within the wider eastern empire; tribute and military levies; communication routes and intelligence; Imperial Aramaic administrative practice; reliance on local rulers, elites and communities. "Uniform satrapal administration" is an overclaim; distinguish royal claim, tribute, local elite mediation and uncertain direct reach. (✅ owner 12 §13.1, §22)
**Breakdown:**
- Satrapal supervision (✅ owner 12 §13.1)
- Tribute and levies (✅ owner 12 §13.1)
- Routes and intelligence (✅ owner 12 §13.1)
- Aramaic practice (✅ owner 12 §13.1)
- Local elites (✅ owner 12 §13.1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Eastern limits in Punjab debated (✅ owner 12 §13.1).
**Links:** ANC-2102, ANC-2104, ANC-2105, ANC-2107
**Trap:** A royal list is not proof of uniform direct rule. (✅ owner 12 §13.1)
**Mains hook:** Use the mechanisms list to build a control paragraph with a graded limit.
**Back:** *"Satrap, tribute, route, Aramaic, local elite: the machinery, not a border."*

### Flashcard ANC-2107 — Kharoṣṭhī: right-to-left script linked to Aramaic and the north-west
**Front:** What is Kharoṣṭhī, how is it written, and how is it linked to Achaemenid contact?
**Definition (learn):** Kharoṣṭhī was written right-to-left and remained in use in the north-west into the early centuries AD. Imperial Aramaic under the Achaemenids provides the strongest context for its development; the exact transmission process and chronology are not directly documented. (✅ owner 12 §1, §14; ⚠️ transmission debated per owner)
**Breakdown:**
- Direction: right-to-left (✅ owner 12 basic §1, §4)
- Region: north-west (✅ owner 12)
- Duration: into the early centuries AD (✅ owner 12 §1)
- Link: structurally linked to Aramaic; broadly accepted model; mechanism debated (✅ owner 12 §8)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Early centuries AD (✅ owner 12 §1).
**Links:** ANC-2106, ANC-2108, ANC-2100
**Trap:** "Kharoṣṭhī is a south-Indian script" is wrong; it is north-western and Aramaic-linked. "Persians invented Kharoṣṭhī for India" is also wrong (✅ owner 12 §5, §22).
**Mains hook:** "Use Kharoṣṭhī to show how scripts can preserve evidence of cultural contact better than political control." (✅ owner 12 §7)
**Back:** *"Kharoshthi runs right-to-left: Aramaic gave the grammar, the north-west kept the script."*

### Flashcard ANC-2108 — Iranian influence on Mauryan forms: dipi/lipi, capitals and "selective adaptation"
**Front:** Which Achaemenid parallels are claimed for Mauryan forms, and what is the correct caution?
**Definition (learn):** R.S. Sharma notes Achaemenid parallels in Mauryan capital forms (bell-shaped capitals in the owner's evidence table) and epigraphic vocabulary such as dipi/lipi. These are later comparative similarities and selective adaptations within an Indian imperial idiom; influence is not copying. (✅ owner 12 basic §1; ✅ owner 12 §8, §14; ✅ owner 12 advanced §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Capitals: bell-shaped capitals (✅ owner 12 §8 table)
- Vocabulary: dipi/lipi (✅ owner 12 §1)
- Caution: influence ≠ copying; Mauryan art has indigenous elements (✅ owner 12 §8)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Later similarities, not contemporary proof of institutional continuity (✅ owner 12 §1).
**Links:** ANC-2107, ANC-2109, owner 14 (Mauryan capitals and Ashokan edicts)
**Trap:** "Mauryan art and administration were copied from Persia" is wrong (✅ owner 12 §22). "Mauryan administration cannot be derived line by line from Achaemenid institutions" (✅ owner 12 §14).
**Mains hook:** Use the parallels to illustrate transmission through contact, qualified by indigenous development.
**Back:** *"Dipi, lipi, bell capital: parallels, not photocopies."*

### Flashcard ANC-2109 — Coinage: Achaemenid sigloi and the origin of punch-marked coins
**Front:** What do Achaemenid coin traditions show, and why is Indian punch-marked coinage not simply Persian?
**Definition (learn):** Achaemenid sigloi, bent-bar and related coin traditions circulated or influenced north-western monetary environments. Indian punch-marked coinage did not simply originate from Persian coins; coin origins were plural. (✅ owner 12 §14, §22)
**Breakdown:**
- Achaemenid sigloi, bent-bar (✅ owner 12 §14)
- Influence on north-west monetary environment (✅ owner 12 §14)
- Punch-marked coinage not simply Persian (✅ owner 12 §14)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated in owner.
**Links:** ANC-2091 (punch-marked coins), ANC-2108
**Trap:** "Persians invented punch-marked coins for India" is wrong (✅ owner 12 §22).
**Mains hook:** Use coinage to show that influence was plural.
**Back:** *"Siglos in the north-west, punch marks in India: influence, not origin."*

### Flashcard ANC-2110 — Alexander's route: Hindu Kush, Kabul–Swat, Aornos and the crossing of the Indus
**Front:** What was Alexander's route in the north-west, and what are the key stages?
**Definition (learn):** 334–330 BCE: conquest of the Achaemenid core (background, not Indian). 327 BCE: crossed the Hindu Kush and divided forces. 327–326 BCE: campaigns in Kabul/Swat highlands and siege of Aornos (conventionally linked with Pir-Sar, identification debated). 326 BCE: crossed the Indus; received by Ambhi/Taxiles at Taxila. (✅ owner 12 §15)
**Breakdown:**
- Hindu Kush crossing 327 BCE (✅ owner 12 §15)
- Kabul–Swat highlands; Aornos (✅ owner 12 §15, §16)
- Indus crossing and Taxila (✅ owner 12 §15)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Alexander in India about nineteen months (326–325 BCE) (✅ owner 12 basic §4).
**Links:** ANC-2100, ANC-2111, ANC-2112, ANC-2116, ANC-2127
**Trap:** "Khyber route" is shorthand and should not replace the staged geography (✅ owner 12 §1). Alexander campaigned only on the north-western rim (✅ owner 12 §5).
**Mains hook:** "Use the route to show that the north-west is a corridor of stages, not a single gateway."
**Back:** *"Hindu Kush 327, Kabul-Swat and Aornos, Indus and Taxila 326: staged, not a single pass."*

### Flashcard ANC-2111 — Ambhi of Taxila (Taxiles/Omphis): alliance as strategy
**Front:** Who was Ambhi, and why is his submission not "Indian surrender"?
**Definition (learn):** Ambhi of Taxila (Greek Taxiles/Omphis) allied with Alexander against regional rivals and provided supplies and troops. This was diplomacy and strategic agency, not passive surrender by a unified India. (✅ owner 12 basic §1; ✅ owner 12 §16)
**Breakdown:**
- Taxila: route junction and political centre (✅ owner 12 §10)
- Alliance against regional rivals (✅ owner 12 §16)
- Supplies and troops (✅ owner 12 §16)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 326 BCE (✅ owner 12 §15).
**Links:** ANC-2110, ANC-2112, ANC-2127
**Trap:** "India submitted to Alexander" is wrong; there was no unified modern-national resistance nor unified submission. (✅ owner 12 §10)
**Mains hook:** Use Ambhi to show indigenous agency in the north-west.
**Back:** *"Ambhi chose the Greeks to beat rivals: agency, not surrender."*

### Flashcard ANC-2112 — Battle of the Hydaspes (Jhelum) and Porus: contested outcome
**Front:** What do the Greek sources claim about the battle against Porus, and what is the settlement?
**Definition (learn):** The battle against Porus on the Hydaspes (Jhelum), 326 BCE. Greek sources narrate a difficult river crossing, monsoon conditions, cavalry manoeuvre and elephants, and claim Macedonian battlefield success. Porus was restored and his territory enlarged under Alexander's political arrangement. The outcome was neither an unequivocal Porus victory nor simple annihilation; it was a costly battle followed by negotiated indirect rule. (✅ owner 12 §16; ✅ owner 12 basic §1, §5)
**Breakdown:**
- Greek narrative: river crossing, monsoon, cavalry, elephants (✅ owner 12 §16)
- Claimed Macedonian success (✅ owner 12 §16)
- Porus restored and enlarged (✅ owner 12 §16)
- Speeches, totals and portraits are literary constructions (✅ owner 12 §16)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** May 326 BCE (✅ Wikipedia "Battle of the Hydaspes"; owner 12 §15 gives 326 BCE). **Source check:** Wikipedia says Porus was "restored, not enlarged as such" and made a satrap, while owner 12 §16 says he was "restored and his territory enlarged"; flagged as an owner-vs-source conflict (⚠️). Casualty figures are disputed: Arrian gives about 23,000 Indian dead and captured (20,000 infantry, 3,000 cavalry, with prisoners possibly counted in the total); Diodorus gives 12,000 killed and over 9,000 captured (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** ANC-2111, ANC-2113, ANC-2118, ANC-2119
**Trap:** "Porus decisively defeated Alexander" and "Porus was simply annihilated" are both wrong. Use "restored as satrap" (Wikipedia wording) rather than "enlarged" unless the owner's wording is qualified. (✅ owner 12 §5, §22; ✅ Wikipedia)
**Mains hook:** Use the Porus settlement to show that battlefield claims and political results can diverge.
**Back:** *"Hydaspes: Greeks claim the field, Porus keeps and grows his kingdom."*

### Flashcard ANC-2113 — The halt at the Hyphasis (Beas): a multicausal crisis
**Front:** Why did the army refuse to advance beyond the Hyphasis (Beas)?
**Definition (learn):** The halt was a logistical, environmental, military and morale crisis: long campaigning (exhaustion accumulated from the Balkans through the Achaemenid world); monsoon, flooded rivers, humidity, disease; Indian resistance (highland, Porus, Malloi); distance and supply; reports of eastern powers (Nanda and Gangetic armies and elephants, rhetorical); troop morale and command conflict. The halt was not proof that one Nanda army figure frightened the force. (✅ owner 12 §15, §17)
**Breakdown:**
- Exhaustion (✅ owner 12 §17)
- Monsoon and river logistics (✅ owner 12 §17)
- Indian resistance (✅ owner 12 §17)
- Distance and supply (✅ owner 12 §17)
- Eastern reports: rhetorical (✅ owner 12 §17)
- Morale and command conflict (✅ owner 12 §17)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 326 BCE (✅ owner 12 §15).
**Links:** ANC-2078 (Nanda), ANC-2084, ANC-2112, ANC-2119
**Trap:** "The army stopped only because it feared Nanda numbers" is wrong (✅ owner 12 §22). Do not treat Greek army figures as intelligence audits (✅ owner 12 §17).
**Mains hook:** "Explain why Alexander's army refused to go further, combining environment, logistics and resistance." Qualify each factor.
**Back:** *"Tired, wet, supplied badly, resisted, far from home, and unwilling: a halt, not a fear story."*

### Flashcard ANC-2114 — The Malloi and the Indus descent: regional resistance and Alexander's wound
**Front:** What happened during the Indus descent (326–325 BCE), and why does it refute a smooth conquest?
**Definition (learn):** The Malloi and neighbouring groups resisted severely during the Indus descent; Alexander was seriously wounded in an assault. This episode refutes a smooth, completed conquest narrative and demonstrates continued regional resistance after Hydaspes. (✅ owner 12 §15, §16)
**Breakdown:**
- Resistance: Malloi and neighbours (✅ owner 12 §16)
- Alexander seriously wounded (✅ owner 12 §16)
- Continued regional resistance after Hydaspes (✅ owner 12 §15)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 326–325 BCE (✅ owner 12 §15).
**Links:** ANC-2113, ANC-2115, ANC-2110
**Trap:** Do not present the Indian north-west as an empty corridor (✅ owner 12 §10).
**Mains hook:** Use the Malloi episode to show indigenous agency and costs of conquest.
**Back:** *"Malloi bite back: Alexander wounded on the Indus descent."*

### Flashcard ANC-2115 — Patala, Nearchus's voyage and the Gedrosian return (325 BCE)
**Front:** What were the routes and arrangements at Patala and the return through Gedrosia?
**Definition (learn):** In 325 BCE Alexander made Patala/delta arrangements, Nearchus undertook a maritime voyage, and Alexander returned through Gedrosia (Makran), a return route associated with severe losses. Routes improved geographic knowledge but did not "open" a previously closed world. (✅ owner 12 §15, §10)
**Breakdown:**
- Patala/delta: fleet and maritime reconnaissance (✅ owner 12 §10)
- Nearchus's maritime voyage (✅ owner 12 §15)
- Gedrosia/Makran return; losses filtered through narratives (✅ owner 12 §10)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 325 BCE (✅ owner 12 §15).
**Links:** ANC-2110, ANC-2114, ANC-2118, ANC-2119
**Trap:** "Routes opened a closed world" is an overclaim. Classical place identifications require caution (✅ owner 12 §10, §15).
**Mains hook:** Use the return route to show route knowledge and cost.
**Back:** *"Patala and Nearchus 325, Gedrosia's cost: knowledge gained, world not opened."*

### Flashcard ANC-2116 — Alexander's time in India and the 327 Hindu Kush crossing
**Front:** How long did Alexander remain in India, and what dates frame the Indian campaign?
**Definition (learn):** Alexander remained in India for about nineteen months (326–325 BCE). He crossed the Hindu Kush in 327 BCE; he conquered the Achaemenid western and central core 334–330 BCE (background; Darius III defeated). The campaign was short and its effects were north-western. (✅ owner 12 basic §4, §15; ✅ owner 12 advanced §4)
**Breakdown:**
- 334–330 BCE: Achaemenid core conquest (background) (✅ owner 12 §15)
- 327 BCE: Hindu Kush crossing (✅ owner 12 §15)
- 326–325 BCE: Indian campaign (~19 months) (✅ owner 12 basic §4)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Advanced owner dates the campaign 327–326 BCE; basic owner dates the stay 326–325 BCE. Both are consistent with the phased route. (✅ owner 12)
**Links:** ANC-2110, ANC-2100
**Trap:** "Alexander conquered the whole of India" is wrong (✅ owner 12 §5).
**Mains hook:** "Assess Alexander's Indian campaign as a short military episode whose strategic consequences far outweighed its territorial impact."
**Back:** *"Nineteen months, Hindu Kush in 327, core conquered before India."*

### Flashcard ANC-2117 — After Alexander: Philip, Peithon, Eudemus, the 323 BCE succession wars and Seleucus
**Front:** How did the Macedonian arrangements in India fragment after Alexander's death in 323 BCE?
**Definition (learn):** Alexander retained or enlarged local rulers (Ambhi, Porus) while appointing Macedonian/Greek officers, satraps and garrisons; named officers in frontier contexts include Philip, Peithon and Eudemus. After Alexander's death in 323 BCE, succession wars divided imperial authority; officers were killed, withdrew or competed; local rulers reasserted agency; garrisons did not amount to a stable pan-regional state. Seleucus I's eastern claims and settlement with Chandragupta mark the transition only; they belong to the Mauryan owner. (✅ owner 12 §19, §9)
**Breakdown:**
- Local rulers kept (Ambhi, Porus) (✅ owner 12 §19)
- Officers: Philip, Peithon, Eudemus (✅ owner 12 §19)
- After 323 BCE: succession wars; officers killed or competing (✅ owner 12 §19)
- Local rulers reasserted agency (✅ owner 12 §19)
- Seleucus: transition marker only (✅ owner 12 §9, §19)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 323 BCE (✅ owner 12 §19). The Seleucid principality in the north-west is recorded in advanced owner §1 (✅ owner 12 advanced §1).
**Links:** ANC-2100, ANC-2121, ANC-2124, owner 14 (Seleucus–Chandragupta)
**Trap:** "Greek rule in India lasted for centuries after Alexander" is wrong; his Indian possessions were soon lost to the Mauryas (✅ owner 12 basic §5).
**Mains hook:** Use fragmentation to show the north-west was a favourable context, not a direct cause.
**Back:** *"323 BCE: successors fight, officers fade, Porus and Ambhi remain Indian."*

### Flashcard ANC-2118 — Macedonian vs Achaemenid: a comparison pair
**Front:** How do Achaemenid and Macedonian contacts differ on form, evidence, mechanism, reach, duration and legacy?
**Definition (learn):** Achaemenid: longer imperial incorporation or tributary frontier; evidence from royal inscriptions, reliefs, Herodotus, coins and Aramaic context; satrapal/fiscal/military networks using local elites; Gandhāra and parts of the Indus frontier with debated limits; multi-generational but unevenly maintained; legacy in the imperial network and Aramaic/Kharoṣṭhī context. Macedonian: rapid conquest and negotiated arrangements; later classical narratives, route and settlements; mobile army, alliances, battles, satraps and garrisons; north-western rim and Indus/Punjab corridors, not the Gangā plain; very short presence followed by fragmentation; legacy in chronology, settlements and Hellenistic contact. (✅ owner 12 §21)
**Breakdown:** Seven axes (✅ owner 12 §21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Common limit: neither can be mapped as uniform rule over India (✅ owner 12 §21).
**Links:** ANC-2100, ANC-2112, ANC-2117
**Trap:** "Achaemenid and Macedonian impacts were identical" is wrong; one was frontier administration, the other a fast military campaign (✅ owner 12 advanced §5). "Darius and Alexander belong to the same invasion" is wrong (✅ owner 12 basic §5).
**Mains hook:** "Achaemenid influence was more administrative and durable in the north-west, whereas the Macedonian impact was briefer but symbolically larger." Qualify both.
**Back:** *"Achaemenid: long, administrative, Aramaic. Macedonian: brief, military, chronicled."*

### Flashcard ANC-2119 — Source ladder for Alexander: Arrian, Curtius, Diodorus, Plutarch, Justin and lost participants
**Front:** Which surviving authors narrate Alexander's Indian campaign, when were they writing, and what are the lost sources behind them?
**Definition (learn):** Diodorus (1st century BCE); Curtius Rufus (probably 1st century CE); Plutarch (1st–2nd centuries CE); Arrian (2nd century CE); Justin's later epitome of Pompeius Trogus. They drew on lost works by participants or near-contemporaries: Ptolemy, Aristobulus, Nearchus, Onesicritus and Cleitarchus traditions. (✅ owner 12 §11, §18)
**Breakdown:**
- Source controls: compare divergent versions; identify heroic, moral and apologetic agendas; treat speeches, motives, marvels, casualties, totals as high-risk; test with route and archaeology; silence not proof (✅ owner 12 §18)
- Herodotus (for Achaemenid context): Greek-audience writer (✅ owner 12 §11)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As stated; late and genre-shaped (✅ owner 12 §18).
**Links:** ANC-2112, ANC-2113, ANC-2120, ANC-2122
**Trap:** "Greek accounts are neutral eyewitness records" is wrong (✅ owner 12 §22). Participant works are lost.
**Mains hook:** A source-critical line for "critically examine" questions.
**Back:** *"Diodorus, Curtius, Plutarch, Arrian, Justin: late writers, lost eyewitnesses behind them."*

### Flashcard ANC-2120 — Indian silence in the sources
**Front:** Why is the absence of an Indian campaign narrative not proof of insignificance?
**Definition (learn):** Indian textual silence is an absence of a matching indigenous campaign narrative. Silence is not proof of no resistance, no impact or no memory. The owner's rule: do not infer that Indian silence proves the event was insignificant or that Greek claims are uncontested. (✅ owner 12 §11, §18)
**Breakdown:**
- Silence in Indian sources (✅ owner 12 §11)
- Greek claims are uncontested? No (✅ owner 12 §18)
- Test: route, topography, archaeology (✅ owner 12 §18)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** ANC-2119, ANC-2113
**Trap:** Silence cuts both ways. (✅ owner 12 §18)
**Mains hook:** Use the silence to frame source limits in a 20-mark answer.
**Back:** *"Indian silence is not absence: the north-west had memory without an epic."*

### Flashcard ANC-2121 — Consequences: defensible versus overclaimed
**Front:** What consequences of the north-west episode are defensible, and which are overclaims?
**Definition (learn):** Defensible: temporary rearrangement of north-western rulers, satrapies, garrisons and settlements; fragmentation as one favourable condition for Magadhan–Mauryan expansion; campaigns surveyed and documented routes; Greek accounts give a dated external narrative; a strong 326–325 BCE anchor; Boukephala, Nikaia and Alexandrias as Hellenistic claims; Hellenistic and Indo-Greek interaction affected regional art and coinage. Overclaims: Alexander conquered or unified India; Alexander caused the Mauryan Empire; trade began only in 326 BCE; all ethnography is neutral; place names prove centuries of Macedonian rule; Alexander personally introduced Gandhāra art or all Indian coinage. (✅ owner 12 §20)
**Breakdown:** Seven dimensions with a defensible column and an overclaim column (✅ owner 12 §20).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** The durable outcome was intensified contact and documentation, not civilisational replacement (✅ owner 12 §20).
**Links:** ANC-2100, ANC-2117, ANC-2122, ANC-2124
**Trap:** "Alexander caused the Mauryan Empire" is a standard false cause. (✅ owner 12 §20)
**Mains hook:** Use the defensible/overclaim split to write a balanced verdict.
**Back:** *"Rearranged, documented, contacted: never conquered, never the cause of the Mauryas."*

### Flashcard ANC-2122 — Settlements: Boukephala, Nikaia and the Alexandrias
**Front:** What do Boukephala, Nikaia and the Alexandrias represent, and what do they not prove?
**Definition (learn):** Boukephala, Nikaia and several Alexandrias are Greek settlements or city claims in the north-west. They represent Hellenistic military and urban claims. They do not prove centuries of uninterrupted Macedonian rule or pan-Indian control. (✅ owner 12 advanced §1, §4, §20)
**Breakdown:**
- Boukephala (✅ owner 12 advanced §1)
- Nikaia (✅ owner 12 advanced §1)
- Several Alexandrias (✅ owner 12 advanced §1)
- Greek settlement survives in place-names and memory longer than in empire (✅ owner 12 advanced mini-timeline)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Post-326 BCE (✅ owner 12 advanced mini-timeline).
**Links:** ANC-2119, ANC-2121, ANC-2118
**Trap:** "Boukephala and Nikaia prove permanent Greek rule" is wrong; they show settlement and contact (✅ owner 12 advanced §5).
**Mains hook:** Use the settlements to show that contact outlived conquest.
**Back:** *"Boukephala, Nikaia, Alexandrias: settlements and memory, not empire."*

### Flashcard ANC-2123 — Chandragupta and Alexander: a retrospective tradition
**Front:** What is the status of later Greek traditions linking Chandragupta with Alexander?
**Definition (learn):** Later Greek traditions (Plutarch, Justin) link Chandragupta with Alexander's age, knowledge of his methods and the political vacuum created by the destruction of petty north-western states. These are external and late; Indian sources do not confirm direct contact. Full Mauryan consolidation belongs to Topic 14. (✅ owner 12 §8, §9; ✅ owner 12 basic §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Tradition: Chandragupta knew Alexander's methods (✅ owner 12 §8)
- Vacuum: destruction of petty north-western states (✅ owner 12 §8)
- External and late; Indian confirmation absent (✅ owner 12 §8)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated beyond "later".
**Links:** ANC-2117, ANC-2121, owner 14 (Mauryan Empire)
**Trap:** "Alexander caused the Mauryan Empire" (✅ owner 12 §20). "Alexander paved the way for the Mauryas" is only a tradition-supported inference (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Use the tradition as a bounded inference, not a cause.
**Back:** *"Plutarch and Justin link Chandragupta to Alexander: a late tale, not a cause."*

### Flashcard ANC-2124 — Indo-Greek and Hellenistic legacies: coinage and art belong to later histories
**Front:** Why must Gandhāra art and Indo-Greek coins not be credited to Alexander's 326 BCE campaign?
**Definition (learn):** Later Hellenistic and Indo-Greek interaction affected regional art and monetary forms; these belong to longer, later Hellenistic regional histories. Alexander did not personally introduce Gandhāra art or all Indian coinage. Indo-Greek and Kushan contact is studied in the Central Asian owner. (✅ owner 12 §20, §22; ✅ owner 12 advanced §3 study link)
**Breakdown:**
- Hellenistic/Indo-Greek influence on art and coinage (✅ owner 12 §20)
- Overclaim: Alexander introduced Gandhāra art (✅ owner 12 §20)
- Central Asian owner 16 for Indo-Greek and Kushan impact (✅ owner 12 advanced §3)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Later periods.
**Links:** ANC-2109, ANC-2121, ANC-2117
**Trap:** "Gandhāra art and Indo-Greek coins were direct products of 326 BCE" is wrong (✅ owner 12 §22).
**Mains hook:** Use to separate event from long-term process.
**Back:** *"Gandhara art and Indo-Greek coins are later histories, not 326 BCE products."*

### Flashcard ANC-2125 — Hostile-search trap register: Achaemenid and Macedonian invasions
**Front:** Which unsafe claims must be corrected in an answer on the invasions?
**Definition (learn):** The owner's register includes: "first foreign invasions"; Darius conquered Punjab and Sindh precisely in 516 BCE; Herodotus gives exact tribute and army statistics; Achaemenid control was uniform satrapal administration; Persians invented Kharoṣṭhī and punch-marked coins; Mauryan art and administration copied from Persia; Alexander conquered India; Porus clearly won or lost; the army stopped only because of Nanda numbers; Greek accounts are neutral; Alexander opened trade and caused Mauryan unification; Gandhāra art and Indo-Greek coins were 326 BCE products. (✅ owner 12 §22)
**Breakdown:** Eleven unsafe claims with marks-safe corrections (✅ owner 12 §22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** ANC-2100, ANC-2101, ANC-2105, ANC-2107, ANC-2108, ANC-2112, ANC-2113, ANC-2119, ANC-2121, ANC-2124
**Trap:** Words "first", "all", "precisely", "copied", "alone" are signals.
**Mains hook:** Use corrections to qualify any Achaemenid or Macedonian answer.
**Back:** *"First, all, precisely, copied, alone: five words to test before you write."*

### Flashcard ANC-2126 — Answer architecture: invasions (10, 15, 20 marks)
**Front:** What answer skeletons does the owner give for Achaemenid and Macedonian questions?
**Definition (learn):** 10 marks/150 words: define the north-western scale; three named evidence units; one source or extent limit; conclude "bounded control, durable connection." 15 marks: for influence, Achaemenid evidence → network → Aramaic/Kharoṣṭhī → coin/art/vocabulary parallels → Mauryan limit → judgement; for Alexander, route → encounters → halt causes → arrangements → fragmentation → effects → source caveat. 20 marks: geography → objectives → evidence → control → duration → agency and resistance → consequences → Mauryan boundary → verdict. Paragraph: claim → evidence → inference → counter-evidence/limit. (✅ owner 12 §23)
**Breakdown:** Three word limits and two paragraph routes (✅ owner 12 §23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Word limits as stated.
**Links:** ANC-2100, ANC-2118, ANC-2125
**Trap:** Do not conclude "Alexander conquered India" in any form.
**Mains hook:** "Explain how the north-west became a gateway of contact rather than only an invasion corridor."
**Back:** *"Evidence, network, mechanism, limit: bounded control, durable connection."*

### Flashcard ANC-2127 — North-western geography: corridors, junctions and their limits
**Front:** What are the north-western corridors, and what limit applies to each?
**Definition (learn):** Hindu Kush passes; Kabul and Swat valleys; Indus crossings; Punjab river system; lower Indus and Makran/Gedrosian coast linked highlands, plains, Central Asia, the Iranian plateau and the Arabian Sea. Zones: Gandhāra–Taxila (route junction; borders changed); Kabul–Swat highlands (multiple approaches; "Khyber route" oversimplifies); Indus crossings (communication and tribute; a crossing does not establish basin-wide administration); Punjab rivers (Hydaspes; monsoon and channel conditions); lower Indus/Patala (delta, fleet, reconnaissance; identifications need caution); Gedrosia/Makran (return route, severe losses). (✅ owner 12 §10)
**Breakdown:** Six corridors with limits (✅ owner 12 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** ANC-2100, ANC-2110, ANC-2115, ANC-2102
**Trap:** "The north-west was an empty gateway" is wrong. (✅ owner 12 §10)
**Mains hook:** "Explain how the north-west became a gateway of contact."
**Back:** *"Hindu Kush, Kabul-Swat, Indus, Punjab rivers, Patala, Gedrosia: connected, not empty."*

### Flashcard ANC-2128 — Achaemenid evidence ladder: royal inscriptions, reliefs, Herodotus and archaeology
**Front:** Which evidence classes support Achaemenid claims, and what does each establish or fail to establish?
**Definition (learn):** Royal inscriptions name Gandhāra and/or Hindush as imperial claims (formulaic; not a survey). Reliefs and imperial art show subject peoples and court ideology (procession imagery does not map depth). Herodotus gives satrapy groupings, tribute traditions and Indian participation in Xerxes' army (written for a Greek audience; totals are literary). Archaeology and numismatics show settlement, objects and coin circulation (sparse; rarely fixes a border or ethnicity). (✅ owner 12 §11)
**Breakdown:** Four classes with mandatory cautions (✅ owner 12 §11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** ANC-2101, ANC-2104, ANC-2105, ANC-2119
**Trap:** Formulaic royal lists are not district surveys. Herodotus's totals are not audited accounts. (✅ owner 12 §11)
**Mains hook:** Use the ladder to show "attested claim vs probable control".
**Back:** *"Inscription claims, relief shows, Herodotus tells, archaeology hints."*

### Flashcard ANC-2129 — Hyphasis halt versus Mauryan threshold: where Topic 12 stops
**Front:** Where does the invasions owner stop, and what does it hand to the Mauryan owner?
**Definition (learn):** The owner stops after Alexander's arrangements and their rapid fragmentation. Seleucus appears only as a transition marker. Chandragupta's expansion, the Seleucid–Mauryan settlement and Mauryan institutions belong to Topic 14 (the Mauryan Empire). (✅ owner 12 §9)
**Breakdown:**
- Owned here: Achaemenid incorporation; Alexander's campaign; fragmentation (✅ owner 12 §9)
- Handed over: Chandragupta; Seleucid–Mauryan settlement; institutions (✅ owner 12 §9)
- Mauryan threshold: Nanda overthrow c. 322/321 BCE (✅ owner 11 §10, cross-owner)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 323 BCE (Alexander's death).
**Links:** ANC-2117, ANC-2123, ANC-2094
**Trap:** Do not use Topic 12 to explain Mauryan institutions. (✅ owner 12 §9)
**Mains hook:** Bridge: the north-west fragmented; Magadha consolidated; the Mauryas followed.
**Back:** *"Topic 12 ends at 323: the Mauryas begin where the successors stop."*

### Flashcard ANC-2130 — PYQ coverage note: zero direct PYQs for the invasions
**Front:** Which PYQs are routed to the invasions owner, and which adjacent items are used instead?
**Definition (learn):** The repository routing ledgers contain zero direct Prelims or Mains PYQs for the invasions owner through 2026. Bounded adjacent practice: 2025 Prelims Q17 (Aśmaka/Kamboja/Avanti/Kosala geography, owned by Topic 11) and 2023 Mains GS-I Q1 (the role of geography, owned by the geographic framework). They do not become direct invasion PYQs. (✅ owner 12 §24)
**Breakdown:**
- Direct PYQs: none (✅ owner 12 §24)
- Adjacent: 2025 Q17; 2023 GS-I Q1 (✅ owner 12 §24)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Ledgers checked across 2018–2023, 2024–2025 and 2026 (✅ owner 12 §24).
**Links:** ANC-2095, ANC-2125, ANC-2126
**Trap:** Do not invent a PYQ route for this owner. (✅ owner 12 §24)
**Mains hook:** Use the adjacent geography to practise frontier-geography answers.
**Back:** *"No direct PYQ for the invasions: study by sequence and impact."*

### GROUP — State and Varna Society in the Age of the Buddha (basic owner 13 + advanced owner 13)

### Flashcard ANC-2131 — Concept: the material base of the second urbanisation and the state–varna relationship
**Front:** What is the core thesis of Buddha-age state and society, and how do state, varna and heterodoxy connect?
**Definition (learn):** The age of the Buddha (6th–5th centuries BCE and its longer early-historic setting) saw iron-based agrarian surplus, towns, coins and taxation make larger states possible, while varna norms hardened and heterodox movements criticised ritual hierarchy. "Cities, kings and renunciants" is Upinder Singh's frame: urbanisation, state formation and new religious movements must be studied together. (✅ owner 13 basic §1; ✅ owner 13 advanced §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Surplus → taxes, tribute, tolls, officials, fortifications, organised force (✅ owner 13 basic §1)
- Varna functions specified in law; state and hierarchy grow together (✅ owner 13 basic §2)
- Heterodox movements arise from within the same urban-agrarian churn (✅ owner 13 advanced §1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 600–321 BCE second urbanisation / Mahājanapadas (✅ owner 13 basic mini-timeline); c. 322/321 BCE Mauryan threshold marker (⚠️ owner 13).
**Links:** ANC-2063, ANC-2132, ANC-2138, ANC-2149, ANC-2155, ANC-2159
**Trap:** Do not present the age as purely pastoral or as a single sudden break in 600 BCE (✅ owner 13 §5, §19).
**Mains hook:** "The age of the Buddha marks a decisive stage of state formation, social stratification and urban growth in the middle Gangetic basin." Qualify with regional limits.
**Back:** *"Surplus builds towns, towns build coins, coins fund armies, and varna hardens as the heterodox respond."*

### Flashcard ANC-2132 — Source corpus for the Buddha age and its evidence matrix
**Front:** Which source classes reconstruct the Buddha-age society, and what caution applies to each?
**Definition (learn):** Early Buddhist Nikāya and Vinaya traditions (kings, clans, cities, householders, monastics, occupations, gifts, law); Jātaka stories and later Buddhist literature (urban trades, credit, guild memory; many layers later); Jain canonical traditions (renunciants, lay communities, rulers, towns; sectarian redaction); Dharmasūtras and Gṛhyasūtras (prescriptive varna, household, marriage, inheritance; norm not observation); Pāṇini and grammatical evidence (categories; date and region control interpretation); archaeology (settlements, NBPW, fortifications, iron, crafts, coins; rarely identifies varna directly); numismatics (issuer, date and social reach uncertain). (✅ owner 13 §9.1)
**Breakdown:** Seven source classes (✅ owner 13 §9.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Method: source fact → historical inference → limitation (✅ owner 13 §9.1). "Buddhist", "Jain", "Brahmanical" are source locations, not homogeneous social blocs (✅ owner 13 §9.1).
**Links:** ANC-2002 (Later Vedic corpus), ANC-2060, ANC-2096, ANC-2131
**Trap:** Do not describe society "neutrally" from one source class. (✅ owner 13 §19)
**Mains hook:** Use the evidence matrix to frame any social answer as norm versus practice.
**Back:** *"Nikaya tells, Jataka recounts, Dharmasutra prescribes, archaeology hints, coins date."*

### Flashcard ANC-2133 — NBPW threshold: "fifth century BCE" versus site-specific dating
**Front:** When does the NBPW phase begin, and why must the date be site-specific?
**Definition (learn):** R.S. Sharma uses a fifth-century BCE NBPW threshold; wider archaeological chronologies may place early NBPW earlier at some sites. The basic owner's must-know entry states "around the 5th century BC" in the Gangetic plains, while its caution says the horizon runs from c. 7th century BCE onward and "varies by site". The owner therefore records a conflict to be resolved by site dating. (✅ owner 13 basic §1, §4, mini-timeline; ✅ owner 11 §13.1 for the 7th-to-2nd/1st-century standard chronology)
**Breakdown:**
- R.S. Sharma threshold: 5th c. BCE (✅ owner 13)
- Wider chronologies: earlier at some sites (⚠️ owner 13)
- Standard chronology: c. 7th to 2nd/1st century BCE (✅ owner 11 §13.1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Site-specific dating mandatory (✅ owner 13 §1).
**Links:** ANC-2088, ANC-2134, ANC-2077
**Trap:** "NBPW belongs to the Harappan period" is wrong; it marks the early historic Gangetic phase (✅ owner 13 §5).
**Mains hook:** Treat NBPW as one marker within a bundle, with dating caution.
**Back:** *"NBPW: fifth century in textbooks, earlier at some sites; always check the site."*

### Flashcard ANC-2134 — Towns of the urbanising zone: Rājagṛha, Pāṭaliputra, Campā, Vaiśālī, Chirand and Śrāvastī
**Front:** Which towns belonged to the urbanising zone of the Buddha age, and what caution applies to "town" labels?
**Definition (learn):** Rājagṛha, Pāṭaliputra, Campā, Vaiśālī and Chirand belonged to this urbanising zone; Śrāvastī is also listed in the evidence table. Urban definition varies; some sites may be large villages rather than true cities. (✅ owner 13 basic §1; ✅ owner 13 §8 evidence units)
**Breakdown:**
- Rājagṛha; Pāṭaliputra; Campā; Vaiśālī; Chirand (✅ owner 13 basic §1)
- Śrāvastī (✅ owner 13 §8)
- Urban identification bundle required (✅ owner 13 §10; ✅ owner 11 §13.1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Middle Ganga basin, eastern UP, Bihar and connected routes are the clearest evidence zone (✅ owner 13 §10).
**Links:** ANC-2076, ANC-2087, ANC-2131
**Trap:** Do not generalise the Ganga-basin urban evidence to the north-west, Deccan, far south or forest zones (✅ owner 13 §10, §18.3).
**Mains hook:** Use the town list to show a regionally uneven urban system.
**Back:** *"Rajgir, Pataliputra, Champa, Vaishali, Chirand, Shravasti: a Ganga-basin network, not all India."*

### Flashcard ANC-2135 — Pāli coin terms: kahāpaṇa, nikkha, kaṃsa, pāda, māsaka, kākaṇikā
**Front:** Which Pāli coin terms are high-yield, and what is their evidentiary status?
**Definition (learn):** Pāli texts give early definite coin terms: kahāpaṇa, nikkha, kaṃsa, pāda, māsaka and kākaṇikā; these are corroborated by archaeological finds of mostly silver punch-marked coins. Meanings and value relationships require textual context. (✅ owner 13 basic §1, §4; ✅ owner 13 §12.3; ✅ owner 13 advanced §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Six Pāli terms (✅ owner 13)
- Archaeological corroboration: silver punch-marked coins (✅ owner 13 basic §1)
- Value relationships require context (✅ owner 13 §12.3)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** The 2026 printed question on Pāli coin evidence is recorded as adjacent, not routed to Topic 13 (✅ owner 13 §21).
**Links:** ANC-2136, ANC-2091, ANC-2130
**Trap:** Do not claim that every Pāli term denotes a fixed-weight coin with known value without textual context. (✅ owner 13 §12.3)
**Mains hook:** Use coin terms as evidence of monetised exchange, qualified by barter.
**Back:** *"Kahapana, nikkha, kamsa, pada, masaka, kakanika: Pali coin words, checked by punch marks."*

### Flashcard ANC-2136 — Punch-marked coins and uneven monetisation
**Front:** What do silver punch-marked coins show, and what do they not show?
**Definition (learn):** Artisans and traders increasingly used coins; silver punch-marked series, hoards and textual coin terms indicate uneven monetisation, not a single universal beginning of money. Rural payments in kind and barter continued. Coinage was not universal and state-minted in one form. (✅ owner 13 basic §1; ✅ owner 13 §12.3; ✅ owner 13 §19)
**Breakdown:**
- Silver punch-marked series (✅ owner 13)
- Hoards and textual terms (✅ owner 13)
- Uneven distribution; rural areas may have remained non-monetary (✅ owner 13 §7)
- Barter and kind continued (✅ owner 13 §12.3)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Earliest coins generally punch-marked and linked to towns/trade (✅ owner 13 basic §4).
**Links:** ANC-2091, ANC-2109, ANC-2135
**Trap:** "Coinage began only under the Mauryas" is wrong; pre-Mauryan coin use is evidenced (✅ owner 13 advanced §5). "Coinage was universal and state-minted" is wrong (✅ owner 13 §19).
**Mains hook:** Use coins to explain trade and taxation, with the barter caveat.
**Back:** *"Punch-marked silver before the Mauryas; universal money, no; barter, still yes."*

### Flashcard ANC-2137 — Iron, agriculture and surplus: the causal chain without determinism
**Front:** How did iron tools and alluvial agriculture contribute to state capacity, and what is the causal limit?
**Definition (learn):** Iron tools in alluvial areas helped agricultural expansion, producing surplus from which rulers could collect taxes. The chain is production → household surplus → market, gift and tax routes → differentiated wealth and state or monastic capacity. It is not "iron → surplus → empire" without labour, property, demand and institutions. (✅ owner 13 basic §1, §4; ✅ owner 13 §12.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Iron tools in alluvial plains (✅ owner 13)
- Rice, rainfall, alluvium, irrigation/water knowledge (✅ owner 13 §12.1)
- Land access, household organisation and extraction determine outcomes (✅ owner 13 §12.1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond the chain.
**Links:** ANC-2005, ANC-2006, ANC-2090
**Trap:** "Iron caused cities, states and surplus" is wrong (✅ owner 13 §19).
**Mains hook:** Use the chain to write a causal paragraph with a named limit.
**Back:** *"Iron clears, surplus feeds, tax funds: not iron alone."*

### Flashcard ANC-2138 — Taxation and standing armies before the Mauryas
**Front:** How did regular taxation relate to armies in the Buddha age, and what is the trap?
**Definition (learn):** Regular taxes and tributes supported armed forces; fiscal capacity made professional armies possible. Fortifications and long-service troops show organised coercive capacity, but exact standing-army size before the Mauryas cannot be recovered reliably. "Standing armies were impossible before the Mauryas" is a trap. (✅ owner 13 basic §2, §4, §5; ✅ owner 13 §11.4)
**Breakdown:**
- Taxes, tolls and tribute (✅ owner 13 §11.4)
- Fortifications; long-service troops (✅ owner 13 §11.4)
- Army size not recoverable (✅ owner 13 §11.4)
- Diplomacy: envoys, marriage, alliance, intelligence, threat, war (✅ owner 13 §11.4)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** ANC-2010, ANC-2084, 
**Trap:** Do not state precise army numbers; the Nanda army figures are rhetorical (ANC-2084).
**Mains hook:** Use fiscal capacity to explain why large states could sustain armies.
**Back:** *"Taxes pay, forts hold, armies follow: size unknown, existence likely."*

### Flashcard ANC-2139 — Monarchy and gaṇa-saṅgha: institutional axes in the Buddha age
**Front:** How do monarchy and gaṇa-saṅgha compare on authority, institutions, extraction, risk and social limit?
**Definition (learn):** Monarchy: dynastic king and court; officials, council, local intermediaries and army; royal claims to tax, tribute and tolls; concentrated command with succession and court faction risks; subjects lacked equal political power. Gaṇa/saṅgha: elite clan assembly with chief or smaller executive; assembly hall and political members; claims distributed among ruling clan oligarchs; shared elite stake with coalition and faction problems; membership narrower than the population. (✅ owner 13 §11.2)
**Breakdown:** Five axes (✅ owner 13 §11.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Assembly terms (sabhā, pariṣad, saṅgha) vary by source; santhāgāra and śalākā must be dated (✅ owner 13 §11.2).
**Links:** ANC-2075, ANC-2140, ANC-2068
**Trap:** Do not state that monarchical states were always superior. (✅ owner 13 §1)
**Mains hook:** "Compare monarchy and gaṇa-saṅgha without projecting modern democratic ideas onto ancient republics." (✅ owner 13 §7)
**Back:** *"King commands, clan deliberates, both extract; membership is narrow in both."*

### Flashcard ANC-2140 — Gaṇa-saṅghas are oligarchies, not modern democracies
**Front:** Why is "republic" an analytically unsafe label for the gaṇa-saṅghas, and what evidence supports the correction?
**Definition (learn):** Gaṇa-saṅghas were corporate political bodies dominated by aristocratic lineages, not egalitarian democracies or universal adult democracy. Evidence: the Vajji/Licchhavi confederacy and the Śākya republic. Modern democratic ideas must not be projected onto them. (✅ owner 13 advanced §1, §5; ✅ owner 13 basic §5, §7)
**Breakdown:**
- Corporate bodies; aristocratic lineages (✅ owner 13 advanced §1)
- Vajji/Licchhavi confederacy; Shākya republic (✅ owner 13 §7)
- Power usually with aristocratic/kin groups (✅ owner 13 advanced §5)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** ANC-2068, ANC-2139, ANC-2093
**Trap:** "Republics meant modern democracy" and "gaṇas had universal political participation" are both wrong. (✅ owner 13 §5, advanced §5)
**Mains hook:** Use the gaṇa-saṅgha to show political diversity without modern labels.
**Back:** *"Republic in name, aristocracy in practice: a gana is not a modern vote."*

### Flashcard ANC-2141 — Officials, village leaders and local administration
**Front:** Which officials and village leaders do the Buddha-age texts mention, and what caution applies?
**Definition (learn):** Textual traditions mention high officers (mahāmātra/amātya), ministers, commanders, judges/accountants and village leaders (grāmika/gāmaṇi/bhojaka). Their functions were not identical in every state, and later Mauryan offices cannot be projected backward. (✅ owner 13 §11.3)
**Breakdown:**
- mahāmātra/amātya: high officers (✅ owner 13 §11.3)
- grāmika/gāmaṇi/bhojaka: village leaders (✅ owner 13 §11.3)
- Judges and accountants (✅ owner 13 §11.3)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** ANC-2009 (ratnins), ANC-2064, owner 14 (Mauryan offices)
**Trap:** "Mauryan bureaucracy already existed" is wrong (✅ owner 13 §19).
**Mains hook:** Use officials to show layered local administration without a bureaucracy.
**Back:** *"Mahamatra leads, gramika keeps the village: functions varied, offices did not yet."*

### Flashcard ANC-2142 — Taxation, coercion and law: norm versus collection
**Front:** How were produce shares, tribute and dues collected, and why were they not voluntary?
**Definition (learn):** Produce shares, tribute (bali), tolls, fines and other dues supported rulers. Named fractions in normative texts are ideals, not uniform rates. Collection depended on officials, local power and coercion; peasants did not simply donate surplus. Law and punishment reflected status and varna prescriptions, but actual adjudication is poorly documented. (✅ owner 13 §11.4)
**Breakdown:**
- Shares; bali; tolls; fines (✅ owner 13 §11.4)
- Named fractions are normative (✅ owner 13 §11.4)
- Collection by coercion (✅ owner 13 §11.4)
- Law shaped by status; adjudication poorly documented (✅ owner 13 §11.4)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated; the "one-sixth" fraction is not a verified uniform rate (ANC-2090 cross-reference).
**Links:** ANC-2010, ANC-2141, ANC-2090
**Trap:** "Peasants gave surplus voluntarily" is an over-simplification; "one-sixth was the rate" is unverified. (✅ owner 13 §11.4, owner 11 §13.2)
**Mains hook:** Use extraction as the hinge between economy and state capacity.
**Back:** *"Bali, tolls, fines: ideals in texts, coercion on the field."*

### Flashcard ANC-2143 — Land: overlapping claims, neither wholly private nor wholly royal
**Front:** What does the evidence say about land ownership in the Buddha age, and what is the trap?
**Definition (learn):** Evidence supports overlapping claims by cultivators, households, village groups, chiefs/kings and revenue beneficiaries. It does not establish one uniform system of absolute private landed property or complete royal ownership of all land. (✅ owner 13 §12.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Cultivators; households; village groups; chiefs/kings; revenue beneficiaries (✅ owner 13 §12.1)
- No uniform private or royal ownership (✅ owner 13 §12.1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** ANC-2142, ANC-2144
**Trap:** "Land was either fully private or wholly royal" is wrong (✅ owner 13 §19).
**Mains hook:** Use overlapping claims to describe land as a layered institution.
**Back:** *"Many hands on the land: cultivators, villages, chiefs, revenue-takers."*

### Flashcard ANC-2144 — Surplus sustains specialists, courts, armies and monasteries
**Front:** What did surplus support in the Buddha age, and what is the chain?
**Definition (learn):** Surplus sustained non-producing specialists and urban populations; courts, officials, armies and fortifications; merchants, lenders and craft production; and monastic or renunciant communities through lay gifts. The chain: production → household surplus → market/gift/tax routes → differentiated wealth and state or monastic capacity. (✅ owner 13 §12.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Specialists and towns (✅ owner 13 §12.2)
- Courts, officials, armies, fortifications (✅ owner 13 §12.2)
- Merchants, lenders, crafts (✅ owner 13 §12.2)
- Monasteries via lay gifts (✅ owner 13 §12.2)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** ANC-2137, ANC-2016, ANC-2155
**Trap:** Do not describe the chain as iron → surplus → empire. (✅ owner 13 §12.2)
**Mains hook:** Use the chain to link economic surplus and religious patronage.
**Back:** *"Surplus feeds courts, armies, merchants and monks: one chain, many takers."*

### Flashcard ANC-2145 — Gahapati and seṭṭhi: property-holder and urban financier
**Front:** How do gahapati and seṭṭhi differ, and what is each one's trap?
**Definition (learn):** Gahapati (gṛhapati): householder or property-holder, a substantial property-owning producer and household head, often agrarian but not exclusively; not every ordinary householder was a wealthy landed magnate. Seṭṭhi (śreṣṭhin): prominent merchant, financier or corporate head in urban networks; not interchangeable with gahapati and not proof of a formal banking system. (✅ owner 13 §13.1; ✅ owner 13 advanced §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Gahapati: property-holder and producer (✅ owner 13 §13.1)
- Seṭṭhi: urban business and credit elite (✅ owner 13 §13.1)
- Wealth, gifts, credit and access to rulers created status beyond ritual rank (✅ owner 13 §13.1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gahapati/setthi as key categories of this age (✅ owner 13 advanced §4).
**Links:** ANC-2089, ANC-2131, ANC-2146
**Trap:** "Gahapati = seṭṭhi" and "every householder was wealthy" are both wrong. (✅ owner 13 §13.1)
**Mains hook:** Use gahapati and seṭṭhi to show that economic power complicated ritual hierarchy (advanced §7).
**Back:** *"Gahapati owns the house and field; setthi holds the purse in the city."*

### Flashcard ANC-2146 — Merchants, artisans and corporate groups: śreṇi, pūga, nigama and "eighteen guilds"
**Front:** Which merchant and artisan groups are named, and what caution applies to the Jātaka guild lists?
**Definition (learn):** Merchants (vāṇija) and specialists included metalworkers, carpenters, potters, weavers, ivory workers, leather workers, painters and transporters. Corporate terms śreṇi, pūga and nigama suggest association; detailed lists of "eighteen guilds" in Jātakas may reflect later layers. Pāli texts mention puga (guilds). (✅ owner 13 §13.2; ✅ owner 13 advanced §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Artisans and merchants (✅ owner 13 §13.2)
- Corporate terms (✅ owner 13 §13.2)
- Eighteen-guild list: possibly later (✅ owner 13 §13.2)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** ANC-2089, ANC-2145, ANC-2007
**Trap:** "Guilds were fully developed corporations by 600 BCE" is wrong (✅ owner 11 §19; owner 13 §19).
**Mains hook:** Use guild terms as evidence of organisation, with a dating caution.
**Back:** *"Sreni, puga, nigama: associations yes, eighteen guilds maybe later."*

### Flashcard ANC-2147 — Peasants, labour and dependence: kassaka, kammakāra, bhataka, dāsa/dāsī
**Front:** What do kassaka, kammakāra, bhataka and dāsa/dāsī denote, and why must dāsa not be equated with one slavery system?
**Definition (learn):** Cultivators (kassaka/kṛṣaka) were a major production base, differing in resources, dependence and obligation. Hired labour terms (kammakāra, bhataka) indicate wage, service or dependent work. Dāsa/dāsī may be translated as slave, bondsperson or servant depending on context; it must not be equated automatically with one later or modern slavery system. Debt, capture, birth, service and poverty could produce dependence. (✅ owner 13 §13.3)
**Breakdown:**
- kassaka/kṛṣaka: cultivators (✅ owner 13 §13.3)
- kammakāra, bhataka: hired or service labour (✅ owner 13 §13.3)
- dāsa/dāsī: context-dependent (✅ owner 13 §13.3)
- Dependence causes: debt, capture, birth, service, poverty (✅ owner 13 §13.3)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No reliable population proportions (✅ owner 13 §13.3).
**Links:** ANC-2013 (Later Vedic dāsa), ANC-2149, ANC-2148
**Trap:** Do not translate dāsa as "race" or equate it with one modern slavery system. (✅ owner 13 §13.3; owner 9 §13)
**Mains hook:** Use dependent labour to show a spectrum rather than a binary.
**Back:** *"Dasa is context: servant, bondsperson, slave, but never one system."*

### Flashcard ANC-2148 — Marginal and excluded groups: caṇḍāla, niṣāda and pukkusa
**Front:** Which terms mark despised or excluded groups, and what caution applies?
**Definition (learn):** Terms such as caṇḍāla, niṣāda and Pāli pukkusa can mark despised, forest, occupational or socially excluded groups in particular sources. Translations and chronology vary; these labels must not be treated as one unchanging pan-Indian caste list. Forest communities and hunters remained part of the political economy through labour, tribute, forest products, conflict and exchange. (✅ owner 13 §13.4)
**Breakdown:**
- caṇḍāla; niṣāda; pukkusa (✅ owner 13 §13.4)
- Forest and frontier communities in the economy (✅ owner 13 §13.4)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** ANC-2147, ANC-2149
**Trap:** Do not present these terms as a single caste ladder. (✅ owner 13 §13.4)
**Mains hook:** Use exclusion as a source-specific social fact.
**Back:** *"Candala, nishada, pukkusa: source-specific labels, not a caste ladder."*

### Flashcard ANC-2149 — Four-varna prescriptions and their complications
**Front:** What do the four varnas prescribe, and how do lived complications qualify each?
**Definition (learn):** Brāhmaṇa: ritual, teaching, purity and legal privilege, but Brāhmaṇas also served as ministers, land/revenue beneficiaries and householders. Kṣatriya: rulership and warfare; Buddhist texts sometimes privilege Kṣatriya lineage, yet kings depended on non-kin specialists. Vaiśya: production, agriculture, cattle and trade; Pāli vessa, gahapati and seṭṭhi do not map neatly. Śūdra: service and subordinated labour in Brahmanical prescriptions; actual occupations, wealth and Buddhist/Jain membership were more complex. (✅ owner 13 §14.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Four varnas as normative representation (✅ owner 13 §14.1)
- Dharmasūtra penalties and purity rules articulate hierarchy (✅ owner 13 §14.1)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** ANC-2013, ANC-2150, ANC-2145, ANC-2147
**Trap:** "Vaiśya equals every merchant; Śūdra equals every labourer or slave" is wrong (✅ owner 13 §19).
**Mains hook:** "Varna order sharpened alongside agrarian and state growth, but occupations and wealth cannot be reduced to four boxes."
**Back:** *"Varna is the prescription, lived society is wider: four boxes, many overlaps."*

### Flashcard ANC-2150 — Varna, jāti, occupation and class are distinct
**Front:** Why is "varna = caste = occupation = class" analytically false?
**Definition (learn):** Varṇa is a broad ideological order. Jāti can mean birth, kind, lineage or social/occupational group depending on the text; it should not be equated with the fully developed later caste system. Occupation could be hereditary yet change through migration, poverty, patronage, skill or political service. Kin/clan status mattered in gaṇa-saṅgha membership. Wealth and control over labour and credit created class-like differences. (✅ owner 13 §14.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Varṇa: ideology (✅ owner 13 §14.2)
- Jāti: birth, kind, lineage or occupation group (✅ owner 13 §14.2)
- Occupation: hereditary but mobile (✅ owner 13 §14.2)
- Class-like differences: wealth, labour and credit (✅ owner 13 §14.2)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** ANC-2013, ANC-2149, ANC-2151
**Trap:** "Varna disappeared because Buddhism rose" is wrong; varna norms hardened even as heterodox movements criticised ritual hierarchy (✅ owner 13 §5).
**Mains hook:** Analyse hierarchy as ideology plus class, kinship and occupation.
**Back:** *"Varna, jati, occupation, class: four different tools, one label is never enough."*

### Flashcard ANC-2151 — Mobility and its limits in the Buddha age
**Front:** How could people move up in status, and what constrained mobility?
**Definition (learn):** Rulers could elevate officials and donors; merchants and gahapatis could gain influence; renunciation offered an alternative institutional identity. Mobility remained constrained by birth ideology, household patriarchy, dependence, stigma, law and unequal access to resources. (✅ owner 13 §14.3)
**Breakdown:**
- Routes of mobility: office, donation, trade, renunciation (✅ owner 13 §14.3)
- Constraints: birth ideology, patriarchy, dependence, stigma, law, access (✅ owner 13 §14.3)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** ANC-2150, ANC-2155
**Trap:** Do not present renunciation as universal equality. (✅ owner 13 §16)
**Mains hook:** Use mobility to show the limited openness of the social order.
**Back:** *"Office, gift, trade, renunciation open doors; birth and stigma keep most shut."*

### Flashcard ANC-2152 — Women: household authority, property, courtesans, nuns and work
**Front:** What do the sources show about women's position in the Buddha age, and what are the caution points?
**Definition (learn):** Sources are mostly male-authored and elite or normative but record women as household managers, donors, workers, courtesans, renunciants, patrons and political actors. Marriage: normative texts favour controlled marriage and patrilineal continuity; forms varied. Property: women could possess gifts and resources and act as donors; inheritance privileged male lines in many traditions. Household authority: senior women managed wealth, labour and religious giving. Nuns: bhikkhunīs and Jain nuns created female renunciant institutions with constrained admission. (✅ owner 13 §15)
**Breakdown:**
- Marriage (✅ owner 13 §15)
- Property and inheritance (✅ owner 13 §15)
- Household authority (✅ owner 13 §15)
- Nuns; work (✅ owner 13 §15)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** ANC-2015, ANC-2040, ANC-2153, ANC-2154
**Trap:** "Women had no agency" and "Buddhist equality" are both wrong. (✅ owner 13 §19)
**Mains hook:** Use the mix of constraint and visibility to write a qualified gender answer.
**Back:** *"Donors, managers, courtesans, nuns: agency inside patriarchy, not equality."*

### Flashcard ANC-2153 — Ambapālī: courtesan, donor and urban actor
**Front:** Who was Ambapālī, and how should her visibility be read?
**Definition (learn):** Ambapālī is a courtesan (gaṇikā) figure who appears as a wealthy urban actor and donor in Buddhist memory; she links urban wealth, sexuality, civic status and patronage. Visibility and wealth do not equal general female freedom. (✅ owner 13 §15)
**Breakdown:**
- Gaṇikā; wealth; donor (✅ owner 13 §15)
- Urban civic status (✅ owner 13 §15)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** ANC-2152, ANC-2154
**Trap:** Do not generalise Ambapālī to uncomplicated equality. (✅ owner 13 §15)
**Mains hook:** Use her to show the urban elite woman within strong constraints.
**Back:** *"Ambapali: wealth, donation, civic presence: rare, not representative."*

### Flashcard ANC-2154 — Viśākhā and Mahāpajāpatī Gotamī: Buddhist memory anchors
**Front:** Why are Viśākhā and Mahāpajāpatī Gotamī cited as memory anchors, and what limit applies?
**Definition (learn):** Mahāpajāpatī Gotamī and Viśākhā are Buddhist memory anchors for women's religious prominence (Gotamī for the order of nuns; Viśākhā as a donor). Their prominence must not be generalised into uncomplicated equality. (✅ owner 13 §15; ✅ owner 10 §13.3 for Gotamī)
**Breakdown:**
- Gotamī: nuns' order (✅ owner 10 §13.3)
- Viśākhā: donor memory (✅ owner 13 §15)
- Limit: prominence is not uniform equality (✅ owner 13 §15)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** ANC-2040, ANC-2152, ANC-2153
**Trap:** Do not present the nuns' order as equal authority with monks. (✅ owner 10 §13.3)
