- Function: projected royal voice beyond the court; adapted messages to regions and scripts
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Prakrit-Brahmi predominant; Kharosthi in the north-west; Greek and Aramaic for frontier audiences (✅ owner 15 advanced section 11.3).
**Links:** ANC-3006, ANC-3007, ANC-3010. Topic 14 owns the edict detail.
**Trap:** Edicts did not create mass literacy or guarantee comprehension. Scribes, officials and oral proclamation mattered.
**Mains hook:** Argue that the edicts were a unique datable archive of kingship, but their reach must not be exaggerated.
**Back:** *"Brahmi for the many, Kharosthi for the north-west, Greek and Aramaic for the edge."*

### Flashcard ANC-3010 — Mauryan significance: scale and integration, with limits
**Front:** Why was Mauryan rule historically significant, and what limits qualify that claim?
**Definition (learn):** Mauryan rule created the first very large subcontinental formation for which extensive political and inscriptional evidence survives. Its significance is unprecedented scale and communication, not uniform administration or a modern nation-state.
**Breakdown:**
- Scale: Magadhan core linked to provincial nodes, corridors and frontiers
- Administration: king, court, council, provincial centres, princes, district officers, revenue, justice, army
- Economy: circulation along major corridors; roads, rivers, coins, guild-like groups
- Limits: distance, ecology, regional scripts, local elites, frontier peoples, uneven archaeology
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Ashokan inscriptions name Tamil polities but these were not demonstrated provinces (✅ owner 15 advanced section 11.1).
**Links:** ANC-3001, ANC-3008, ANC-3009, ANC-3016.
**Trap:** Do not claim the Mauryas unified India like a modern state, or that every village was governed by a uniform bureaucracy. Use "unprecedented imperial integration with uneven control".
**Mains hook:** Discuss significance as capacity and connectivity rather than uniform control; qualify with the Arthashastra's prescriptive status.
**Back:** *"Vast, connected, uneven: capacity without uniformity."*

### Flashcard ANC-3011 — Decline model: Brahmanical reaction
**Front:** What is the Brahmanical-reaction explanation of Mauryan decline, and why is it contested?
**Definition (learn):** This model holds that anti-sacrificial or dhamma policies provoked Brahmanas, culminating in Pushyamitra's coup. It is one of the models R.S. Sharma lists as a hypothesis.
**Breakdown:**
- Mechanism: dhamma and anti-sacrificial policy provoke Brahmana reaction
- Evidence: Pushyamitra was a Brahmana; later persecution stories
- Criticism: Ashoka respected Brahmanas; the reaction is not proved as an empire-wide cause
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Model listed as a hypothesis by R.S. Sharma (✅ owner 15 basic section 1; section 13 matrix).
**Links:** ANC-3005, ANC-3012, ANC-3017.
**Trap:** Pushyamitra's Brahmana identity does not prove a religious persecution campaign. Harshacharita and Buddhist persecution narratives are later and motivated.
**Mains hook:** Treat the model as a hypothesis with a source limit; show that the Shunga transition does not prove an empire-wide religious revolution.
**Back:** *"A Brahmana took the throne: the story is later, the revolution unproved."*

### Flashcard ANC-3012 — Decline models: Ashokan pacifism and Buddhist expenditure
**Front:** Why are "Ashokan pacifism" and "Buddhist donations emptied the treasury" weak single-cause explanations?
**Definition (learn):** Pacifism holds that renouncing conquest after Kalinga weakened the army. The Buddhist-expenditure thesis holds that donations drained the treasury and alienated groups. Both are listed in the decline matrix and both are weak.
**Breakdown:**
- Pacifism: dhamma-vijaya rhetoric after Kalinga
- Counter-evidence: army and coercion persisted; frontier warnings continued; no evidence Ashoka disbanded defence
- Buddhist expenditure: Buddhist narratives of generosity
- Counter-evidence: hagiographic stories are not fiscal records; diverse sects were patronised
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Kalinga led to dhamma-vijaya rhetoric (✅ owner 15 section 13). Hagiographic donation stories are not treasury accounts (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** ANC-3011, ANC-3013, ANC-3016. Kalinga detail: Topic 14.
**Trap:** "Ashoka disbanded the army after Kalinga" and "Buddhist donations bankrupted the empire" are both unsafe claims.
**Mains hook:** Reject the moral single cause and show how dhamma policy coexisted with coercive authority.
**Back:** *"Non-violence did not disarm the empire; donation stories are not the accounts."*

### Flashcard ANC-3013 — Decline model: financial crisis (RS Sharma versus Upinder Singh)
**Front:** What do RS Sharma and Upinder Singh each say about a Mauryan financial or fiscal crisis?
**Definition (learn):** RS Sharma lists financial pressure among possible causes. Upinder Singh is cautious: she questions strong financial-crisis claims because the evidence is limited and some older explanations may be anachronistic.
**Breakdown:**
- Fiscal-administrative pressure: army, officials, court and monuments strained revenue (model)
- Evidence: prescriptive tax lists and perception of a large establishment
- Criticism: no empire-wide bankruptcy archive; no treasury accounts
- Upinder Singh's caution: financial crisis is debated, not a proven fact
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Financial crisis debated; Upinder Singh notes lack of clear evidence (✅ owner 15 advanced section 1 and section 2).
**Links:** ANC-3008, ANC-3012, ANC-3016.
**Trap:** Do not state a Mauryan financial crisis as proven in an advanced answer.
**Mains hook:** Show the difference between a hypothesis (RS Sharma) and an evidentiary caution (Upinder Singh) and use the matrix to rank them.
**Back:** *"Sharma proposes a fiscal strain; Upinder asks for the accounts."*

### Flashcard ANC-3014 — Decline models: Bactrian Greek pressure, frontier neglect and provincial rule
**Front:** How do external pressure and provincial abuse feature in the Mauryan decline debate?
**Definition (learn):** Bactrian Greek pressure in the north-west, frontier neglect and oppressive provincial rule are three linked models. Pressure accelerated contraction but cannot explain simultaneous internal change.
**Breakdown:**
- Frontier neglect: weak north-west allowed Bactrian Greek intrusion (post-Mauryan Greek expansion)
- Oppressive provincial rule: Taxila complaint traditions; Kalinga concern about unjust imprisonment
- Criticism: literary and royal admonition evidence does not prove universal rebellion
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** An invasion by the Bactrian Greeks further weakened the empire (✅ owner 15 advanced section 1). Taxila and Kalinga are localised concerns (✅ owner 15 basic section 13).
**Links:** ANC-3016, ANC-3017, ANC-3018 (Central Asian contacts).
**Trap:** The Bactrian Greek invasion did not "destroy the entire empire". Frontier pressure was regional and inside a wider fragmentation.
**Mains hook:** Show how external pressure worked on a weakened internal structure; use Taxila and Kalinga as bounded examples.
**Back:** *"Greeks at the gate, Taxila in complaint: pressure, not the whole story."*

### Flashcard ANC-3015 — Decline models: weak successors, succession and overcentralisation
**Front:** What is the "weak successors" explanation, and how should a good answer convert it into a mechanism?
**Definition (learn):** The model holds that short reigns and poor leadership weakened command. The stronger version specifies succession disputes, elite and military cohesion, overstretch of a vast empire and dependence on court and provincial cooperation.
**Breakdown:**
- Weak successors: long first three reigns, then many short ones (✅ owner)
- Succession and court politics: divergent king lists, Brihadratha-Pushyamitra tradition
- Overcentralisation/overstretch: distance, ecology, provincial centres, regional assertion
- Criticism: "weak" is a label, not a mechanism; evidence on individuals is sparse
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** The first three Mauryan rulers had long reigns; later rulers had short reigns (✅ owner 15 advanced section 4).
**Links:** ANC-3003, ANC-3004, ANC-3016.
**Trap:** Do not explain decline by "weak successors" alone. Specify the mechanism.
**Mains hook:** Use succession as one structural strain among several, with a feedback loop to revenue and compliance.
**Back:** *"Short reigns are a symptom: name the mechanism behind them."*

### Flashcard ANC-3016 — Multicausal synthesis and feedback loop of Mauryan decline
**Front:** What is the best multicausal model of Mauryan decline, and what feedback loop links the causes?
**Definition (learn):** The best model combines structural vulnerabilities (scale, unequal depth of control, fiscal-military dependence, reliance on elite cooperation) with political shocks (succession disputes, provincial assertion, frontier pressure, court or military takeover).
**Breakdown:**
- Structural: very large scale; unequal control depth; fiscal-military dependence; elite cooperation
- Shocks: succession disputes; provincial assertion; frontier pressure; takeover at Pataliputra
- Feedback loop: succession uncertainty → weaker provincial compliance and revenue → reduced military and communication capacity → more regional assertion → narrower imperial resources
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Feedback loop given by owner 15 section 14. No model independently explains chronology, regional variation and dynastic termination together.
**Links:** ANC-3010, ANC-3011 to ANC-3015, ANC-3017.
**Trap:** The loop describes a model to test region by region, not synchronous collapse.
**Mains hook:** A 20-mark answer: source problem → significance matrix → post-Ashoka chronology → decline models → interaction mechanism → dynastic fall versus continuity → balanced conclusion.
**Back:** *"Succession loosens revenue, revenue loosens the army, provinces slip away."*

### Flashcard ANC-3017 — Three endings: dynastic fall, imperial fragmentation, social continuity
**Front:** What are the three distinct endings a Mauryan decline answer must separate?
**Definition (learn):** The dynastic fall ended Mauryan kingship at Pataliputra. Imperial fragmentation meant the loss or reconfiguration of territorial and provincial control. Social, economic and religious continuity or transformation continued regionally, at different dates.
**Breakdown:**
- Dynastic fall: termination of Mauryan kingship at Pataliputra (c. 187/185 BCE)
- Imperial fragmentation: loss or reconfiguration of provinces
- Continuity: settlements, routes, crafts, religions and institutions continued or changed regionally
- Note: these did not occur everywhere on one date
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Dynastic end c. 187/185 BCE (✅ owner 15 section 9). Continuity table: section 16 owner 15.
**Links:** ANC-3001, ANC-3005, ANC-3016, ANC-3039 (post-Mauryan regional states).
**Trap:** "The dynasty fell, so society and economy collapsed" is wrong. Separate dynasty, empire and continuing regional life.
**Mains hook:** Argue that Mauryan significance and Mauryan collapse must be judged separately; the legacy outlived the dynasty without direct institutional descent being proved.
**Back:** *"Three endings: the throne, the territory, the life."*

### GROUP — Central Asian Contacts: Indo-Greeks, Shakas, Parthians, Kushanas (basic owner 16; advanced owner 16)

### Flashcard ANC-3018 — Concept: Central Asian contacts after the Mauryas (c. 200 BCE–300 CE)
**Front:** What is the core idea of Topic 16, and why is "invasion" an inadequate word for it?
**Definition (learn):** After the Mauryas, north-western India was a connected zone of Indo-Greek, Shaka, Indo-Parthian and Kushana formations. These overlapped, competed and ruled different regions; they were not one linear dynasty chain. The period is best read as interaction and innovation.
**Breakdown:**
- Indo-Greeks, Shakas, Indo-Parthians (Pahlavas), Kushanas (Yuezhi-Kushana) — overlapping polities
- Contact covered armies, pastoral groups, merchants, monks, artisans, envoys and families
- Effects: coinage, cavalry, trade, religion, art, language, polity
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Period begins about 200 BCE; Gandhara and Mathura schools flourish in this zone (✅ owner 16 basic sections 1 and 9). Upinder Singh frames c. 200 BCE–300 CE as interaction and innovation (✅ owner 16 advanced section 1).
**Links:** ANC-3019 to ANC-3037. Preceded by Mauryan decline: ANC-3001.
**Trap:** Do not present Indo-Greeks → Shakas → Parthians → Kushanas as one neat succession.
**Mains hook:** "The post-Mauryan north-west was a zone of interaction and innovation, not only invasion." Qualify the claim with source and region.
**Back:** *"Overlap, not a line: many polities, one connected frontier."*

### Flashcard ANC-3019 — Indo-Greeks: Menander (Milinda) and the Milindapanha
**Front:** Who was Menander, when did he rule, and what is the Milindapanha?
**Definition (learn):** Menander (Milinda) was an Indo-Greek king of the mid-second century BCE, known securely through coins and political distribution. The Milindapanha is a layered Buddhist dialogue between King Milinda and the monk Nagasena.
**Breakdown:**
- Menander: Indo-Greek ruler; coins establish him more securely than biographical details
- Dating: RS chronology 165–145 BCE (owner); mid-second century BCE
- Milindapanha: dialogue text; not a court transcript; not proof of exclusive royal conversion
- Association: Sakala/Sagala and wide coin circulation
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 165–145 BCE in RS chronology (✅ owner 16 basic must-know). Milindapanha layered Buddhist text (✅ owner 16 advanced section 12.3).
**Links:** ANC-3020, ANC-3038. Topic 2 (literature) owns the Milindapanha's literary history.
**Trap:** "Menander certainly converted and Nagasena wrote the Milindapanha" is unsafe. Treat the dialogue as a layered tradition.
**Mains hook:** Use Menander to show how coins and Buddhist texts give different kinds of evidence about the same king.
**Back:** *"Coins prove the king; the dialogue preserves the debate."*

### Flashcard ANC-3020 — Indo-Greek coinage: bilingual portraits and round and square issues
**Front:** What did Indo-Greek coins introduce, and why are they useful for chronology?
**Definition (learn):** Indo-Greek coins introduced royal portraiture and named rulers. They combined carefully struck round issues with regionally adapted square issues, bilingual legends (often Greek and Prakrit) and Greek script with Kharosthi on many issues.
**Breakdown:**
- Royal portraits and named rulers
- Round issues alongside regionally adapted square issues
- Bilingual legends (Greek and Prakrit); Greek script with Kharosthi
- Epithets, deity programmes, monograms, weight standards and overstrike sequences useful for chronology
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Indo-Greek bilingual portrait coinage (✅ owner 16 basic section 12.2 and must-know; the owner notes gold issues are securely Kushana, not Indo-Greek).
**Links:** ANC-3019, ANC-3030, ANC-3035.
**Trap:** Coins reveal claims and circulation, not a complete map of direct taxation or control. Indo-Greek bilingual coins are not proof of Indo-Greek gold coinage at subcontinental scale.
**Mains hook:** Use coinage to show political communication across audiences, then qualify with the limits of circulation as evidence of rule.
**Back:** *"Portrait, two scripts, round or square: the Indo-Greek money message."*

### Flashcard ANC-3021 — Heliodorus pillar at Besnagar and Antialcidas
**Front:** What does the Heliodorus pillar at Besnagar record, and what does it not prove?
**Definition (learn):** The Besnagar (Vidisha) pillar records Heliodorus, an ambassador of the Indo-Greek king Antialcidas, at the court of Bhagabhadra. Heliodorus calls himself a Bhagavata, and the pillar is associated with Vasudeva/Garuda devotion.
**Breakdown:**
- Ambassador: Heliodorus, from Antialcidas to Bhagabhadra
- Religious self-description: Bhagavata; Vasudeva/Garuda devotion
- Significance: interstate diplomacy; Prakrit-Brahmi public inscription; cross-regional networks
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Besnagar pillar; Antialcidas; Bhagabhadra (✅ owner 16 basic section 12.4). Exact date not given in the owner file (⚠️).
**Links:** ANC-3019, ANC-3032 (Mathura and religious plurality).
**Trap:** "Heliodorus proves all Indo-Greeks became Vaishnavas" is wrong. It proves one ambassador's inscriptional Bhagavata affiliation.
**Mains hook:** Use the pillar to show religious affiliation as personal and political agency, not ethnicity.
**Back:** *"An ambassador's pillar: one Bhagavata, not a converted kingdom."*

### Flashcard ANC-3022 — Rajuvula and Strato III: Indo-Scythian pressure in the east Punjab
**Front:** Who was Rajuvula, what does the Wikipedia article date him to, and how is his capture of Sagala presented?
**Definition (learn):** Rajuvula was an Indo-Scythian Great Satrap (Mahakshatrapa) who ruled around Mathura and replaced the last Indo-Greek kings. His coinage imitated the types of the rulers he supplanted.
**Breakdown:**
- Title: Indo-Scythian Mahakshatrapa; coins use "Satrap" (Chatrapasa)
- Indo-Greek replacement: Strato II and Strato III (Wikipedia)
- Coinage: mint in Sagala possibly; coins found near Sankassa and in east Punjab
- Mathura lion capital consecrated under his reign; queen Aiyasi Kamuia named as chief donor (Konow's view)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Reign c. 10–25 CE (✅ Wikipedia: Rajuvula). The owner's "early 1st c. CE" date matches this range loosely (✅ owner 16 advanced). The defeat of Strato III and capture of Sagala/Sialkot are owner claims NOT found on the Wikipedia page, which says only "invaded" and "possibly minted in Sagala" (⚠️).
**Links:** ANC-3023, ANC-3019, ANC-3032.
**Trap:** The Wikipedia page names Strato II and Strato III as replaced; do not assert Sagala's capture as a fact without a secure source.
**Mains hook:** Use Rajuvula to show how Indo-Scythian rulers adopted Indo-Greek coin idioms; qualify the military claims.
**Back:** *"Rajuvula: Indo-Scythian in Mathura, Greek coins in his purse, Sagala under question."*

### Flashcard ANC-3023 — Shakas: branches, Maues and the Kshatrapa titles
**Front:** Who were the Shakas, what titles did they use, and what is Maues's place?
**Definition (learn):** Shakas (Scythians) were mobile groups who entered through Central Asia and the north-west. They formed several ruling branches in the north-west, Mathura and western India. Kshatrapa and Mahakshatrapa were satrap and great-satrap titles, meaning delegated or regionally autonomous authority.
**Breakdown:**
- Branches: north-west, Taxila/Punjab, Mathura, western India, upper Deccan (✅ owner)
- Maues: early Shaka ruler in the north-west, known from coins and inscriptions; date and extent debated
- Titles: Kshatrapa and Mahakshatrapa; not one fixed constitutional tier
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Shakas controlled a larger part of India than the Greeks, with several branches (✅ owner 16 basic section 1). Maues: date debated (✅ owner 16 advanced section 13.2).
**Links:** ANC-3022, ANC-3024, ANC-3025.
**Trap:** "Shaka" covers multiple branches and periods; it is not a single dynasty. Do not equate the Kshatrapa title with Achaemenid administration.
**Mains hook:** Use Shaka titles to show how delegated authority could become dynastic and regionally autonomous.
**Back:** *"Many Shaka branches, one satrap title: delegated power that grew teeth."*

### Flashcard ANC-3024 — Western Kshatrapas: Nahapana and the Kardamaka line
**Front:** Who was Nahapana, and what is the Chashtana (Kardamaka) line's significance for western India?
**Definition (learn):** Nahapana of the Kshaharata line ruled parts of western India and supported trade-route and religious donations, for example through Ushavadata (Rishabhadatta). The Kardamaka line associated with Chashtana established durable Western Kshatrapa power.
**Breakdown:**
- Nahapana: Kshaharata ruler of western routes and coasts
- Ushavadata: donor linked to trade-route Buddhist gifts (owner)
- Chashtana and Kardamaka: durable Western Kshatrapa power
- Satavahana conflict is a bounded bridge; full Satavahana history sits in ANC-3039 onward
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Nahapana's Kshaharata power on strategic western routes (✅ owner 16 basic section 13.4 and advanced 11.1). Wikipedia on Gautamiputra confirms Nahapana's son-in-law Rishabhadatta held land that was re-granted (✅ Wikipedia: Gautamiputra Satakarni).
**Links:** ANC-3042 (overstrikes), ANC-3041, ANC-3023.
**Trap:** "Nahapana is a Satavahana topic" is wrong. He is a Western Kshatrapa-linked ruler; Satavahana conflict is a bridge.
**Mains hook:** Use Nahapana to show the western coast as a contested route zone between Shakas and Satavahanas.
**Back:** *"Nahapana holds the west; Gautamiputra takes the coins back."*

### Flashcard ANC-3025 — Rudradaman I and the Junagadh prashasti
**Front:** When did Rudradaman I rule, what is the Junagadh inscription, and what does it claim about the Sudarshana lake and Satakarni?
**Definition (learn):** Rudradaman I was a Western Kshatrapa ruler. His Junagadh inscription is a major early long Sanskrit royal prashasti, recording genealogy, conquests, learning and virtues. It claims he repaired the Sudarshana lake embankment and spared a Satakarni ruler, probably Vashishtiputra Satakarni, because of kinship.
**Breakdown:**
- Reign: c. 130–150 CE (✅ Wikipedia: Rudradaman I)
- Junagadh inscription: dated 150 CE (✅ Wikipedia); Sanskrit
- Sudarshana lake: embankment repaired; the lake had been built under the Mauryas (✅ Wikipedia)
- Satakarni: defeated twice in fair fight, then spared because of their connection; Vashishtiputra Satakarni married Rudradaman's daughter (✅ Wikipedia)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Reign 130–150 CE; Junagadh dated 150 CE (✅ Wikipedia: Rudradaman I). The owner's claim that the repair involved no forced labour or tax levy is NOT stated on the Wikipedia page (⚠️).
**Links:** ANC-3024, ANC-3041, ANC-3058.
**Trap:** The Junagadh text is a royal prashasti, not a neutral military or fiscal report. Do not state the "no forced labour" line as a fact without the inscription text.
**Mains hook:** Use the inscription as a prashasti: a source of political claim, kinship politics and public works, read with caution.
**Back:** *"Junagadh: a Sanskrit eulogy, a lake repaired, a kinsman spared."*

### Flashcard ANC-3026 — Indo-Parthians (Pahlavas) and Gondophares
**Front:** Who were the Indo-Parthians (Pahlavas), and what does Gondophares tell us?
**Definition (learn):** Indo-Parthian power was centred in parts of Afghanistan, Gandhara and the north-west in the first century CE. Gondophares is known through coins and inscriptions, including a dated Takht-i-Bahi context.
**Breakdown:**
- Area: Afghanistan, Gandhara, north-west
- Gondophares: coins and inscriptions; dated Takht-i-Bahi context (owner)
- Later Christian tradition (Acts of Thomas) associates Gondophares with St Thomas, but is a later tradition
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gondophares attested by coins and inscriptions (✅ owner 16 advanced section 14). Takht-i-Bahi dated context (✅ owner 16 basic section 14). Dates not given in owner file; ⚠️ for any precise date.
**Links:** ANC-3018, ANC-3023, ANC-3027.
**Trap:** "Gondophares proves every St Thomas tradition" is wrong. Coins and inscriptions attest the ruler; the Christian narrative is later.
**Mains hook:** Show that Indo-Parthians were not an empty bridge between Shakas and Kushanas; they had their own political and religious history.
**Back:** *"Gondophares on coin and stone; Thomas on the later page."*

### Flashcard ANC-3027 — Yuezhi to Kushana: the ladder of Kushana rulers
**Front:** How did the Kushana power form, and what is the ladder of its main rulers?
**Definition (learn):** Chinese histories describe the Yuezhi moving west, dominating Bactria. A branch, the Guishuang (Kushana), consolidated the others. The ruler sequence is strongest where coins, inscriptions and archaeology converge.
**Breakdown:**
- Kujula Kadphises: early consolidation; coin adaptations and titulature
- Vima Takto / Soter Megas: identification debated (Rabatak genealogy)
- Vima Kadphises: major gold and copper issues; Shiva/Oesho iconography
- Kanishka I: Rabatak genealogy; wide inscriptions and coins; Buddhist patronage
- Huvishka: long reign; diverse coin pantheon; Mathura and Central Asian evidence
- Vasudeva I: later major ruler with Indic royal name; Oesho coin prominence
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Kujula Kadphises, Vima Kadphises, Kanishka I, Huvishka, Vasudeva I appear in owner 16 section 15. Rabatak inscription central for genealogy (✅ owner 16 basic).
**Links:** ANC-3028, ANC-3029, ANC-3030, ANC-3036.
**Trap:** Dates and territorial stages of early Kushanas are debated; the order is reconstructed from coins and inscriptions.
**Mains hook:** Show the Kushanas as a consolidation of Central Asian groups into a multi-regional empire with plural religion and trade.
**Back:** *"Kujula, Vima, Kanishka, Huvishka, Vasudeva: the coin-and-stone ladder."*

### Flashcard ANC-3028 — Kanishka's chronology: AD 78 versus about AD 127 (trap)
**Front:** When did Kanishka accede, and why is the AD 78 link to the Shaka era unsafe?
**Definition (learn):** Older scholarship linked Kanishka's accession with the Shaka era of AD 78. Modern chronologies place his accession about AD 127. The Shaka era and Kanishka's accession are separate questions.
**Breakdown:**
- Older view: accession c. AD 78 = start of Shaka era (R.S. Sharma follows it in the owner)
- Modern view: accession c. AD 127 (Upinder Singh; Wikipedia: "historians no longer regard this date as that of Kanishka's accession")
- Kanishka's reign: c. 127–150 CE (Wikipedia gives 127–150; succession table 127–151)
- Indian National Calendar uses the Shaka era; this does not settle Kanishka's accession
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Shaka era AD 78; Kanishka c. AD 127 (✅ Wikipedia: Kanishka; ✅ owner 16 advanced). Some Wikipedia tables give 127–151 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** ANC-3027, ANC-3029, ANC-3025 (Rudradaman's date anchors the Shaka-era debate).
**Trap:** "Kanishka founded the Shaka era in 78 CE" is an older thesis. Do not equate AD 78 with his accession.
**Mains hook:** Show how a chronology dispute changes the reading of Kushana power and Buddhist patronage; state the preferred dating and the debate.
**Back:** *"Seventy-eight was the old date; one-twenty-seven is the new."*

### Flashcard ANC-3029 — Kanishka's Buddhist patronage and the Kashmir council claim
**Front:** What does the tradition say about Kanishka's Buddhist council, and what is the Wikipedia qualification?
**Definition (learn):** Kanishka is strongly associated with Buddhist patronage in the north-west: Purushapura (Peshawar) as main capital, a major stupa and monastic networks. A council in Kashmir under Kanishka, with Sarvastivada scholastic compilation, is preserved in later tradition.
**Breakdown:**
- Patronage: monasteries, stupa, coins, inscriptions (✅ owner)
- Capital: Purushapura (Peshawar) main; Mathura another major capital (✅ Wikipedia)
- Kashmir council: tradition says he headed the Fourth Buddhist Council in Kashmir, with Vasumitra and Ashvaghosha presiding (✅ Wikipedia report)
- Wikipedia notes the council claim rests on a single source (Beg 2020) and that he never became a Buddhist, though he gave royal sponsorship (✅ Wikipedia)
- Mahayana emerged gradually; Kanishka did not single-handedly create it (✅ owner 16 advanced)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Main capital Purushapura; Fourth Council in Kashmir claim (✅ Wikipedia: Kanishka).
**Links:** ANC-3027, ANC-3028, ANC-3033, ANC-3037.
**Trap:** "Kanishka created Mahayana at one council" is unsafe. Buddha image-making cannot be attributed to one ruler or sect.
**Mains hook:** Argue that royal sponsorship and Buddhist transmission depended on monks, merchants and monasteries, not a single royal mission.
**Back:** *"Patron, not convert: a Kashmir council on one source, a stupa at Purushapura."*

### Flashcard ANC-3030 — Kushana coin pantheon: plural royal iconography
**Front:** Which deities appear on Kushana coins, and what do they show about kingship?
**Definition (learn):** Kanishka and Huvishka issues show Buddhist, Brahmanical, Iranian and Greek divine names and forms. A deity on a coin is royal iconography, not proof of exclusive personal conversion.
**Breakdown:**
- Buddha (BODDO), Shiva and Oesho (strong Shiva associations)
- Iranian: Mithra (Miiro), Atash, Nana, Mao
- Greek: Helios, Selene
- Rule: plural ideology and audiences; patronage could be plural and strategic
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Coin representations of Buddha and Shiva, Atash and Mithra, Helios and Selene (✅ owner 16 advanced section 1 and must-know). Oesho and Miiro named (✅ owner 16 basic section 18).
**Links:** ANC-3020, ANC-3027, ANC-3029, ANC-3032.
**Trap:** "Kushana coinage was purely Buddhist" is wrong; "Vasudeva's name proves Vaishnavism" is also unsafe.
**Mains hook:** Use the coin pantheon to show a cosmopolitan ruling ideology, then qualify with the limits of coins as religious evidence.
**Back:** *"Buddha, Shiva, Mithra, Helios on one purse: a plural realm, not a single creed."*

### Flashcard ANC-3031 — Gandhara art: Greco-Bactrian elements (2019 Mains route)
**Front:** What are the Central Asian and Greco-Bactrian elements in Gandhara art, and how should a 10-mark answer use them?
**Definition (learn):** Gandhara art was a layered north-western Buddhist workshop tradition. It shows Hellenistic modelling and Central Asian costume alongside Indic Buddhist iconography. It is not "imported Greek art".
**Breakdown:**
- Greco-Bactrian (Hellenistic): modelled anatomy, wavy hair, naturalistic faces, heavy pleated drapery (owner route)
- Central Asian: Kushana patronage; tunic and boot costume; mobile artisans; route context
- Materials: schist and later stucco
- Judgement: Buddhist subject and Indic ritual context prevent the label "imported Greek art"
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2019 GS-I Mains Q1, 10 marks, 150 words (✅ owner 16 basic section 26; advanced integration block).
**Links:** ANC-3032, ANC-3018, ANC-3029, ANC-3030.
**Trap:** "Gandhara is Greek; Mathura is purely Indian" is unsafe. Both were regional, interactive workshop traditions.
**Mains hook:** Open with Gandhara as north-western Buddhist production; give the Greco-Bactrian and Central Asian dimensions; conclude against "imported Greek art".
**Back:** *"Greek hair, Kushana boots, Buddhist subject: a hybrid north-west, not an import."*

### Flashcard ANC-3032 — Mathura school: red sandstone and plural religion
**Front:** How does the Mathura school differ from Gandhara, and what religions does it show?
**Definition (learn):** Mathura developed an indigenous Indian sculptural idiom with red sandstone and established local workshops. Its patrons included rulers, guilds, monasteries and plural religious communities; it shows Buddhist, Jaina and Brahmanical images.
**Breakdown:**
- Material: red sandstone; established local workshop traditions
- Visual base: yaksha-derived and local forms plus Buddhist, Jaina and Brahmanical innovations
- Patrons: rulers, guilds, monasteries, plural religious communities
- Mathura lion capital consecrated under Rajuvula (see ANC-3022)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Red sandstone; Mathura as a Kushana capital (✅ owner 16 basic section 19). Mathura lion capital under Rajuvula (✅ Wikipedia: Rajuvula).
**Links:** ANC-3031, ANC-3022, ANC-3030.
**Trap:** "Mathura was a copy of Gandhara" is wrong. It had its own idiom and patronage.
**Mains hook:** Compare Gandhara and Mathura as two regional idioms shaped by patronage and mobility, not foreign vs Indian.
**Back:** *"Gandhara wears Hellenism; Mathura keeps its red sandstone."*

### Flashcard ANC-3033 — Kushana governance: King of Kings, Son of God, tiered empire
**Front:** What titles and governance forms did the Kushanas use, and why is their empire called "tiered"?
**Definition (learn):** Kushana rulers used royal titles such as King of Kings and Son of God (devaputra). They governed through satraps and regional intermediaries, local rulers, cities, guilds and religious establishments, with military mobility and route control.
**Breakdown:**
- Titles: King of Kings (shaonano shao); Son of God (devaputra)
- Intermediaries: satraps, kshatrapas, local rulers
- Institutions: cities, guilds, religious establishments
- Tools: military mobility, route control, multilingual coin and inscription communication
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** King of Kings (shaonano shao) and Son of God (devaputra) (✅ owner 16 basic section 16). Rabatak claims to Saketa, Kaushambi, Pataliputra and Champa are royal claims, not secure territory (✅ owner).
**Links:** ANC-3027, ANC-3034, ANC-3037.
**Trap:** Do not draw a maximalist Kushana map from one inscription or coin distribution.
**Mains hook:** Argue that the Kushanas ran a tiered, layered empire; its strength was route control plus local partnership.
**Back:** *"King of Kings on top, satraps below: a tiered realm, not a uniform block."*

### Flashcard ANC-3034 — Silk Routes: plural networks and Kushana position
**Front:** How should the "Silk Route" be described, and where did Kushana polities stand?
**Definition (learn):** Kushana and related polities occupied strategic positions linking northern India with Bactria, the Tarim Basin, China, Iran and the Roman world. Indian Ocean routes linked western ports to the Red Sea and western Asia. "Silk Routes" should be plural and networked.
**Breakdown:**
- Land: Bactria, Tarim Basin, China, Iran
- Sea: western Indian ports to the Red Sea and western Asia
- Actors: caravan communities, monasteries, ports, local producers
- Taxation and protection: states taxed, protected or contested routes
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Plural Silk Routes; Kushana strategic position (✅ owner 16 basic section 17.1).
**Links:** ANC-3033, ANC-3035, ANC-3091 (Roman trade).
**Trap:** "Silk trade alone created Kushana prosperity" is wrong; add agriculture, crafts, tribute and local markets.
**Mains hook:** Use the plural network to explain how the Kushanas converted location into political and commercial advantage, with limits.
**Back:** *"Routes in the plural: states taxed them, merchants carried them."*

### Flashcard ANC-3035 — Kushana gold coinage and the "Roman gold" trap
**Front:** Why are Kushana gold coins important, and why is "Roman trade alone caused Kushana gold" insufficient?
**Definition (learn):** Kushana rulers issued extensive gold and copper coinage with royal portraits, titles and diverse deities. Gold availability could reflect trade, tribute, bullion recycling and imperial fiscal choices; trade alone does not explain it.
**Breakdown:**
- Coin types: royal portraits, titles, diverse deities
- Gold: extensive; mostly securely Kushana in subcontinental scale (owner)
- Explanations: trade, tribute, bullion recycling, fiscal choice
- Coin circulation can outlast or move beyond political rule
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Extensive Kushana gold and copper coinage (✅ owner 16 basic section 17.3 and must-know).
**Links:** ANC-3020, ANC-3030, ANC-3096 (coin hoard versus circulation).
**Trap:** Coin finds do not prove direct political rule or routine market use. Do not attribute the gold to one cause.
**Mains hook:** Use coinage to show royal ideology and monetised exchange, with the limits of coin evidence as a measure of prosperity.
**Back:** *"Gold speaks of kings and bullion, not of trade alone."*

### Flashcard ANC-3036 — Rabatak inscription and the Bactrian language in Greek script
**Front:** What is the Rabatak inscription, and what did Kushana royal writing use?
**Definition (learn):** The Rabatak inscription is central for Kushana genealogy, royal titulature, language policy and territorial claims. Kushana royal contexts used Bactrian written in Greek script, alongside Greek, Kharosthi, Brahmi, Prakrit and growing Sanskrit use.
**Breakdown:**
- Rabatak: genealogy, titulature, language policy, territorial claims
- Scripts: Bactrian in Greek script in major royal contexts; Kharosthi, Brahmi, Prakrit, Sanskrit
- Multilingual evidence does not prove equal literacy or a single cosmopolitan identity
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Rabatak central for genealogy (✅ owner 16 basic section 15.1 and section 20).
**Links:** ANC-3027, ANC-3033, ANC-3009 (Ashokan scripts).
**Trap:** Translation changed titles, deity names and royal images; do not assume a one-to-one reading of Bactrian royal terms.
**Mains hook:** Use the Rabatak to show how language and script were tools of royal communication across audiences.
**Back:** *"Bactrian in Greek letters: the Rabatak's language of kingship."*

### Flashcard ANC-3037 — Kushana contraction: Sasanian pressure and Lesser Kushans
**Front:** How did Kushana power contract, and what successor formations persisted?
**Definition (learn):** Kushana contraction involved dynastic division and regionalisation, rising Sasanian pressure with Kushano-Sasanian power west of the Indus, assertion of regional polities in north India and changing route and fiscal conditions. The Lesser Kushans persisted.
**Breakdown:**
- Dynastic division and regionalisation
- Sasanian pressure; Kushano-Sasanian power west of the Indus
- Regional polities in north India
- Successor: Lesser Kushans and other formations
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Kushano-Sasanian and Lesser Kushan formations (✅ owner 16 advanced section 23). Dates not fixed in owner file (⚠️).
**Links:** ANC-3027, ANC-3033, ANC-3016.
**Trap:** "One invasion or one weak ruler" cannot explain every region. Coins and art continued in transformed political contexts.
**Mains hook:** Treat the contraction as a process with external and internal drivers, not one collapse.
**Back:** *"Sasanians from the west, regions from within: contraction, not a single fall."*

### Flashcard ANC-3038 — Trap register: non-linear sequence and foreignness
**Front:** Which unsafe claims about Central Asian contacts must an answer avoid?
**Definition (learn):** The period's unsafe claims come from treating the sequence as linear, coins as proof of rule, foreigners as wholly separate from Indian society, and art as ethnicity.
**Breakdown:**
- "Indo-Greeks → Shakas → Parthians → Kushanas everywhere" (wrong: overlapping)
- "Coin finds prove direct rule" (wrong: circulation exceeds borders)
- "Kanishka was an Indo-Greek ruler" (wrong: Kushana)
- "Central Asians remained foreign to Indian society" (wrong: migration and adaptation)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner trap register, owner 16 basic section 24.
**Links:** ANC-3018, ANC-3019, ANC-3028, ANC-3031, ANC-3032.
**Trap:** Foreign origin is not cultural separateness; analyse adaptation without binary assimilation.
**Mains hook:** Write the Central Asian story as interaction and transformation, not invasion-only.
**Back:** *"Overlap, not line; coin, not rule; adaptation, not binary."*

### GROUP — The Satavahanas and the Deccan (basic owner 17; advanced owner 17)

### Flashcard ANC-3039 — Concept: the Satavahanas as a network polity
**Front:** What is the core idea of the Satavahanas as a historical polity, and why is "network" the right frame?
**Definition (learn):** The Satavahanas were one of the earliest durable large Deccan formations after the Mauryas. Their power rested on regional chiefs (maharathis and mahabhojas), merchants, Buddhist monks and Brahmana donees, linked by inland routes, ports and Brahmanical and Buddhist idioms. It was not a Mauryan-style bureaucratic state.
**Breakdown:**
- Political base: Maharashtra-Andhra core; at times Malwa, Kathiawar and Karnataka (✅ owner)
- Power: layered control through aharas, officials, chiefs and donors
- Networks: inland routes, western and eastern coastal trade, Buddhist caves
- Legitimacy: Brahmanical self-fashioning plus Buddhist donative patronage
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Core Maharashtra-Andhra; Shaka conflict in the west (✅ owner 17 basic sections 1 and 2; advanced section 1).
**Links:** ANC-3040, ANC-3045, ANC-3046, ANC-3051, ANC-3056.
**Trap:** Do not present the Satavahanas as a centralised empire or as a loose tribal chiefdom. Qualify as layered, regionally negotiated power.
**Mains hook:** "A layered network polity linking agrarian inland zones with western and eastern trade circuits." Qualify with prashasti inflation and source limits.
**Back:** *"Chiefs, merchants, monks, Brahmanas: a network, not a bureaucracy."*

### Flashcard ANC-3040 — Satavahana chronology and origins: the Andhra equation
**Front:** What do the Puranas, inscriptions and coins say about the Satavahana origin and dates, and what is the Andhra equation?
**Definition (learn):** Puranic texts refer to Andhras or Andhrabhrityas; inscriptions and coins use Satavahana and Satakarni names. Their equation is conventional and plausible but does not settle the homeland, the earliest dates, or one unbroken line over three centuries.
**Breakdown:**
- Early phase: Simuka (conventional founder; date debated)
- Span: from early debated beginnings to roughly mid-3rd century CE (owner)
- Puranic lists: names and durations vary
- End: c. mid-3rd c. CE; regional successor states (✅ owner)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Span to roughly mid-3rd century CE (⚠️ owner 17 basic must-know; no single date scheme is uncontested). Andhra equation conventional (✅ owner 17 advanced section 9.1).
**Links:** ANC-3039, ANC-3055.
**Trap:** Do not state "Simuka founded the dynasty in one known year and place" as fact; dates and origin are reconstructed.
**Mains hook:** Use the source conflict (Puranas, inscriptions, coins) to show why early Deccan chronology is reconstructed.
**Back:** *"Andhras in the Puranas, Satakarnis on the coins: a conventional equation."*

### Flashcard ANC-3041 — Gautamiputra Satakarni and the Nashik prashasti
**Front:** Who was Gautamiputra Satakarni, what does the Nashik prashasti claim, and who issued it?
**Definition (learn):** Gautamiputra Satakarni restored Satavahana power after Shaka pressure. The Nashik prashasti, issued by his mother Gautami Balashri, eulogises him as destroyer of the Kshaharatas, conqueror of wide territories and protector of varna order.
**Breakdown:**
- Issuer: Gautami Balashri, mother; inscription in Pandavleni Cave 3, Nashik (✅ Wikipedia)
- Dated to 19th regnal year of Vashishthiputra Pulumavi (✅ Wikipedia)
- Grant: village grant to Buddhist monks of the Bhadrayaniya sect (✅ Wikipedia)
- Claims: destroyer of Kshaharatas; "lord of Benakataka"; king of many territories; "ekabrahmana" (✅ Wikipedia)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Dates of Gautamiputra are disputed: Wikipedia lists 1st or 2nd century CE with several datings (86–110; 103–127; 106–130; infobox c. 60–85). The owner's "early second century CE" is one reconstruction, so flag it. (⚠️ conflict owner 17 versus Wikipedia)
**Links:** ANC-3024, ANC-3042, ANC-3047, ANC-3058, ANC-3060.
**Trap:** The prashasti is royal eulogy, not a neutral biography. Its claims exceed securely mapped control (Wikipedia: "Sinopoli doubts he had effective control over all of it").
**Mains hook:** Read the prashasti as political self-fashioning: use its names and rivals, but cross-check the praise with coins and rival records.
**Back:** *"Mother's eulogy, Nashik stone: destroyer of Kshaharatas, claimant of many lands."*

### Flashcard ANC-3042 — Nahapana's overstrikes and the Jogalthambi hoard
**Front:** What do Nahapana's overstruck coins at Jogalthambi show, and what do they not prove?
**Definition (learn):** A hoard of Nahapana's silver coins at Jogalthambi, Nashik district, includes coins re-struck by Gautamiputra. Overstriking shows a political reversal and revalidation of currency, but not extermination of every Shaka formation or permanent control of former Kshatrapa territory.
**Breakdown:**
- Hoard site: Jogalthambi, Nashik district (✅ Wikipedia)
- Overstrike types: arched chaitya (three-arched hill) and Ujjain symbol (✅ Wikipedia)
- Significance: major political reversal in the west
- Limit: no exact battle, date or extent (owner)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Count of re-struck coins: the owner's "8000+" figure is NOT confirmed by Wikipedia, which gives no count (⚠️ owner 17 basic evidence table).
**Links:** ANC-3024, ANC-3041, ANC-3058, ANC-3096.
**Trap:** A restriking is not a territorial census. "Gautamiputra destroyed all Shakas" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Use the hoard as hard numismatic evidence of a reversal, while noting the limits of coin-based political inference.
**Back:** *"Nahapana's silver, Gautamiputra's stamp: a reversal in the purse, not the whole west."*

### Flashcard ANC-3043 — Naneghat inscription of Naganika
**Front:** What does the Naneghat inscription record, and why is Queen Naganika important?
**Definition (learn):** Naganika's Naneghat inscription records genealogy, royal titles, major Vedic sacrifices and gifts. Its location on a pass links the western plateau to Konkan routes. It shows queenly dynastic and public agency.
**Breakdown:**
- Content: genealogy, titles, Vedic sacrifices, gifts
- Location: Naneghat pass; plateau to Konkan route
- Agency: queenly dynastic memory and public commemoration
- Limit: a royal-donative inscription, not a full political biography
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Naneghat records Vedic sacrifices and gifts (✅ owner 17 advanced section 10.2 and basic section 17). Satakarni I and Naganika's role (owner).
**Links:** ANC-3039, ANC-3047, ANC-3059.
**Trap:** Do not confuse Naneghat's Satakarni with the later Gautamiputra; repeated Satakarni names are hard to identify.
**Mains hook:** Use Naganika to show royal women's agency within a patrilineal order.
**Back:** *"Naganika's pass-stone: sacrifices, gifts and a queen's name."*

### Flashcard ANC-3044 — Yajna Sri Satakarni and the ship-motif coins
**Front:** What are the ship-motif coins associated with Yajna Sri Satakarni, and what do they prove?
**Definition (learn):** Yajna Sri Satakarni is a later major ruler known through inscriptions and coins. His issues with ship motifs are useful for maritime associations, but they do not alone prove state control of all sea trade.
**Breakdown:**
- Ruler: late second or early third century CE (owner)
- Coins: ship motifs; coastal associations
- Limit: extent and date range vary
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Late second or early third century CE; ship-motif coinage (⚠️ owner 17 basic timeline and advanced section 15). Exact dates not confirmed by a fetched source.
**Links:** ANC-3050, ANC-3054, ANC-3055.
**Trap:** Ship coins do not prove state monopoly of the sea.
**Mains hook:** Use the ship coins to show maritime links, while qualifying state control of sea trade.
**Back:** *"Ship on the coin: sea links, not sea monopoly."*

### Flashcard ANC-3045 — Satavahana administrative terms: ahara, amatya, mahamatra, mahasenapati
**Front:** What were the main Satavahana administrative terms, and what is the limit on reading them as a fixed hierarchy?
**Definition (learn):** District-level units were called ahara; officials included amatyas and mahamatras; the military office was mahasenapati; village officers were gramikas. The terms show administrative layering, not a Mauryan-level uniform machinery.
**Breakdown:**
- Ahara: territorial or revenue-administrative unit (district)
- Amatya and mahamatra: officials; executive and fiscal roles
- Mahasenapati: military office; sometimes regional
- Gramika: village-level officer; scattered evidence
- Limit: titles do not prove Mauryan continuity of function
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Aharas, amatyas, mahamatras, mahasenapatis, gramikas (✅ owner 17 basic section 2 and advanced section 4).
**Links:** ANC-3039, ANC-3046, ANC-3056.
**Trap:** "Aharas prove Mauryan-level centralisation" is wrong; they show administrative continuity, not identical machinery.
**Mains hook:** Use the terms to show a network of officers and chiefs; avoid claiming a uniform bureaucracy.
**Back:** *"Ahara the district, amatya the official, mahasenapati the general."*

### Flashcard ANC-3046 — Maharathis and mahabhojas: regional chiefs and cave donors
**Front:** Who were the maharathis and mahabhojas, and why do they matter for Satavahana power?
**Definition (learn):** Maharathis and mahabhojas were powerful local or regional chiefs who appear as donors in western Deccan Buddhist caves and had matrimonial ties with the Satavahanas. They show power distributed among regional elites.
**Breakdown:**
- Terms: maharathi; mahabhoja (chief or donor)
- Role: cave donors; matrimonial partners; regional authority
- Limit: "feudatory" is analytical shorthand, not a fixed feudal constitution
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Maharathis and mahabhojas as cave donors with matrimonial ties (✅ owner 17 advanced section 1 and basic section 7 table).
**Links:** ANC-3039, ANC-3051, ANC-3055.
**Trap:** Do not call them "feudal lords" without evidence of fixed hereditary tenure.
**Mains hook:** Use the chiefs as evidence that Satavahana power depended on regional partners rather than a Mauryan bureaucracy.
**Back:** *"Maharathi on the cave wall: a partner, not a provincial governor."*

### Flashcard ANC-3047 — Matronymics: Gautamiputra and Vashishthiputra are not matriarchy
**Front:** What do names such as Gautamiputra and Vashishthiputra signal, and what do they not signal?
**Definition (learn):** Matronymic names identify maternal gotra or lineage connections and can express marriage alliance and legitimacy. They do not prove matriarchy, matrilineal royal succession, female ownership of the state, or the absence of paternal dynastic descent.
**Breakdown:**
- Signal: maternal lineage and marriage alliance
- Not: matriarchy; matrilineal succession
- Women's agency: Naganika and Gautami Balashri issued or commissioned inscriptions
- Context: predominantly patrilineal and patriarchal order
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Matronymics signal lineage, not matriarchy (✅ owner 17 advanced and basic must-know).
**Links:** ANC-3041, ANC-3043, ANC-3059.
**Trap:** "Matronymics prove matriarchy" is a standard UPSC trap.
**Mains hook:** Use royal women's inscriptions to show agency inside patrilineal rule.
**Back:** *"Gautami's son is a lineage name, not a female throne."*

### Flashcard ANC-3048 — Early land grants with fiscal exemptions
**Front:** What do Satavahana and Kshatrapa grants show, and how far should they be read as feudal?
**Definition (learn):** Early grants of villages, fields or revenues and fiscal exemptions went to Buddhist monastics and Brahmanas. They show experiments with religious endowment, revenue rights and regional authority. They are not the fully developed Gupta-style feudal grant.
**Breakdown:**
- Recipients: Brahmanas and Buddhist monks
- Rights: fiscal exemptions; some revenue and fields
- Limit: each grant must be translated and dated; not all sovereignty transferred
- Terminology: avoid calling every gift brahmadeya without evidence
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Early grants with fiscal exemptions (✅ owner 17 advanced and basic section 14). Satavahanas show an early beginning (✅ owner 17 basic trap).
**Links:** ANC-3039, ANC-3053, ANC-3139 (Gupta-era grants).
**Trap:** "Land grants began only in the Gupta age" is wrong; "grants created developed feudalism" is also wrong.
**Mains hook:** Use Satavahana grants to show a foreshadowing of medieval agrarian grants, without projecting feudalism backwards.
**Back:** *"Early grants, small scale: the seeds, not the feudal tree."*

### Flashcard ANC-3049 — Language and script: Prakrit in Brahmi; Gathasaptasati
**Front:** What language and script did Satavahana royal inscriptions use, and what is the Gathasaptasati?
**Definition (learn):** Most Satavahana royal inscriptions use Prakrit written in Brahmi. The Gathasaptasati (Gaha Sattasai) is traditionally attributed to King Hala and preserves Prakrit lyric traditions, rural and urban imagery. It was compiled and redacted over time.
**Breakdown:**
- Inscriptions: Prakrit in Brahmi (public and donative communication)
- Literature: Gathasaptasati, traditionally Hala
- Limit: compilation extended over time; not one ruler's unchanged text
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Prakrit in Brahmi (✅ owner 17 basic must-know). Hala and the Gathasaptasati (✅ owner 17 basic must-know; advanced section 9.2).
**Links:** ANC-3039, ANC-3057, ANC-3065 (Sangam-era language contrast).
**Trap:** "Satavahana official language was Sanskrit" is wrong. Prakrit dominated their inscriptions.
**Mains hook:** Use Prakrit to show public communication in the Deccan, with Sanskrit later rising in prashastis.
**Back:** *"Prakrit in Brahmi, Hala's verses: the Deccan speaks in Prakrit."*

### Flashcard ANC-3050 — Satavahana lead coinage
**Front:** What metals did Satavahana coins use, and what does lead coinage tell us?
**Definition (learn):** Satavahana coinage used lead extensively, alongside copper, potin and other metals. Portraits, legends, symbols, fabrics and overstrikes help reconstruct rulers and circulation.
**Breakdown:**
- Metals: lead (extensive), copper, potin
- Evidence: legends, symbols, portraits, overstrikes
- Meaning: regional metal choice; denomination and circulation need numismatic study
- Limit: payments in kind continued; monetisation was regional
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Lead coinage extensive (✅ owner 17 basic section 15 and advanced).
**Links:** ANC-3042, ANC-3044, ANC-3096.
**Trap:** "Lead coins imply poverty or no monetisation" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Use lead coinage to show regional monetary strategy, with the limits of coin-based economic inference.
**Back:** *"Lead in the purse: a regional choice, not a poverty sign."*

### Flashcard ANC-3051 — Buddhist cave networks: Nashik, Karle, Junnar, Kanheri, Amaravati
**Front:** Which Buddhist cave and stupa sites are linked to Satavahana-era donors, and what do they show?
**Definition (learn):** Buddhist cave records in Nashik, Karle, Junnar and Kanheri connect monks, nuns, merchants, artisans, Yavanas, chiefs and rulers to western Deccan routes. Amaravati and the lower Krishna landscape show long Buddhist patronage across dynasties.
**Breakdown:**
- Western Deccan caves: Nashik, Karle, Junnar, Kanheri
- Donors: merchants, artisans, monks, nuns, chiefs, Yavanas, rulers
- Eastern Deccan: Amaravati; lower Krishna; long patronage across dynasties
- Use: donors, routes, labour, institutions and political change
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Nashik, Karle, Junnar, Kanheri; Amaravati (✅ owner 17 basic must-know and advanced). Architecture details sit with Indian Art and Culture.
**Links:** ANC-3046, ANC-3052, ANC-3059, ANC-3054.
**Trap:** Caves are evidence for donors and routes, not just for architecture or religion.
**Mains hook:** Use the donor lists to show religion as a node in commercial and political networks.
**Back:** *"Donors carved in stone: merchants, monks and chiefs on the trade road."*

### Flashcard ANC-3052 — Nagarjunakonda: the Ikshvaku trap
**Front:** Why must Nagarjunakonda not be made wholly Satavahana?
**Definition (learn):** Nagarjunakonda belongs mainly to the successor Ikshvaku florescence in the eastern Deccan. Its Buddhist monuments show strong Ikshvaku patronage, not a single Satavahana phase.
**Breakdown:**
- Period: successor Ikshvaku florescence
- Region: lower Krishna, eastern Deccan
- Contrast: Amaravati has a long sequence; Nagarjunakonda has Ikshvaku patronage
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Nagarjunakonda and Ikshvakus (✅ owner 17 advanced section 17 and trap register).
**Links:** ANC-3051, ANC-3055.
**Trap:** "Amaravati and Nagarjunakonda are one Satavahana phase" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Use the Ikshvaku example to show successor powers continuing Buddhist patronage.
**Back:** *"Nagarjuna's hill belongs to the Ikshvakus, not the Satavahanas."*

### Flashcard ANC-3053 — Agrarian base: iron, paddy transplantation and the Krishna-Godavari delta
**Front:** Which agrarian technologies and zones underpinned Satavahana prosperity?
**Definition (learn):** Iron ploughshares, paddy transplantation and cotton production strengthened the Deccan agrarian base. The Krishna-Godavari delta became a major rice-producing zone, connecting political expansion with a productive rural economy.
**Breakdown:**
- Technology: iron ploughshares
- Crops: paddy transplantation; cotton
- Zone: Krishna-Godavari delta (rice)
- Limit: direct Satavahana-era evidence limited; many claims from later inscriptions and archaeology
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Iron ploughshares, paddy transplantation, cotton, Krishna-Godavari (✅ owner 17 basic must-know and section 14.2).
**Links:** ANC-3039, ANC-3048, ANC-3054.
**Trap:** Paddy transplantation is high-yield but is not one date; do not date the technique to a single year.
**Mains hook:** Connect political expansion with agrarian productivity; qualify with evidence of date.
**Back:** *"Iron share, transplanted paddy, delta rice: the Deccan's agrarian base."*

### Flashcard ANC-3054 — Crafts, guilds and towns: Paithan, Tagara, Junnar, Nashik
**Front:** Which towns and crafts connected Satavahana inland production to trade, and what were śreṇis?
**Definition (learn):** Crafts included textiles, metalwork, pottery, beads, ivory and stonework. Guilds (śreṇis) could receive deposits and support monasteries, but there was no economy-wide banking system. Towns such as Paithan, Tagara (Ter), Junnar and Nashik connected inland production to passes and ports.
**Breakdown:**
- Crafts: textiles, metal, pottery, beads, ivory, stone
- Guilds (śreṇi): deposits and donations; not a banking system
- Towns: Paithan, Tagara (Ter), Junnar, Nashik
- Ports: Sopara, Kalyan, Chaul; Bharukachchha-linked networks
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Śreṇi deposits and donations; towns and ports (✅ owner 17 basic section 15).
**Links:** ANC-3085, ANC-3086, ANC-3090 (ports), ANC-3053.
**Trap:** Guilds were economic corporate bodies, not modern banks; do not call deposits "banking".
**Mains hook:** Use guilds and towns to show how crafts and merchants supported monasteries and interregional exchange.
**Back:** *"Śreṇi keep the deposit, not a bank: crafts, towns, passes."*

### Flashcard ANC-3055 — Post-Satavahana successors: Ikshvakus, Abhiras, Western Kshatrapas, Vakatakas
**Front:** Which powers replaced or reconfigured the Satavahanas, and how did the decline unfold?
**Definition (learn):** Satavahana contraction around the third century CE was regional and multicausal: succession problems, renewed Western Kshatrapa power in the west, rising local chiefs, changing routes and ports, and uneven control between western and eastern Deccan. Successors included Ikshvakus in the east, Abhiras in the west, continuing Western Kshatrapas, and Vakataka power in parts of the Deccan.
**Breakdown:**
- East: Ikshvakus
- West: Abhiras; continuing Western Kshatrapas
- Also: local lineages and Vakataka power
- Causes: succession; Western Kshatrapa revival; local chiefs; route and port change; fiscal and coin shifts
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** End c. mid-3rd c. CE (✅ owner 17 basic mini-timeline); successor list (✅ owner 17 advanced section 20).
**Links:** ANC-3052, ANC-3016 (decline models), ANC-3113 (Vakataka regency).
**Trap:** "Dynastic fall caused synchronised Deccan collapse" is wrong. Agriculture, crafts and Prakrit epigraphy continued in changed settings.
**Mains hook:** Argue for regional transformation with continuity, not a single collapse.
**Back:** *"Ikshvaku east, Abhira west, Kshatrapas and Vakatakas around: a regional sunset."*

### Flashcard ANC-3056 — Trap: Pratishthana as a fixed permanent capital
**Front:** Why is "Pratishthana was the permanent capital" an unsafe claim?
**Definition (learn):** Pratishthana (Paithan on the Godavari) was a major western Deccan centre, but Satavahana rule shifted between western and eastern zones. Nashik, Junnar, Naneghat, Paithan, Amaravati, Dharanikota and the Krishna-Godavari region had different political, commercial and religious roles.
**Breakdown:**
- Pratishthana: major western centre
- Other centres: Nashik, Junnar, Naneghat, Amaravati/Dharanikota
- Shift: western and eastern zones alternately central
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Multiple centres, not one fixed capital (✅ owner 17 advanced section 10.3).
**Links:** ANC-3039, ANC-3045, ANC-3054.
**Trap:** Do not impose a fixed capital or empire map across all reigns.
**Mains hook:** Argue that multiple centres signal network rule rather than a fixed administrative core.
**Back:** *"Paithan was a centre, not the only capital."*

### Flashcard ANC-3057 — Trap: Satavahanas were not a north-Indian Ganga dynasty and not a Sanskrit-speaking state
**Front:** What two common Satavahana misconceptions must be corrected?
**Definition (learn):** The Satavahanas were a Deccan power, not a north-Indian Ganga dynasty. Their royal inscriptions used Prakrit written in Brahmi, not Sanskrit as the official language.
**Breakdown:**
- Region: Deccan (Maharashtra-Andhra core)
- Language: Prakrit in Brahmi
- Brahmanical claims: Sanskrit appears in prashastis, but not as the main royal language
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Deccan power; Prakrit in Brahmi (✅ owner 17 basic traps).
**Links:** ANC-3039, ANC-3049.
**Trap:** These two are standard distractors in Prelims statements.
**Mains hook:** Link the Deccan location to the Prakrit public idiom as one measure of regional identity.
**Back:** *"Deccan, Prakrit, Brahmi: not Ganga, not Sanskrit."*

### Flashcard ANC-3058 — Trap: "Gautamiputra destroyed all Shakas"
**Front:** How far did Gautamiputra's victory over the Shakas go?
**Definition (learn):** Gautamiputra's victory over the Kshaharatas, shown by overstrikes and the Nashik prashasti, was a major reversal in the west. It did not destroy every Shaka line; the Western Kshatrapas continued and later revived.
**Breakdown:**
- Shown: overstrikes on Nahapana coins; Nashik eulogy
- Not shown: extermination of all Shaka groups
- Later: Western Kshatrapas persisted and revived (see ANC-3055)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Western Kshatrapas continued (✅ owner 17 advanced section 20; Wikipedia Gautamiputra on the Nahapana defeat).
**Links:** ANC-3041, ANC-3042, ANC-3055, ANC-3025.
**Trap:** A prashasti's "destroyer" claim is a royal claim; the coin evidence supports a major victory only.
**Mains hook:** Use the example to show how royal claims must be cross-checked against numismatic evidence.
**Back:** *"One hoard overstruck, many Shakas still standing."*

### Flashcard ANC-3059 — Satavahana religious profile: Brahmanical sacrifice and Buddhist donation
**Front:** What religious profile do Satavahana sources show, and why is "exclusively Brahmanical" or "exclusively Buddhist" wrong?
**Definition (learn):** Satavahana rulers claimed Brahmana identity and performed Vedic sacrifices (Naneghat), while patronising Buddhist monks and nuns through caves and grants. Vaishnava, Shaiva and other Brahmanical elements also appear. Royal Brahmana claims served legitimacy but did not produce exclusive patronage.
**Breakdown:**
- Brahmanical: Vedic sacrifice; Vasudeva/Sankarshana and Shaiva elements
- Buddhist: Nashik, Karle, Junnar, Kanheri; monks and nuns
- Coexistence: plural patronage
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Naneghat sacrifices; Buddhist cave donations (✅ owner 17 advanced section 1 and section 17).
**Links:** ANC-3043, ANC-3051, ANC-3047.
**Trap:** "Satavahana patronage was exclusively Buddhist" is wrong; Brahmanical sacrifice coexisted.
**Mains hook:** Argue that plural patronage was a political strategy of legitimacy across social groups.
**Back:** *"Sacrifice at Naneghat, monks at Nashik: plural patronage."*

### Flashcard ANC-3060 — Prashasti as source: how to read royal eulogy
**Front:** How should a historian use a prashasti such as the Nashik or Junagadh text?
**Definition (learn):** A prashasti is a eulogy. It must be mined for names, titles, territories and rivals, but its praise language must be cross-checked with coins, archaeology and rival inscriptions.
**Breakdown:**
- Use: names, titles, territories, rivals
- Check: coins, archaeology, rival records
- Trap: praise is not a neutral account
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Prashastis as eulogies (✅ owner 17 advanced concept card; ✅ Wikipedia: Rudradaman and Gautamiputra pages).
**Links:** ANC-3041, ANC-3025, ANC-3020.
**Trap:** The Nashik prashasti is not a modern biography, nor is the Junagadh text a neutral military report.
**Mains hook:** Use the method "claim → coin or archaeological test → regional limitation" for any eulogy.
**Back:** *"Praise is evidence, but it is also a claim: test before you trust."*

### GROUP — The Sangam Age and the Deep South (basic owner 18; advanced owner 18)

### Flashcard ANC-3061 — Concept: the Sangam Age as a literary-archaeological horizon
**Front:** What is the Sangam Age, and how must it be reconstructed?
**Definition (learn):** "Sangam Age" is a modern label for early historic Tamilakam, reconstructed from poetic anthologies, grammar and poetics, inscriptions, archaeology, coins and external texts. It is a convenient horizon, not one date range or one centralised system.
**Breakdown:**
- Sources: poetry (anthologies, idylls), Tolkappiyam, later epics, Tamil-Brahmi inscriptions, archaeology, coins, Greco-Roman accounts
- Megalithic background: iron, burials, black-and-red ware (ANC-3073)
- Sangam texts speak of warriors, taxation, judiciary, merchants, craftsmen and farmers (✅ owner)
- Method: read poems with archaeology and inscriptions
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sangam period c. 300 BCE–300 CE (✅ Wikipedia: Sangam literature). Megaliths from c. 1000 BC (✅ owner 18 basic mini-timeline).
**Links:** ANC-3062, ANC-3066, ANC-3073, ANC-3075.
**Trap:** Do not treat Sangam poems as a single dated inscription, or as a literal court chronicle.
**Mains hook:** Argue that Sangam is a case of regional state formation read through poetry and archaeology together.
**Back:** *"Poem, potsherd, pass: the Sangam is reconstructed, not read off."*

### Flashcard ANC-3062 — Sangam date range: c. 300 BCE–300 CE and the alternatives
**Front:** What date range is given for the Sangam period, and how do scholars differ?
**Definition (learn):** Most scholars place the Sangam period about c. 300 BCE–300 CE. Zvelebil prefers 100 BCE–250 CE. Post-Sangam literature is dated after c. 250 CE and before c. 600 CE.
**Breakdown:**
- Broad span: c. 300 BCE–300 CE (majority)
- Alternative: 100 BCE–250 CE (Zvelebil)
- Post-Sangam: c. 250–600 CE
- Caveat: texts, inscriptions and sites have different dates
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 300 BCE–300 CE; 100 BCE–250 CE; post-Sangam c. 250–600 CE (✅ Wikipedia: Sangam literature).
**Links:** ANC-3061, ANC-3063, ANC-3064.
**Trap:** The "Sangam period" is a horizon, not a securely bounded political age.
**Mains hook:** Use the range to show that periodisation is a convention, and qualify with site-specific chronology.
**Back:** *"Three hundred before, three hundred after: a convention, not a border."*

### Flashcard ANC-3063 — Eight Anthologies (Ettuttokai)
**Front:** Name the Eight Anthologies (Ettuttokai) and state their main content.
**Definition (learn):** The Ettuttokai (Eight Collections) are the anthologies of akam and puram poetry. They describe chiefs, war, gifts, love, work, landscape and exchange.
**Breakdown:**
- Natrrinai
- Kuruntokai
- Ainkurunuru
- Patirruppattu
- Paripatal
- Kalittokai
- Akananuru
- Purananuru
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Eight anthologies as listed (✅ Wikipedia: Sangam literature). Owner: akam/puram, chiefs, gifts, landscape (✅ owner 18 advanced section 10).
**Links:** ANC-3062, ANC-3065, ANC-3064.
**Trap:** Individual poems are hard to date; oral composition and anthology selection intervene.
**Mains hook:** Use the anthologies as a social record with selection and genre caveats.
**Back:** *"Eight in the Ettuttokai; poems, not dated annals."*

### Flashcard ANC-3064 — Ten Idylls (Pattuppattu) and Tolkappiyam
**Front:** What are the Ten Idylls (Pattuppattu), and what is the Tolkappiyam?
**Definition (learn):** The Pattuppattu (Ten Idylls) is a set of ten long poems, from Tirumurukarruppatai to Malaipatukatam (Wikipedia), describing courts, towns, routes, ports and patrons. The Tolkappiyam is the oldest surviving Tamil grammar and poetics, giving the tinai framework.
**Breakdown:**
- Pattuppattu: courts, towns, routes, ports, patrons; praise and abundance are not quantitative reporting
- Tolkappiyam: grammar, poetics, social and literary categories, tinai
- Chronology: composite; later layers debated
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Ten songs from Tirumurukarruppatai to Malaipatukatam (✅ Wikipedia: Sangam literature). Tolkappiyam as oldest grammar within the corpus (✅ Wikipedia). Tolkappiyam's exact date: not given by Wikipedia fetch (⚠️).
**Links:** ANC-3063, ANC-3071, ANC-3065.
**Trap:** Do not date the Tolkappiyam to one century from the anthologies alone; its layers are debated.
**Mains hook:** Use the idylls for ports and courts while cross-checking praise with archaeology.
**Back:** *"Ten idylls on ports and courts; one grammar for the poems."*

### Flashcard ANC-3065 — Akam and puram: the two poetic divisions
**Front:** What are akam and puram, and what share of Sangam poetry does each form?
**Definition (learn):** Sangam poetry is broadly divided into akam (inner, love and emotion) and puram (outer, war, gifts, fame and public life). About three-fourths of the poetry is akam and about one-fourth puram.
**Breakdown:**
- Akam: romantic love, emotions, feelings
- Puram: war, heroic deeds, gifts, public life
- Share: roughly 3:1 akam to puram (Wikipedia)
- Use: literary keys linked to tinai landscapes
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Three-fourths akam, one-fourth puram (✅ Wikipedia: Sangam literature). Akam/puram as divisions (✅ owner 18 basic section 1).
**Links:** ANC-3063, ANC-3064, ANC-3071, ANC-3078.
**Trap:** Akam poems are conventional and are not statistical marriage practice.
**Mains hook:** Use akam and puram to show emotional ideals and public heroism, with the limit that poetry idealises.
**Back:** *"Akam is love within; puram is war and gift without."*

### Flashcard ANC-3066 — Three Sangams: legend versus history
**Front:** What is the legend of the three Sangams at Madurai, and how do historians assess it?
**Definition (learn):** The traditional story of three successive Sangam academies around Madurai (and Kapatapuram) comes from later literary and commentarial memory. Scholars regard the mythical chronology as ahistorical; the first two have no evidence.
**Breakdown:**
- Legend: three gatherings, very long durations
- Scholars: "ahistorical and mythical" (✅ Wikipedia)
- Zvelebil's view: a scholarly academy in Madurai at the beginning of the Christian era (✅ Wikipedia)
- Shulman: no evidence for any such literary academies (✅ Wikipedia)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Three Sangams: legend; first two lack evidence (✅ Wikipedia: Sangam literature). Owner: three academies not independently verified (✅ owner 18 advanced section 9).
**Links:** ANC-3061, ANC-3062, ANC-3063.
**Trap:** "Three historical Sangam academies produced the corpus" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Separate the memory of Sangams from the historical literary community that may have existed in Madurai.
**Back:** *"Three academies: legend. One Madurai circle: possibly history."*

### Flashcard ANC-3067 — Muvendar: the Chera, Chola and Pandya crowned lineages
**Front:** Who were the Muvendar, and what were their remembered cores?
**Definition (learn):** The Muvendar ("three crowned kings") were the Chera, Chola and Pandya lineages. Their remembered cores were western Tamilakam and Kongu (Chera), the Kaveri basin (Chola), and Madurai with the far south (Pandya). They were not modern empires with fixed borders.
**Breakdown:**
- Chera: western coast; Kerala; Kongu; Vanji or Karur traditions
- Chola: Kaveri basin; Uraiyur and Puhar
- Pandya: Madurai; Korkai pearls
- Caution: chiefs and clans could shift allegiance
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Three crowned lineages with cores (✅ owner 18 advanced section 12.1).
**Links:** ANC-3068, ANC-3069, ANC-3070, ANC-3074.
**Trap:** "Chera, Chola and Pandya were centralised empires with fixed borders" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Use the Muvendar to illustrate chiefdom and early-state spectrum, not territorial empire.
**Back:** *"Three crowns, three cores, shifting allegiance."*

### Flashcard ANC-3068 — Chera: Muziris (Muchiri) and the Pattanam debate
**Front:** Which Chera port is linked to Muziris, and what is the Pattanam debate?
**Definition (learn):** The Chera west-coast port associated with the pepper trade is Muziris (Muchiri). Its exact location is debated; Pattanam is a major candidate, not a settled identification.
**Breakdown:**
- Chera core: western Tamilakam and Kerala
- Port: Muziris (Muchiri); pepper trade
- Pattanam: strong but debated candidate
- Evidence: Muziris trade contract (papyrus), Periplus, Pliny, Ptolemy traditions (owner)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Muziris papyrus and Greco-Roman accounts (✅ owner 18 advanced section 15.2). Pattanam is a candidate, not proven (✅ owner 18 advanced trap).
**Links:** ANC-3067, ANC-3071, ANC-3075, ANC-3090.
**Trap:** "Muziris = Pattanam is fully settled" is wrong; say "debated candidate".
**Mains hook:** Use Muziris to show Indian Ocean connections; qualify with identification debate.
**Back:** *"Muziris on the pepper coast: the site is debated, the trade is not."*

### Flashcard ANC-3069 — Chola: Uraiyur, Puhar and Karikala
**Front:** Which Chola towns and ruler are remembered in the Sangam tradition?
**Definition (learn):** Chola power is associated with the Kaveri delta: Uraiyur and Puhar (Kaveripattinam, Poompuhar). Karikala is a remembered ruler, linked to the Kaveri embankment tradition, though literary memory varies.
**Breakdown:**
- Core: Kaveri basin
- Towns: Uraiyur; Puhar (Kaveripattinam, Poompuhar)
- Ruler: Karikala (embankment tradition)
- Caution: later imperial Chola borders must not be projected back
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Uraiyur and Puhar (✅ owner 18 basic section 2 and advanced 12.1). Karikala's embankment is a literary tradition (✅ owner 18 advanced section 12.3).
**Links:** ANC-3067, ANC-3071, ANC-3090, ANC-3074.
**Trap:** "Later imperial Chola institutions existed in the Sangam Age" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Use Puhar to show a delta port linking Chola power to maritime trade.
**Back:** *"Karikala's embankment, Puhar's market: literary Chola, not imperial Chola."*

### Flashcard ANC-3070 — Pandya: Madurai, Korkai and the pearl coast
**Front:** Which Pandya centres and ports are associated with the early Tamil period?
**Definition (learn):** The Pandya lineage is associated with Madurai and the far south, with Korkai as a pearl and fishery port on the Gulf of Mannar coast. Political reach varied and literary patronage claims are selective.
**Breakdown:**
- Core: Madurai and far south
- Port: Korkai (pearl and fishery; Gulf of Mannar routes)
- Rulers: Nedunjeliyan and Mudukudumi Peruvazhuthi traditions
- Caution: coastline and harbour shifted
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Korkai pearl and coastal zone (✅ owner 18 advanced section 12.1 and section 15.1).
**Links:** ANC-3067, ANC-3077, ANC-3090.
**Trap:** Pearls and Korkai are associated with the Pandya coast; do not give Korkai to the Cholas by default.
**Mains hook:** Use Korkai to show coastal pearl economies and trade on the Gulf of Mannar.
**Back:** *"Korkai's pearls belong to the Pandyas."*

### Flashcard ANC-3071 — Tinai: the five poetic landscapes
**Front:** What are the five tinai, with their landscape, livelihood and deity?
**Definition (learn):** Tinai is a literary-ecological grammar linking landscape, livelihood, emotion, deity and social action. It is not a deterministic revenue map.
**Breakdown:**
- Kurinji: mountains; hunting, gathering, millet; Murugan (Seyon); union
- Mullai: pastoral woodland; cattle; Mayon
- Marutam: wet plains; paddy; Indra or Ventan
- Neital: coast; fishing, salt, pearls; Varunan
- Palai: arid zone; raiding and separation; Korravai
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Five tinai table (✅ owner 18 advanced section 11).
**Links:** ANC-3063, ANC-3065, ANC-3077.
**Trap:** Tinai is not a literal map; test it against archaeology. Palai is often a seasonal poetic condition.
**Mains hook:** Use tinai to show how ecology structured the social imagination, and why literal readings are unsafe.
**Back:** *"Mountain, pasture, field, shore, dry: five tinai, five livelihoods."*

### Flashcard ANC-3072 — Velir chiefs and bardic patronage
**Front:** Who were the velir chiefs, and which poets are associated with the courts?
**Definition (learn):** Velir are regional chiefs remembered in bardic memory. Pari, Ori, Ay and Adiyaman appear in poetry. Poets such as Kapilar and Avvaiyar connect courts, gifts, war and reputation.
**Breakdown:**
- Velir chiefs: Pari, Ori, Ay, Adiyaman
- Poets: Kapilar, Avvaiyar
- Bardic fame: reputation and lineage memory
- Limit: patronage shapes whose voice survives
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Velir named chiefs and poets (✅ owner 18 advanced section 12.2).
**Links:** ANC-3065, ANC-3067, ANC-3080.
**Trap:** Poetic praise is not a court register; do not treat bards as administrators.
**Mains hook:** Use bardic patronage to show the social economy of gifts, war and fame.
**Back:** *"Pari gave, Kapilar sang: a gift economy in verse."*

### Flashcard ANC-3073 — Megalithic background: iron, burials and black-and-red ware
**Front:** Why does R.S. Sharma start the Deep South with megaliths, and what are the markers?
**Definition (learn):** The deep south before large states is reconstructed through megalithic burials: graves, iron objects and black-and-red ware. Megaliths are mainly funerary, not settlements. Megalithic is an archaeological class, not an ethnic name.
**Breakdown:**
- Beginnings: c. 1000 BC (R.S. Sharma)
- Markers: graves with urns, iron objects, black-and-red ware, grave goods
- Wide presence: 5th–1st c. BC (upland and deep south)
- Caution: burials do not reveal complete settlements or political systems
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 1000 BC beginnings; 5th–1st c. BC wide presence (✅ owner 18 basic mini-timeline).
**Links:** ANC-3061, ANC-3074, ANC-3079.
**Trap:** "All megaliths are settlements" is wrong; many are burials. "Megalithic equals Sangam" is also wrong.
**Mains hook:** Use megaliths to show that Sangam polities grew from older material cultures, not from ready-made kingdoms.
**Back:** *"Graves first, kingdoms later: megaliths before the Muvendar."*

### Flashcard ANC-3074 — Ashokan references to Cholas, Pandyas and Keralaputras
**Front:** Which southern polities are named in Ashoka's inscriptions, and what are the caveats?
**Definition (learn):** Ashokan inscriptions mention Cholas, Pandyas and Keralaputras (Satiyaputra and Keralaputra are named as neighbouring polities). They were probably still in the late megalithic phase and were not demonstrated provinces.
**Breakdown:**
- Named: Cholas, Pandyas, Keralaputras (and Satiyaputra)
- Status: neighbouring polities, not provinces
- Context: late megalithic phase
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Ashokan mention of Cholas, Pandyas, Keralaputras (✅ owner 18 basic must-know; advanced section 9 of owner 15).
**Links:** ANC-3061, ANC-3067, ANC-3073, ANC-3010.
**Trap:** Ashokan naming does not prove that these polities were Mauryan provinces.
**Mains hook:** Use the Ashokan reference to show southern neighbours within a dhamma horizon, not imperial rule.
**Back:** *"Ashoka names the crowns, but does not rule them."*

### Flashcard ANC-3075 — Arikamedu: pre-Roman settlement and the Wheeler-Begley debate
