**Trap:** "Untouchability began uniformly in Gupta times" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Use Faxian with legal texts to show exclusion, with regional and chronological limits.
**Back:** *"Faxian heard the bell of separation: regional, not universal."*

### Flashcard ANC-3145 — Nalanda: gradual monastic growth, not a Kumaragupta foundation
**Front:** What is the Gupta-era evidence for Nalanda, and why should it not be called a modern university founded by Kumaragupta?
**Definition (learn):** Nalanda has Gupta-period archaeological and patronage associations. It developed over time as a major Buddhist monastic learning centre, and later traditions link it to Gupta rulers. Claims that Kumaragupta founded it are not secure; growth had multiple patrons and phases.
**Breakdown:**
- Gupta associations: archaeology and patronage
- Growth: gradual; monastic institution
- Later traditions: linked to Gupta rulers
- Caution: "university" is shorthand; not a single founding date
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Nalanda's gradual growth (✅ owner 21 basic section 20).
**Links:** ANC-3116, ANC-3129, ANC-3161.
**Trap:** "Nalanda was founded as a modern university by Kumaragupta" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Use Nalanda to show how learning institutions grow through many patrons.
**Back:** *"Nalanda grew by patrons, not by one founder's decree."*

### Flashcard ANC-3146 — Mandsaur silk-weavers' guild inscription
**Front:** What do the Mandsaur silk-weavers' inscriptions show, and what do they not show?
**Definition (learn):** The Mandsaur silk-weavers' inscriptions record a migrant corporate group, temple patronage and occupational identity over time. They show mobility and collective action, not a universal guild economy.
**Breakdown:**
- Group: silk weavers (migrant)
- Patronage: temple
- Identity: occupational and corporate
- Limit: one group, not a universal economy
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mandsaur silk weavers (✅ owner 21 basic section 12). Date not given in the owner file (⚠️).
**Links:** ANC-3085, ANC-3054, ANC-3103.
**Trap:** Do not treat one guild record as proof of a universal guild economy.
**Mains hook:** Use the weavers to show mobility and corporate identity in the Gupta period.
**Back:** *"Weavers on the move, a temple gift: one guild, not an economy."*

### Flashcard ANC-3147 — Trap register: Gupta golden age and related claims
**Front:** Which common claims about Gupta culture and society must be replaced with bounded statements?
**Definition (learn):** The golden-age label is defensible for classical achievements but misleading when it implies universal prosperity, equality, uniform culture, exclusive patronage or pan-Indian control. The owner's trap register lists about a dozen unsafe claims.
**Breakdown:**
- "Golden for everyone": replace with beneficiaries and exclusions
- "Gold proves prosperity": test copper, agrarian and wage evidence
- "Grants were feudalism": identify rights and chronology
- "Dharmashastra described lived society": treat as prescription
- "Aryabhata invented zero": separate the five claims
- "Ajanta is Gupta": Vakataka patronage
- "All classical works are Gupta-dated": use authorship and manuscript chronology
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner trap register (✅ owner 21 advanced section 23).
**Links:** ANC-3128, ANC-3133, ANC-3138, ANC-3141, ANC-3144.
**Trap:** Use each replacement, not the original claim.
**Mains hook:** Write the Gupta age as classical in some dimensions and uneven in others.
**Back:** *"Replace the slogan with the beneficiary, the limit and the date."*

### Flashcard ANC-3148 — Smritis, legal pluralism and the ideal of law
**Front:** What do the Smriti traditions (Narada, Yajnavalkya) show about law in the Gupta age?
**Definition (learn):** Narada and Yajnavalkya and other Smriti traditions discuss property, contracts, inheritance, marriage, slavery and punishment. They show legal reasoning and normative hierarchy, not one enforced Gupta code. Guild and customary rules, local institutions and royal courts created legal pluralism.
**Breakdown:**
- Smritis: normative rules on property, contracts, inheritance, marriage, slavery, punishment
- Legal pluralism: guilds, customary rules, grants, courts
- Caution: prescription is not practice
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Narada, Yajnavalkya (✅ owner 21 advanced section 22).
**Links:** ANC-3139, ANC-3141, ANC-3142.
**Trap:** "Dharmashastra describes lived society" is wrong; triangulate.
**Mains hook:** Use the Smritis as norms to test against inscriptions and archaeology.
**Back:** *"Smritis write the norm; courts and guilds write the practice."*

### GROUP — Post-Gupta India, Harsha and Eastern India (basic owner 22; advanced owner 22)

### Flashcard ANC-3149 — Concept: post-Gupta regionalisation without a dark age
**Front:** What is the core idea of Topic 22, and how should the post-Gupta period be described?
**Definition (learn):** After Gupta paramountcy fragmented from about the sixth century, north India contained several competing centres: later Guptas, Maukharis at Kannauj, Pushyabhutis at Thanesar, Maitrakas at Valabhi, Gauda under Shashanka, Kamarupa under Bhaskaravarman and Chalukyas south of the Narmada. The age was one of regional restructuring, not darkness.
**Breakdown:**
- North: later Guptas; Maukharis; Pushyabhutis (Harsha); Maitrakas (Valabhi)
- East: Gauda (Shashanka); Kamarupa (Bhaskaravarman); Odisha and Bengal local states
- South of Narmada: Chalukyas under Pulakeshin II
- Process: grants, agrarian expansion, monasteries, changing capitals and alliances
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Regional list (✅ owner 22 basic section 11 and advanced).
**Links:** ANC-3150, ANC-3154, ANC-3155, ANC-3160, ANC-3169.
**Trap:** "Post-Gupta India was a dark age" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** "A phase of regional state formation in which Harsha was important but not all-encompassing."
**Back:** *"Gupta's fall was many rises: regional, granted, restructured."*

### Flashcard ANC-3150 — Harsha: Pushyabhuti dynasty, April 606 to 647
**Front:** When did Harsha reign, from which base, and where did his power centre move?
**Definition (learn):** Harsha of the Pushyabhuti line built a major north-Indian hegemony from 606 to 647 CE. The dynastic base was Thanesar (Sthanvesvara); he made Kanyakubja (Kannauj) his imperial capital. Kannauj's Maukhari link and central Ganga position made it the major political centre.
**Breakdown:**
- Accession: April 606 (Wikipedia) at Kannauj; reign to death in 647
- Base: Thanesar (Vardhana seat); capital: Kannauj
- Family: Prabhakaravardhana; Rajyavardhana; Rajyasri (married the Maukhari Grahavarman)
- Crisis: Malava king kills Grahavarman; Shashanka kills Rajyavardhana (Bana's narrative)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Reign April 606 to 647 (✅ Wikipedia: Harsha; ✅ owner 22 basic and advanced). Thanesar to Kannauj shift (✅ owner 22 basic section 12).
**Links:** ANC-3149, ANC-3151, ANC-3158, ANC-3167.
**Trap:** "Harsha's capital was always Kannauj" is partly wrong; Thanesar remained the dynastic base.
**Mains hook:** Argue that the shift to Kannauj was political and strategic, not Gupta succession.
**Back:** *"Thanesar begot him; Kannauj crowned him: 606 to 647."*

### Flashcard ANC-3151 — Harshacharita by Banabhatta
**Front:** Who wrote the Harshacharita, and what is it?
**Definition (learn):** The Harshacharita is a Sanskrit courtly biography (kavya) by Banabhatta, Harsha's court poet. It gives the Pushyabhuti genealogy, family crisis and early Harsha narrative. It is rhetorical and incomplete, not straightforward biography.
**Breakdown:**
- Author: Banabhatta (court poet; Wikipedia)
- Content: Pushyabhuti genealogy; Rajyasri; accession narrative
- Genre: kavya; eulogistic
- Also: Kadambari attributed to Bana (owner)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Banabhatta is the author (✅ Wikipedia: Harsha). Date not given in Wikipedia's Harsha article (⚠️).
**Links:** ANC-3150, ANC-3166, ANC-3171.
**Trap:** The Harshacharita was written by Banabhatta, not Xuanzang.
**Mains hook:** Use Bana as a court source; cross-read with Xuanzang and inscriptions.
**Back:** *"Bana wrote the Harsha story; Xuanzang watched the court."*

### Flashcard ANC-3152 — Xuanzang: the pilgrim, Nalanda and Harsha's court
**Front:** Who was Xuanzang, what did he visit, and what dates does the owner give?
**Definition (learn):** Xuanzang was a Chinese Buddhist pilgrim who stayed in India for about fifteen years, studied at Nalanda under Shilabhadra, visited Harsha's court and wrote a favourable account. The owner gives departure from China in AD 629 and return in AD 645 with Buddhist texts. Wikipedia gives no dates for his time in India.
**Breakdown:**
- Stay: about fifteen years (owner)
- Left China: 629 CE (owner)
- Nalanda: studied under Shilabhadra (owner)
- Harsha's court: favourable account, justice and generosity (✅ Wikipedia)
- Returned: 645 CE with texts (owner)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 629 and 645 (✅ owner 22 basic must-know; dates not on Wikipedia, ⚠️ for the fetched check).
**Links:** ANC-3150, ANC-3163, ANC-3166, ANC-3168.
**Trap:** Xuanzang was a Buddhist pilgrim and scholar, not a secular administrator.
**Mains hook:** Use Xuanzang to show how travel accounts reconstruct political and educational history, with pilgrim bias.
**Back:** *"Fifteen years in India, out in 629, back in 645 with texts."*

### Flashcard ANC-3153 — Aihole and Pulakeshin II versus Harsha: defeat or check?
**Front:** What does the Aihole inscription claim about Pulakeshin II and Harsha, and how do the owner files and Wikipedia word it?
**Definition (learn):** The Aihole inscription of Ravikirti celebrates Pulakeshin II's check of Harsha, conventionally near the Narmada. The owner's advanced file says it records that Pulakeshin II defeated Harsha; the owner's basic file says he claimed to have checked him. Wikipedia's lead says Harsha "was defeated" at the Battle of Narmada, but its reign section says Pulakeshin "repelled" the invasion in winter 618–619 and a treaty fixed the Narmada as the border.
**Breakdown:**
- Source: Aihole inscription (Ravikirti; rival Chalukya prashasti)
- Owner (basic): "claim to have checked"
- Owner (advanced): "defeated Harsha"
- Wikipedia: "defeated" (lead); "repelled" (reign); Narmada border
- Limit: rival eulogy, not an audited battle report
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Winter 618–619; Narmada border (✅ Wikipedia: Harsha). Aihole inscription content (✅ owner 22 basic table). Aihole is not mentioned on the fetched Wikipedia page (⚠️).
**Links:** ANC-3150, ANC-3158, ANC-3167, ANC-3164.
**Trap:** "Harsha conquered the Deccan" is wrong; Pulakeshin checked him.
**Mains hook:** Use the rival inscriptions to show that a check is not a surveyed frontier.
**Back:** *"Aihole says checked; Wikipedia says repelled at the Narmada, border drawn."*

### Flashcard ANC-3154 — Shashanka and Gauda: the eastern rival
**Front:** Who was Shashanka, where was his power, and what does Xuanzang say about him?
**Definition (learn):** Shashanka ruled Gauda from Karnasuvarna and became Harsha's major eastern rival. Xuanzang's later account accuses him of hostility to Buddhism and damage to the Bodhi tree. This is important Buddhist memory, not neutral proof of a uniform persecution policy.
**Breakdown:**
- Base: Gauda, Karnasuvarna
- Rival: Harsha
- Bana's narrative: Shashanka killed Rajyavardhana by treachery (owner)
- Xuanzang: anti-Buddhist accusation; Bodhi tree damage
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gauda under Shashanka, early seventh century (✅ owner 22 advanced section 20). Bodhi tree claim (✅ owner 22 basic section 13).
**Links:** ANC-3150, ANC-3155, ANC-3152, ANC-3171.
**Trap:** "Shashanka's Buddhist persecution is straightforward fact" is wrong; preserve Xuanzang's perspective.
**Mains hook:** Use Shashanka to show how religious memory shapes political reputation.
**Back:** *"Shashanka of Karnasuvarna: Bana's traitor, Xuanzang's persecutor, both partisan."*

### Flashcard ANC-3155 — Bhaskaravarman, Kamarupa and the Nidhanpur grant
**Front:** Who was Bhaskaravarman, and what does the Nidhanpur copper-plate show?
**Definition (learn):** Bhaskaravarman of Kamarupa allied with Harsha against Gauda. The Nidhanpur copper-plate tradition illuminates genealogy, grants and eastern political networks. The alliance did not make Kamarupa a Harshan province; Kamarupa remained a distinct polity.
**Breakdown:**
- Polity: Kamarupa (Assam)
- Alliance: with Harsha against Shashanka
- Evidence: Nidhanpur copper-plate; grants; Brahmana settlements
- Limit: alliance is not annexation
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Bhaskaravarman, Kamarupa, Nidhanpur (✅ owner 22 basic section 13 and advanced section 20). Dates not in fetched Wikipedia (⚠️).
**Links:** ANC-3154, ANC-3158, ANC-3170.
**Trap:** "Kamarupa was a Harshan province" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Use Kamarupa to show alliances as bounded political instruments.
**Back:** *"Bhaskaravarman allied, not annexed: Nidhanpur keeps the record."*

### Flashcard ANC-3156 — Harsha's religion: Shaiva, Buddhist and plural patronage
**Front:** What is Harsha's religious profile, and why is "last great Hindu emperor" unsafe?
**Definition (learn):** Harsha's seals and land grants describe him as a Shaiva (Parama-maheshvara). Xuanzang portrays him as a Buddhist, though conversion, if any, came late. Wikipedia calls him eclectic; Goyal and Sohoni consider him personally Shaiva. Court religion was plural, and "last great Hindu emperor" is an unsafe label.
**Breakdown:**
- Shaiva: seals and land grants (Parama-maheshvara)
- Buddhist: Xuanzang; Nagananda invokes Buddhist compassion
- Plural: solar and other idioms
- Caution: avoid a simple one-time conversion narrative
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Parama-maheshvara in grants and seals (✅ Wikipedia: Harsha; ✅ owner 22 advanced section 17).
**Links:** ANC-3157, ANC-3159, ANC-3167, ANC-3168.
**Trap:** "Harsha converted simply from Shaivism to Buddhism" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Use plural patronage to show political religion, not religious uniformity.
**Back:** *"Shaiva on the seal, Buddhist in Xuanzang's eye: plural court."*

### Flashcard ANC-3157 — Harsha's works: Ratnavali, Priyadarsika and Nagananda
**Front:** Which plays are traditionally credited to Harsha, and what is the attribution debate?
**Definition (learn):** Harsha is traditionally credited with the plays Ratnavali, Priyadarsika and Nagananda. Wikipedia says Harsha is widely believed to be the author. Mammata held that the court poet Dhavaka wrote them on commission; Wendy Doniger is persuaded that Harsha wrote them.
**Breakdown:**
- Plays: Ratnavali; Priyadarsika; Nagananda
- Nagananda: story of the Bodhisattva Jimutavahana; opens with a verse honouring the Buddha (✅ Wikipedia)
- Attribution: Harsha (traditional); Dhavaka (Mammata)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Attribution per Wikipedia (✅ Wikipedia: Harsha).
**Links:** ANC-3151, ANC-3156, ANC-3169.
**Trap:** Royal authorship is not equally secure in every detail; do not state it as proven.
**Mains hook:** Use the attribution debate to show how court literature is evidence for patronage.
**Back:** *"Harsha's three plays: Harsha, Dhavaka, or the court: debated."*

### Flashcard ANC-3158 — Harsha's graded authority: core, allies, tributaries and campaign zones
**Front:** What graded categories describe Harsha's power, and how far did it reach?
**Definition (learn):** Harsha's polity was a regional hegemony with a core, subordinate rulers and alliances, not a restored Gupta empire. Zones: core (Thanesar-Kannauj and middle and upper Ganga); strong influence (linked by campaigns and itinerant kingship); allies (Kamarupa); tributary rulers (samantas); campaign horizon; and the Narmada as the southern limit.
**Breakdown:**
- Core: Thanesar-Kannauj; middle and upper Ganga
- Influence: campaigns, court attendance, itinerant kingship
- Allies: Kamarupa and changing partners
- Tributaries: samantas and chiefs
- Campaign passage: not province
- Southern limit: Narmada (Pulakeshin II)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Table (✅ owner 22 basic section 14; advanced section 23).
**Links:** ANC-3150, ANC-3153, ANC-3155, ANC-3171.
**Trap:** "Harsha ruled all northern India uniformly" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Argue that Harsha's power was personal, itinerant and alliance-based.
**Back:** *"Core, allies, samantas, Narmada: Harsha's reach in layers."*

### Flashcard ANC-3159 — The Kannauj assembly and the Prayaga distribution
**Front:** What were Harsha's Kannauj assembly and the Prayaga distribution, and what kind of event were they?
**Definition (learn):** Xuanzang describes a great religious assembly at Kannauj emphasising Mahayana Buddhism and royal display. The quinquennial Prayaga gathering and a large charitable distribution are known chiefly through Xuanzang. They were religious debate, patronage and political theatre, not a modern secular parliament.
**Breakdown:**
- Kannauj assembly: Mahayana emphasis; royal display; debate
- Prayaga: quinquennial gathering; charitable distribution
- Source: Xuanzang (pilgrim narrative)
- Caution: scale and detail need pilgrim caution
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Kannauj assembly and Prayaga (✅ owner 22 advanced section 17).
**Links:** ANC-3152, ANC-3156, ANC-3168.
**Trap:** "Assemblies prove modern tolerance or secularism" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Use assemblies to show religious patronage as political theatre.
**Back:** *"Kannauj's assembly: debate and display, not a parliament."*

### Flashcard ANC-3160 — Tripartite struggle: Pratiharas, Palas and Rashtrakutas for Kannauj
**Front:** What was the tripartite struggle, and in which order do the three powers appear?
**Definition (learn):** In the eighth and ninth centuries, Gurjara-Pratiharas, Palas and Rashtrakutas competed for Kannauj, a contest called the tripartite struggle. Pratihara emergence precedes or overlaps the Pala foundation; Palas begin with Gopala in the mid-eighth century; Imperial Cholas rise from the ninth century; Pallavas have an earlier arc.
**Breakdown:**
- Three contenders: Gurjara-Pratiharas; Palas; Rashtrakutas
- Prize: Kannauj
- Pala start: Gopala, mid-eighth century
- Imperial Chola rise: from the ninth century (owner)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mid-8th c. Gopala; ninth-century Chola rise (✅ owner 22 advanced section 22).
**Links:** ANC-3149, ANC-3161, ANC-3164, ANC-3165.
**Trap:** Do not mix the Pallava, Pratihara, Pala and Chola sequence (see ANC-3164).
**Mains hook:** Use the tripartite contest to show Kannauj's continuing prestige after Harsha.
**Back:** *"Three powers, one city: Pratihara, Pala, Rashtrakuta for Kannauj."*

### Flashcard ANC-3161 — Gopala and the Pala Buddhist centres
**Front:** Who founded the Pala line, and which monastic centres did the Pala phase support?
**Definition (learn):** Gopala emerged in Bengal in the mid-eighth century. "Election" language is a later inscriptional tradition and should not be modernised. Dharmapala and Devapala expanded Pala influence across Bengal and Bihar and took part in Kannauj politics. The Pala phase supported Nalanda, Vikramashila, Odantapuri and Somapura monastic networks.
**Breakdown:**
- Gopala: mid-eighth century, Bengal
- Dharmapala and Devapala: expansion; Kannauj politics
- Centres: Nalanda; Vikramashila; Odantapuri; Somapura
- Circulation: Mahayana and Vajrayana learning toward Nepal and Tibet
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gopala; monastic networks (✅ owner 22 advanced section 21).
**Links:** ANC-3145, ANC-3160, ANC-3163.
**Trap:** "Pala patronage made Bengal uniformly Buddhist" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Use the Pala centres to show Buddhism's regional networks and their limits.
**Back:** *"Gopala begins, Dharmapala spreads: Nalanda, Vikramashila, Odantapuri, Somapura."*

### Flashcard ANC-3162 — Sena power in later Bengal
**Front:** What characterises Sena power in Bengal, and how does it differ from the Pala phase?
**Definition (learn):** Sena power followed in parts of Bengal in the later early-medieval centuries. Brahmanical court and land-grant patronage became prominent, but Buddhist institutions and regional plurality did not vanish instantly. Detailed later-medieval Bengal lies outside this topic.
**Breakdown:**
- Sena: later early-medieval Bengal
- Patronage: Brahmanical court and grants
- Continuity: Buddhist institutions persisted
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sena later phase (✅ owner 22 advanced section 21).
**Links:** ANC-3161, ANC-3163.
**Trap:** "Sena replaced Pala Buddhism in one day" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Use the Pala-to-Sena transition to show gradual religious change.
**Back:** *"Sena after Pala: Brahmanical grants, Buddhist memory, no sudden break."*

### Flashcard ANC-3163 — Pala Buddhism (2020 GS-I Q2): the enumeration route
**Front:** What does the 2020 GS-I Q2 Mains demand ask, and how should the answer be organised?
**Definition (learn):** The 2020 GS-I Q2 asks about the Pala period as most significant in the history of Buddhism in India (10 marks, 150 words; enumerate). The route: Nalanda, Vikramashila, Odantapuri and Somapura; Mahayana and Vajrayana; Atisha and trans-Himalayan networks; art and manuscripts; then qualify against Ashoka and early Buddhism.
**Breakdown:**
- Institutions: Nalanda, Vikramashila, Odantapuri, Somapura
- Doctrine: Mahayana, Vajrayana
- Networks: Atisha; Nepal and Tibet
- Art and manuscripts
- Qualification: Ashokan and early Buddhism remain central; Pala patronage is regional
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2020 GS-I Q2 (✅ owner 22 advanced 2018–2023 block; ✅ owner 22 basic section 26).
**Links:** ANC-3161, ANC-3160, ANC-3145.
**Trap:** Do not claim the Pala phase was the most significant in all senses without qualification.
**Mains hook:** Enumerate the Pala contributions, then qualify with regional and institutional limits.
**Back:** *"Pala enumerated: four monasteries, two schools, one trans-Himalayan road."*

### Flashcard ANC-3164 — Chronology ordering: Pallava, Pratihara, Pala, Chola (2020 Q24)
**Front:** What is the owner's chronological order for the 2020 Q24 demand?
**Definition (learn):** The 2020 Q24 asks for the chronological order of Pratihara, Pallava, Chola and Pala dynasties. The owner's inferred order is Pallava, then Gurjara-Pratihara, then Pala, then Imperial Chola. The official key is not held locally.
**Breakdown:**
- Pallava: earlier major arc
- Gurjara-Pratihara: eighth to ninth century
- Pala: from the mid-eighth century (Gopala)
- Imperial Chola: from the ninth century
- Route: inferred (⚠️), key unavailable
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Order inferred by the owner (✅ owner 22 advanced section 22 and section 26).
**Links:** ANC-3160, ANC-3161, ANC-3165.
**Trap:** Do not use the Pala founding date to place the Pratiharas after the Palas.
**Mains hook:** Use the sequence to show the tripartite contest.
**Back:** *"Pallava, Pratihara, Pala, Chola: the order in the tripartite age."*

### Flashcard ANC-3165 — Places: Bhilsa, Dwarasamudra, Girinagar and Sthanesvara (2020 Q97)
**Front:** Which modern states match Bhilsa, Dwarasamudra, Girinagar and Sthanesvara, per the owner's route?
**Definition (learn):** The 2020 Q97 demand matches four historical places to states: Bhilsa (Vidisha, Madhya Pradesh); Dwarasamudra (Hoysala, Halebidu, Karnataka); Girinagar (Girnar or Junagadh, Gujarat); and Sthanesvara (Thanesar, Haryana). The owner records no option letter.
**Breakdown:**
- Bhilsa: Vidisha, Madhya Pradesh
- Dwarasamudra: Hoysala, Halebidu, Karnataka
- Girinagar: Girnar or Junagadh, Gujarat
- Sthanesvara: Thanesar, Haryana
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2020 Prelims GS-I Q97 (✅ owner 22 2018–2023 block; key unavailable locally).
**Links:** ANC-3150, ANC-3160, ANC-3164.
**Trap:** Keep place names separate: Bhilsa is not Bhopal.
**Mains hook:** Not a Mains item; use for place identification.
**Back:** *"Vidisha, Halebidu, Girnar, Thanesar: four places, four states."*

### Flashcard ANC-3166 — Northern kingdoms after the Gupta decline (2021 Q34)
**Front:** Which northern kingdoms does the 2021 Q34 demand expect after the Gupta decline?
**Definition (learn):** The 2021 Q34 asks about the kingdoms of northern India after Gupta decline. The owner's route includes the Later Guptas, Maukharis, Pushyabhutis, Maitrakas and Gauda. Exclude southern houses when the option asks about the north.
**Breakdown:**
- Later Guptas: Magadha and eastern India
- Maukharis: Kannauj
- Pushyabhutis: Thanesar, Harsha
- Maitrakas: Valabhi
- Gauda: Shashanka
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2021 Prelims GS-I Q34 (✅ owner 22 2018–2023 block; key unavailable locally).
**Links:** ANC-3149, ANC-3150, ANC-3154, ANC-3151.
**Trap:** Southern houses such as the Chalukyas do not belong to the "north" option.
**Mains hook:** Use the northern list to map the post-Gupta regional structure.
**Back:** *"Later Guptas, Maukharis, Pushyabhutis, Maitrakas, Gauda: the north only."*

### Flashcard ANC-3167 — Trap: Harsha as "last great Hindu emperor" and as pan-Indian
**Front:** Why are "last great Hindu emperor" and "pan-Indian ruler like Ashoka" unsafe descriptions of Harsha?
**Definition (learn):** Harsha's influence was large in north India but bounded. His religious patronage included Buddhism, his court was plural, and he was checked at the Narmada. He was not a pan-Indian emperor like Ashoka.
**Breakdown:**
- Religion: Shaiva and Buddhist patronage
- Territory: north India, bounded by Narmada
- Comparison: not Ashoka's pan-Indian reach
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Narmada limit (✅ Wikipedia: Harsha); plural religion (✅ Wikipedia: Harsha).
**Links:** ANC-3156, ANC-3153, ANC-3158.
**Trap:** Keep the qualifiers: north India, plural court, bounded reach.
**Mains hook:** Argue that Harsha was a regional pivot, not a solitary pan-Indian emperor.
**Back:** *"Not last Hindu, not all-India: bounded hegemony at Kannauj."*

### Flashcard ANC-3168 — Trap: Harsha as founder of Nalanda
**Front:** Why is "Harsha founded Nalanda university" wrong, and what did Xuanzang do there?
**Definition (learn):** Nalanda developed across Gupta, Harsha and Pala phases. Harsha was a patron, not its sole founder. Xuanzang studied there under Shilabhadra; "university" is convenient shorthand for a monastic learning centre.
**Breakdown:**
- Phases: Gupta, Harsha, Pala
- Harsha: patron
- Xuanzang: studied under Shilabhadra
- Caution: numerical claims for residents or buildings vary
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Nalanda phases (✅ owner 22 basic section 18).
**Links:** ANC-3145, ANC-3152, ANC-3161.
**Trap:** Nalanda was not founded by Harsha in one act.
**Mains hook:** Use Nalanda to show patronage across several dynasties.
**Back:** *"Harsha patronised Nalanda; he did not found it."*

### Flashcard ANC-3169 — Trap: post-Harsha "dark age" and immediate anarchy
**Front:** Why are "post-Gupta dark age" and "Harsha's death caused immediate anarchy" unsafe?
**Definition (learn):** Harsha died in 647 without a secure dynastic successor. His death did not cause instantaneous anarchy everywhere. Kannauj remained prestigious and regional powers continued. The post-Harsha change was regional and gradual.
**Breakdown:**
- Death: 647 CE; no secure successor
- Effect: regional, not universal
- Continuity: Kannauj's prestige; regional polities
- Later: tripartite struggle (ANC-3160)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Death in 647 (✅ Wikipedia: Harsha; ✅ owner 22).
**Links:** ANC-3149, ANC-3160, ANC-3166.
**Trap:** "Dark age" is an unsafe label for this period.
**Mains hook:** Argue that post-Harsha change was reconfiguration, not darkness.
**Back:** *"Harsha dies in 647; the north reconfigures, it does not sleep."*

### Flashcard ANC-3170 — Land grants and eastern state formation: Odisha and Bengal
**Front:** How did land grants and Brahmana settlements shape eastern India, especially Odisha and Bengal?
**Definition (learn):** Eastern India shows local dynasties, origin myths, Brahmana settlements, grants and agrarian expansion. Grants formed part of larger social and cultural processes. "Spread of civilisation" must not erase local communities or their agency.
**Breakdown:**
- Odisha: local dynasties; inscriptions; religious centres; grants
- Bengal: new states; elephants, horses and boats in military camps
- Assam: integration into Sanskritic and agrarian processes
- Method: state-society formation
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Bengal military camps with elephants, horses, boats (✅ owner 22 basic section 1). Odisha local states (✅ owner 22 advanced section 1).
**Links:** ANC-3139, ANC-3155, ANC-3149.
**Trap:** "Eastern India was outside early historic change" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Argue that eastern India was a zone of state-society formation, not an appendix.
**Back:** *"Grants, camps and Brahmanas: the east forms its own states."*

### Flashcard ANC-3171 — Harsha's administration: samantas, bhuktis and officers
**Front:** Which administrative terms are attested for Harsha's polity, and what do they show?
**Definition (learn):** Inscriptions mention samantas or mahasamantas (subordinate rulers or elites), bhukti, vishaya, pathaka and locality terms, and kumaramatya and other officers. The evidence does not reveal one uniform bureaucracy; revenue came from produce shares, dues, tolls and grants.
**Breakdown:**
- Samanta / mahasamanta: subordinate or regional ruler
- Bhukti / vishaya / pathaka: provincial, district and local
- Kumaramatya and other officers: high administrative service
- Revenue: produce shares, dues, tolls, grants
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Admin terms (✅ owner 22 basic section 15).
**Links:** ANC-3119, ANC-3158, ANC-3170.
**Trap:** Terms vary regionally; do not assume a Mauryan hierarchy.
**Mains hook:** Use the admin terms to show intermediaries and graded authority.
**Back:** *"Samanta, bhukti, kumaramatya: the intermediaries of Harsha."*

### Flashcard ANC-3172 — Harsha's death and the post-Harsha transition
**Front:** When and how did Harsha's reign end, and what remained after it?
**Definition (learn):** Harsha died in 647 CE without a secure dynastic successor. Kannauj remained prestigious, regional powers continued, and by the eighth and ninth centuries Gurjara-Pratiharas, Palas and Rashtrakutas competed for it. Harsha's power was personal and alliance-based; he did not restore Gupta territorial scale or bureaucracy.
**Breakdown:**
- Death: 647 CE (✅ Wikipedia: Harsha)
- Successor: none secure (owner)
- After: tripartite contest for Kannauj
- Nature: personal, itinerant, alliance-based
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 647 CE death (✅ Wikipedia: Harsha); tripartite contest (✅ owner 22 advanced section 22).
**Links:** ANC-3150, ANC-3160, ANC-3169, ANC-3158.
**Trap:** Harsha did not restore the Gupta Empire.
**Mains hook:** Use Harsha as a case of regionalised kingship in the post-Gupta world.
**Back:** *"647: no heir secured, Kannauj still prized, the north in contest."*

## ANCIENT INDIAN HISTORY — Part 4: Peninsular Dynasties, Philosophy, Cultural Interaction and the Cholas

### GROUP — Peninsular India: Pallavas, Chalukyas & Brahmanization (basic 23; advanced 23)

### Flashcard ANC-4001 — Peninsular state formation: the core analytic frame (c. AD 550–750)
**Front:** What is the core analytic frame for Pallava–Chalukya history, and what must it not be reduced to?
**Definition (learn):** Early medieval peninsular history read as the formation of new regional states, expansion of rural settlement, and Brahmanization. It is not a simple sequence of dynastic wars. (✅ owner 23)
**Breakdown:**
- Political: Pallavas of Kanchi vs Chalukyas of Badami; Pandyas a weaker third participant.
- Material: land grants (brahmadeya, devadana) plus tanks, canals and settlement produce new revenue and ritual centres.
- Cultural: temples, caves and bilingual inscriptions act as political communication; Bhakti and vernacular devotion grow.
- Interpretive: feudal, segmentary, integrative and peasantization models compete (see ANC-4030).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner frame is AD 550–750 for the early medieval south (advanced 23). Pulakeshin II c. 609–642; Narasimhavarman I c. 630–668 (✅ owner 23). Tungabhadra often marked the Deccan/deep-south boundary (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4002, ANC-4003, ANC-4004, ANC-4006, ANC-4019, ANC-4022, ANC-4030, ANC-4035.
**Trap:** "Early medieval" names a cluster of changes that did not occur at one time across the peninsula (✅ owner 23 §9.1). Western Deccan, Andhra and Tamil regions moved at different rates.
**Mains hook:** Discuss how warfare, land grants and temple patronage together built peninsular states. Qualified verdict: political and social transformation comes before artistic achievement, and the sources are eulogistic.
**Back:** *"War, grant, temple: three rails of one peninsular state."*

### Flashcard ANC-4002 — Pallavas of Kanchi
**Front:** Who were the Pallavas of Kanchi, and which monuments and features identify them?
**Definition (learn):** A south Indian dynasty centred at Kanchi (Kanchipuram), the main rival of the Badami Chalukyas through the 6th–8th centuries AD. (✅ owner 23)
**Breakdown:**
- Capital: Kanchi. Coastal monuments at Mahabalipuram (Mamallapuram).
- Phases: rock-cut (Mahendravarman I), monolithic rathas and reliefs (Narasimhavarman I), structural temples (Rajasimha).
- Writing: Prakrit then Sanskrit charters; Sanskrit *prashasti* with Tamil documentary sections; Pallava Grantha script.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mahabalipuram is a UNESCO site inscribed 1984 (✅ UNESCO). Pallava rule at Mahabalipuram is tied to the 6th–9th centuries CE (✅ UNESCO). Owner gives 6th–8th centuries for the Pallava–Chalukya struggle (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4003, ANC-4014, ANC-4015, ANC-4016, ANC-4017, ANC-4025, ANC-4036.
**Trap:** Mahabalipuram and Kanchi are Pallava. Aihole, Badami and Pattadakal are Chalukya (see ANC-4036).
**Mains hook:** Use Pallava monuments as evidence of royal ideology, craft networks and bilingual administration, not art-style chronology alone.
**Back:** *"Kanchi: Kailasanatha, Shore Temple, Mamalla rathas."*

### Flashcard ANC-4003 — Chalukyas of Badami (Vatapi)
**Front:** Who were the Chalukyas of Badami, where did they rule from, and how did their power end?
**Definition (learn):** A western Deccan dynasty with its capital at Badami (ancient Vatapi). It emerged in the western Deccan, and its later genealogies were legitimising devices, not literal migration records. (✅ owner 23 §10.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Centre: Badami/Vatapi with rock-cut caves.
- Experimental temple landscape: Aihole, Badami, Pattadakal (see ANC-4019).
- Expansion under Pulakeshin II; recovery under Vikramaditya I; last ruler Kirtivarman II.
- Fall to the Rashtrakutas c. 753 (✅ owner 23 table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Badami Chalukya rule ends c. 753 with Rashtrakuta displacement (✅ owner 23). Eastern Chalukya branch at Vengi continues (see ANC-4032).
**Links:** ANC-4004, ANC-4010, ANC-4013, ANC-4019, ANC-4032, ANC-4035.
**Trap:** The Badami Chalukyas are not the Eastern Chalukyas. The Vengi branch survives Badami's fall.
**Mains hook:** Argue that Chalukya temple-building was a political language of sovereignty, not only artistic display. The Aihole and Pattadakal evidence supports this, but monuments cannot map the full extent of control.
**Back:** *"Vatapi's Chalukyas: Aihole the lab, Badami the seat, Pattadakal the crown."*

### Flashcard ANC-4004 — Pulakeshin II (c. 609–642)
**Front:** What did Pulakeshin II achieve, and what do the sources say about his wars with Harsha and the Pallavas?
**Definition (learn):** The most celebrated Badami Chalukya king. He expanded across the Deccan, checked Harsha, and fought the Pallavas, who later captured his capital. (✅ owner 23)
**Breakdown:**
- Expansion: Deccan and eastern expansion; Eastern Chalukya (Vengi) branch from his eastern campaigns (✅ owner 23 adv).
- Harsha: checked at the Narmada (✅ owner 23; ✅ Wikipedia: Pulakesin II).
- Pallava war: early clashes inconclusive; later Pallava capture of Vatapi (see ANC-4006).
- Sources: the Aihole praśasti (ANC-4005).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Reign c. 609–642 (✅ owner 23; ✅ Wikipedia). Accession year debated, c. 609–611 (✅ Wikipedia). Harsha defeat dating is disputed: c. 612 or earlier (Ramesh, Nilakanta Sastri), or winter 618–619 (Bapat, Sohoni), citing a 619 Bijapur-Mumbai grant (✅ Wikipedia: Pulakesin II). ⚠️ Owner 23 says the Aihole inscription is dated to the mid-630s; the Wikipedia source does not support a 630s date for the Harsha victory.
**Links:** ANC-4005, ANC-4006, ANC-4035, ANC-4036.
**Trap:** Pulakeshin II checked Harsha. He did not defeat the Pallavas permanently. His death in the Pallava war is "probably" the case, not certain (✅ Wikipedia: Pulakesin II).
**Mains hook:** Pulakeshin II shows how a Deccan court built prestige through eulogy. The Harsha date conflict shows why chronology needs source triangulation.
**Back:** *"Pulakeshin II: Harsha halted, Vatapi lost, Aihole praised."*

### Flashcard ANC-4005 — Ravikirti's Aihole inscription (praśasti)
**Front:** What is the Aihole inscription, who composed it, and why must it be read as political praise?
**Definition (learn):** A Sanskrit court eulogy (praśasti) composed by the court poet Ravikirti, engraved on the wall of the Meguti temple at Aihole. It records Pulakeshin II's victories and the construction of a Jinendra temple. (✅ owner 23; ✅ Wikipedia: Pulakesin II)
**Breakdown:**
- Author: Ravikirti, described as a Jain court poet.
- Location: Meguti Jain temple, Aihole.
- Content: overthrow of Mangalesha; defeat of Appayika and the Kadambas; submission of the Alupas, Gangas, Latas, Malavas and Gurjaras; Harsha's defeat at the Narmada; Kosala, Kalinga and Pishtapura; clashes with the Pallavas; a realm bounded by oceans on three sides (✅ Wikipedia: Pulakesin II).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Owner 23 says "dated to the mid-630s". The fetched Wikipedia text gives no date for the inscription; the 634 CE figure appears only in an image caption (✅ Wikipedia: Pulakesin II). Treat the date as unresolved in exam answers.
**Links:** ANC-4004, ANC-4031, ANC-4036.
**Trap:** Aihole praśasti = Chalukya/Pulakeshin II/Ravikirti, not Pallava. It is eulogistic, not a neutral biography.
**Mains hook:** Use the Aihole praśasti as evidence of royal self-presentation, then state its limits: victories are boasts, not proofs of administered territory.
**Back:** *"Ravikirti praises, Meguti records, Harsha's elephants fall."*

### Flashcard ANC-4006 — Narasimhavarman I Mamalla and Vatapikonda
**Front:** Who was Narasimhavarman I, and what did his reign achieve against the Chalukyas and at Mamallapuram?
**Definition (learn):** The Pallava ruler (c. 630–668) who reversed Pallava fortunes. His titles were Mamalla and Vatapikonda ("conqueror of Vatapi"). (✅ owner 23)
**Breakdown:**
- Counter-attack on Pulakeshin II; capture of Vatapi (Badami) c. 642–643, led by general Paranjoti (✅ Wikipedia: Pulakesin II).
- Mamallapuram phase: monolithic rathas and reliefs (see ANC-4015).
- Pallava titles like Vatapikonda preserve his counter-victory (✅ owner 23 §10.3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 630–668 (✅ owner 23). Vatapi captured c. 642 (✅ owner 23; ✅ Wikipedia: c. 642–643).
**Links:** ANC-4004, ANC-4007, ANC-4015, ANC-4035, ANC-4037.
**Trap:** Narasimhavarman I = Mamalla and Vatapikonda. Rajasimha is Narasimhavarman II, not I (see ANC-4016). A campaign success does not mean permanent administration of the western Deccan.
**Mains hook:** The Vatapi victory is evidence of Pallava prestige and military capacity. Permanence and administration need separate evidence.
**Back:** *"Mamalla took Vatapi; Mamallapuram took his name."*

### Flashcard ANC-4007 — Mahendravarman I: the artist-king
**Front:** Who was Mahendravarman I, and which titles and works are linked to him?
**Definition (learn):** An early 7th-century Pallava ruler associated with the first major rock-cut patronage, the Sanskrit farce Mattavilasa-prahasana, and a set of royal titles. (✅ owner 23)
**Breakdown:**
- Titles: Mattavilasa, Vichitrachitta, Gunabhara (✅ owner 23; PYQ 2025 Prelims Q14).
- Works: rock-cut caves (Mandagapattu, Mamandur) (see ANC-4017); the farce (see ANC-4008).
- Conflict: first major Pulakeshin II conflict; early clashes at Pullalur inconclusive (✅ Wikipedia: Pulakesin II).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Early 7th century (✅ owner 23). Predecessor Simhavishnu (ANC-4009).
**Links:** ANC-4008, ANC-4009, ANC-4017, ANC-4037.
**Trap:** Mahendravarman I ≠ Narasimhavarman I. Mahendravarman I gave the early cave phase; Shore Temple and Kailasanatha belong to the later structural phase.
**Mains hook:** Mahendravarman I's work shows Sanskrit court writing and rock-cut patronage together, which supports a bilingual, multi-medium Pallava culture.
**Back:** *"Mahendra: Mattavilasa, Vichitra, Guna; caves first."*

### Flashcard ANC-4008 — Mattavilasa-prahasana (Sanskrit farce)
**Front:** What is the Mattavilasa-prahasana, who is it linked to, and what does it reveal?
**Definition (learn):** A 7th-century Sanskrit farce (prahasana) associated with Mahendravarman I. It satirises Buddhists, Kapalikas and Pashupatas and so reveals inter-religious competition. (✅ owner 23)
**Breakdown:**
- Genre: satirical court farce.
- Targets: Buddhists, Kapalikas, Pashupatas (✅ owner 23).
- Evidence type: a literary source, not a census of religions.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 7th century (✅ owner 23 adv). Linked to Mahendravarman I (see ANC-4007).
**Links:** ANC-4007, ANC-4026, ANC-4031.
**Trap:** A satirical farce is not a census of religious communities. Do not treat its portrayals as neutral description.
**Mains hook:** Use the farce as evidence of competition among sects in Pallava courts; add that comedy is a genre with its own rules.
**Back:** *"Mattavilasa mocks three sects: Buddhist, Kapalika, Pashupata."*

### Flashcard ANC-4009 — Simhavishnu (late 6th century)
**Front:** Who was Simhavishnu, and why does he matter for the Pallava chronology?
**Definition (learn):** A late 6th-century Pallava ruler who consolidated Pallava power southward and was the predecessor of Mahendravarman I. (✅ owner 23)
**Breakdown:**
- Phase: late 6th century consolidation.
- Relation: predecessor of Mahendravarman I (✅ owner 23 table). ⚠️ Common textbooks also call him his father; the owner does not state this.
- Do not confuse with Simhavarman, an earlier figure the 2025 PYQ route asks students to distinguish (✅ owner 23 §19.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Late 6th century (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4007, ANC-4037.
**Trap:** Simhavishnu ≠ Simhavarman ≠ Narasimhavarman I. The 2025 PYQ route separates them (✅ owner 23 §19.1).
**Mains hook:** Use Simhavishnu as the start of the southward Pallava consolidation before the Mahendravarman I era.
**Back:** *"Simhavishnu opened the south; Mahendra held the stage."*

### Flashcard ANC-4010 — Vatapi's founders: Pulakeshin I, Kirtivarman I, Mangalesha
**Front:** Which Badami Chalukya rulers established Vatapi before Pulakeshin II?
**Definition (learn):** The first generations of Badami Chalukya rule that established Vatapi as capital and consolidated power before the expansion of Pulakeshin II. (✅ owner 23 §10.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Pulakeshin I: establishment at Vatapi.
- Kirtivarman I: consolidation.
- Mangalesha: consolidation before Pulakeshin II.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Dates are approximate; the owner gives only the phase, not exact years (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4003, ANC-4004.
**Trap:** Pulakeshin I is not Pulakeshin II. The "II" is the famous eulogised ruler.
**Mains hook:** The founders' consolidation explains why Badami could project power after Pulakeshin II.
**Back:** *"Vatapi founders: Pulakeshin I, Kirtivarman, Mangalesha."*

### Flashcard ANC-4011 — Vikramaditya I: recovery after Vatapi
**Front:** What did Vikramaditya I do for the Chalukyas after the fall of Vatapi?
**Definition (learn):** The Badami Chalukya ruler who led recovery after Vatapi's fall. (✅ owner 23 §10.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Phase: recovery after Vatapi's fall.
- Pattern: renewed attacks and counter-attacks in the Pallava war (see ANC-4035).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** The owner gives no exact years for Vikramaditya I in the basic file.
**Links:** ANC-4006, ANC-4012, ANC-4035.
**Trap:** Vikramaditya I (recovery) ≠ Vikramaditya II (Kanchi campaigns, see ANC-4012).
**Mains hook:** Use the Vikramaditya sequence to show that rivalry was cyclical, not a single decisive conquest.
**Back:** *"Vikramaditya I rebuilt after Vatapi fell."*

### Flashcard ANC-4012 — Vikramaditya II: Kanchi campaigns and Pattadakal
**Front:** What campaigns and patronage are linked to Vikramaditya II?
**Definition (learn):** An early–mid 8th-century Badami Chalukya ruler who campaigned against Kanchi and whose victories are commemorated at Pattadakal. (✅ owner 23)
**Breakdown:**
- Campaigns against Kanchi (✅ owner 23 table).
- Pattadakal: the Virupaksha temple was built after his Pallava campaigns (✅ Wikipedia: Pattadakal).
- Phase range in advanced owner: AD 733–745 (✅ owner 23 adv).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Early–mid 8th century (✅ owner 23). Virupaksha c. 740 (✅ Wikipedia: Pattadakal).
**Links:** ANC-4011, ANC-4018, ANC-4035.
**Trap:** Chalukya records of Kanchi campaigns encode prestige. They do not prove durable annexation of Kanchi (✅ owner 23 §10.3).
**Mains hook:** Pattadakal's Virupaksha commemorates a victory. Use it to show temple-building as political commemoration.
**Back:** *"Vikramaditya II: Kanchi campaigns, Virupaksha raised."*

### Flashcard ANC-4013 — Kirtivarman II and the Rashtrakuta takeover (c. 753)
**Front:** Who was the last Badami Chalukya ruler, and what event marks the end of Badami rule?
**Definition (learn):** Kirtivarman II was the last Badami Chalukya ruler (to c. 753). The Rashtrakuta displacement of the Badami line is the transition boundary of this owner. (✅ owner 23)
**Breakdown:**
- Ruler: Kirtivarman II.
- Event: Rashtrakuta displacement c. 753 (✅ owner 23 table; §18 transition).
- Consequence: Eastern Chalukyas continue in Vengi (see ANC-4032).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 753 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4003, ANC-4032, ANC-4035.
**Trap:** Badami ends; the Chalukya line does not end altogether. The Vengi branch survives.
**Mains hook:** The Rashtrakuta takeover shows that political transition was regional reconfiguration, not the simultaneous disappearance of institutions (✅ owner 23 §18).
**Back:** *"Kirtivarman II, Badami ends; Vengi carries on."*

### Flashcard ANC-4014 — Mahabalipuram (Mamallapuram): the Pallava coastal complex
**Front:** What is Mahabalipuram, which dynasty built it, and what does it contain?
**Definition (learn):** A coastal Pallava monument complex with rock-cut and structural temples, rathas and reliefs. It is a UNESCO World Heritage Site inscribed in 1984. (✅ UNESCO)
**Breakdown:**
- Cave temples (mandapas): Konerimandapa, Mahishamardhini, Varahamandapa (✅ UNESCO).
- Monolithic rathas and reliefs (ANC-4015).
- Structural temple: Shore Temple (ANC-4016).
- Excavated structures around the Shore Temple (✅ UNESCO).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** UNESCO inscription 1984; criteria i, ii, iii, vi (✅ UNESCO). Narasimhavarman I for caves, rathas and reliefs; Rajasimha for structural temples (✅ UNESCO).
**Links:** ANC-4002, ANC-4015, ANC-4016, ANC-4036.
**Trap:** Mahabalipuram is a Pallava centre, not a Chalukya one. The safe current-affairs link is coastal weathering, conservation and visitor management (⚠️ owner 23 §6; verify any ASI intervention).
**Mains hook:** Read Mahabalipuram as a sacred geography of royal power linked to a coastal maritime setting (⚠️ owner 23 §15). Monuments are not a freight ledger.
**Back:** *"Mahabalipuram: Pallava coast, UNESCO 1984."*

### Flashcard ANC-4015 — Mamalla rathas and great reliefs
**Front:** What are the Mamallapuram rathas and the great relief, and why is the Pandava name a trap?
**Definition (learn):** Five monolithic temples, each cut from a single boulder, named "rathas" (chariots) because of their shape. They and the great reliefs date to the Mamalla phase of Narasimhavarman I. (✅ UNESCO; ✅ owner 23)
**Breakdown:**
- Five monolithic rathas, carved with divinities and royal portraits (✅ UNESCO).
- Great reliefs: Arjuna's Penance (the Descent of the Ganges) and Govardhanadhari (✅ UNESCO).
- Naming: "rathas" and "Pandava" labels are modern conventional names, not proof of Pandava construction (✅ owner 23 §17.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mamalla phase, c. 630–668 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4006, ANC-4014, ANC-4016.
**Trap:** Rathas were not built by the Pandavas. Do not use the modern name as evidence of construction.
**Mains hook:** Rock-cut monuments show the Pallava capacity to fund and manage large projects. Royal attribution does not erase artisans or multiple patronage stages.
**Back:** *"Five rathas, not Pandava palaces; Arjuna's Penance on the rock."*

### Flashcard ANC-4016 — Shore Temple, Kailasanatha and Rajasimha (Narasimhavarman II)
**Front:** Which structural Pallava temples are associated with Rajasimha, and what is the name trap?
**Definition (learn):** Rajasimha (Narasimhavarman II) is the ruler of the structural phase. The Shore Temple at Mahabalipuram is the culmination of structural temple building under him, and the Kailasanatha at Kanchi is linked to this phase. (✅ UNESCO; ✅ owner 23)
**Breakdown:**
- Shore Temple: Dravida vimana on the coast (✅ UNESCO).
- Kailasanatha: Kanchi structural temple (✅ owner 23).
- Phase: later structural Pallava phase, not Mahendravarman I's cave phase.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Rajasimha = Narasimhavarman II (✅ owner 23 §19.2).
**Links:** ANC-4006, ANC-4007, ANC-4014, ANC-4017.
**Trap:** Rajasimha ≠ Narasimhavarman I ≠ Mahendravarman I. Shore Temple and Kailasanatha are not from the earliest cave phase.
**Mains hook:** Structural temples show the transition from rock-cut to built monuments, and this is evidence of royal ideology and craft networks.
**Back:** *"Rajasimha builds on stone at the shore and at Kanchi."*

### Flashcard ANC-4017 — Mandagapattu and Mamandur: Mahendravarman I's cave phase
**Front:** Which caves are linked to Mahendravarman I, and what caution applies to "first temple" claims?
**Definition (learn):** The rock-cut phase associated with Mahendravarman I, including Mandagapattu, Mamandur and related caves. Attribution depends on inscriptions and style. (✅ owner 23 §17.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Sites: Mandagapattu, Mamandur (✅ owner 23).
- Phase: earliest rock-cut Pallava patronage.
- Caution: "first temple" claims require qualification.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Early 7th century (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4007, ANC-4016, ANC-4033.
**Trap:** Mandagapattu is not the Shore Temple or Kailasanatha. Those belong to the later structural phase.
**Mains hook:** The cave phase shows Pallava temple technology beginning with rock-cut form, which is useful for the 2024 PYQ on Pallava contributions.
**Back:** *"Mahendra's first cave: Mandagapattu, Mamandur."*

### Flashcard ANC-4018 — Pattadakal: Virupaksha and Mallikarjuna temples
**Front:** Who patronised the Virupaksha and Mallikarjuna temples at Pattadakal, and what do they commemorate?
**Definition (learn):** Two major Chalukya temples at Pattadakal. The Virupaksha was built under Queen Lokamahadevi after Vikramaditya II's Pallava victories, and the Mallikarjuna was sponsored by Queen Trailokyamahadevi. (✅ Wikipedia: Pattadakal; ✅ owner 23 §13.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Virupaksha: patron Queen Lokamahadevi; inscriptional name "Shri Lokeshvara Mahasila Prasada"; c. 740 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Mallikarjuna (Trailokeshwara): mid-8th-century Shiva temple; Queen Trailokyamahadevi (✅ Wikipedia).
- UNESCO: Pattadakal group inscribed 1987, reference 239 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 740 for Virupaksha; mid-8th century for Mallikarjuna (✅ Wikipedia). Owner gives c. 680–740 for Pattadakal temples (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4012, ANC-4019, ANC-4036.
**Trap:** Virupaksha at Pattadakal is Chalukya and queen-associated. It is not the Virupaksha temple of Hampi (✅ owner 23 §19.2).
**Mains hook:** Female patronage at Pattadakal shows elite donor agency within particular institutional settings, not general gender equality (✅ owner 23 §13.2).
**Back:** *"Pattadakal: Lokamahadevi's Virupaksha, Trailokya's Mallikarjuna."*

### Flashcard ANC-4019 — The Chalukya experimental landscape: Badami, Aihole, Pattadakal
**Front:** What role did Badami, Aihole and Pattadakal play in Chalukya temple experimentation?
**Definition (learn):** Three Chalukya sites that together show a range of temple plans, superstructures and religious affiliations across a long chronology. (✅ owner 23 §17.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Badami: capital and rock-cut cave complex with multiple religious affiliations (✅ owner 23).
- Aihole: diverse plans and superstructures; many temples from c. AD 610 (✅ owner 23 table); not every monument is dated to one ruler.
- Pattadakal: ceremonial complex combining northern and southern formal vocabularies (✅ owner 23).
- Pattadakal is a UNESCO site inscribed 1987 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Aihole from c. 610; Pattadakal c. 680–740 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4003, ANC-4018, ANC-4020, ANC-4036.
**Trap:** Do not treat Aihole as one datable building programme. Its chronology is long and uneven.
**Mains hook:** Use the three sites to show that Chalukya architecture was an experiment with interaction and craft movement, not a fixed style.
**Back:** *"Badami caves, Aihole labs, Pattadakal ceremonial."*

### Flashcard ANC-4020 — Nagara, Dravida and Vesara: non-ethnic labels
**Front:** Why must Nagara, Dravida and Vesara not be read as rigid ethnic or regional boxes?
**Definition (learn):** Descriptive architectural classifications for temple forms. Plan, tower, material, workshop and date must each be examined separately. (✅ owner 23 §17.2; ⚠️ owner 23 adv on overlap)
**Breakdown:**
- Nagara: a northern classificatory label.
- Dravida: a southern label.
- Vesara: a hybrid label (⚠️ standard usage; the owner does not define it).
- Caution: forms overlap regionally; early forms show experimentation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner gives no fixed dates for these labels.
**Links:** ANC-4019, ANC-4036.
**Trap:** Dravida is not "southern race". A style label does not identify a dynasty's frontier.
**Mains hook:** Monument style indicates interaction and craft movement; it cannot independently map a dynasty's frontier.
**Back:** *"Style is not ethnicity: plan, tower, material, date."*

### Flashcard ANC-4021 — Brahmadeya, agrahara and devadana: the three grant terms
**Front:** What do brahmadeya, agrahara and devadana mean, and what cautions apply to each?
**Definition (learn):** Brahmadeya is land or a village granted to one or more Brahmanas. Agrahara is a tax-privileged Brahmana settlement or endowment. Devadana is a gift assigned to a deity or temple. (✅ owner 23 §12.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Brahmadeya: Brahmana beneficiaries; rights vary by charter.
- Agrahara: tax-privileged; not every agrahara began as empty land.
- Devadana: temple control and village obligations were not uniform.
- Copper plates list boundaries, taxes, exemptions and rights over resources and labour (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Terms used across the Pallava and Chalukya period (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4024, ANC-4104 (see also Topic 26 cards), ANC-4105.
**Trap:** A grant formula does not transfer every fiscal, judicial and policing function in practice. Formula ≠ implementation.
**Mains hook:** Grants show how Brahmanization, agrarian expansion and rural hierarchy developed together (⚠️ owner 23 §7).
**Back:** *"Brahmadeya to Brahmanas, agrahara tax-free, devadana to God."*

### Flashcard ANC-4022 — Brahmanization: a negotiated process, not conversion
**Front:** What does "Brahmanization" mean in this owner, and what is it not?
**Definition (learn):** Expansion of Brahmana settlement and authority, ritual legitimation, temple and Puranic institutions, Sanskritic idioms and varna-jati claims. It is more than conversion and less than total homogenisation. (✅ owner 23 §13)
**Breakdown:**
- Court seeks legitimacy and locality access; Brahmanas receive patronage.
- Local chiefs seek rank; deities gain wider identities; cultivators and artisans sustain institutions.
- Regional languages reshape received forms (✅ owner 23 §13.1 diagram).
- Brahmana migration is visible through grants, but an inscription records a beneficiary network, not a full population map.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Process spans the Pallava–Chalukya period and beyond (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4001, ANC-4021, ANC-4026, ANC-4036.
**Trap:** Brahmanization ≠ religious conversion ≠ cultural replacement. Sanskritization is not passive assimilation.
**Mains hook:** Define Brahmanization without "conversion" and explain it through land, ritual, language and caste.
**Back:** *"Brahmanization: land, ritual, temple, varna; not conversion."*

### Flashcard ANC-4023 — The Kalabhra interlude and the Velvikudi grant (⚠️ debated)
**Front:** What is the Kalabhra episode, and why is the "Kalabhra revolt" thesis a debated reading?
**Definition (learn):** A period c. 3rd–6th century AD, chronology debated, that later dynastic records remember as a displacement before the Pallava-Pandya recovery. Its social character cannot be reconstructed securely. (⚠️ owner 23 mini-timeline)
**Breakdown:**
- Source: the later Velvikudi grant, a Pandya charter legitimising restoration of earlier grants.
- Reading: R.S. Sharma reads the grant's memory of Kalabhra cancelling Brahmana gifts as evidence of social conflict (⚠️ owner 23 §1).
- Limit: the record is much later and partisan.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 3rd–6th century AD (⚠️ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4024, ANC-4031, ANC-4036.
**Trap:** Do not present the Kalabhra as a securely documented peasant revolution. It is an interpretation built from late, partisan evidence.
**Mains hook:** Present the social-conflict reading as a debate, not a fact, and note the source's purpose.
**Back:** *"Kalabhra: late memory, not proven revolution."*

### Flashcard ANC-4024 — Land grants and peasant burdens
**Front:** How did land grants affect agrarian expansion and peasants in the Pallava–Chalukya zone?
**Definition (learn):** Grants to Brahmanas stimulated agrarian expansion in south Andhra and north Tamil Nadu, but also had adverse effects on peasants. (✅ owner 23 §1 citing R.S. Sharma)
**Breakdown:**
- Agrarian: settlement extension, tanks and wells, new cultivation.
- Social: beneficiaries, peasants, pastoralists and artisans were all active participants.
- Burden: differentiated obligations; "empty land civilised by a grant" is a court-centred description (✅ owner 23 §12.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Pallava and Chalukya grant practice across c. AD 550–750 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4021, ANC-4023, ANC-4108 (Topic 26 peasantization), ANC-4036.
**Trap:** Grants were not harmless religious charity. They reorganised dues and rural power.
**Mains hook:** A grant can both integrate land into the state and create rural hierarchy. Name one grant mechanism and one social cost.
**Back:** *"Grant clears land, burdens tillers."*

### Flashcard ANC-4025 — Pallava language and bilingual epigraphy (Prakrit, Sanskrit, Tamil, Grantha)
**Front:** How did Pallava charters divide writing between Sanskrit and Tamil, and what is Pallava Grantha?
**Definition (learn):** Early Pallava charters used Prakrit, then increasingly Sanskrit. Later records often combine Sanskrit eulogy (prashasti) with Tamil documentary portions. Pallava Grantha is a script tradition used for writing Sanskrit in the south. (✅ owner 23 §§1, 16)
**Breakdown:**
- Prakrit then Sanskrit: a change in chancery and prestige practice, not evidence of ethnicity (✅ owner 23 §16).
- Sanskrit prashasti: royal eulogy.
- Tamil documentary: regional administration.
- Pallava Grantha: a script, not a spoken language; it influenced some Southeast Asian scripts (✅ owner 23; ✅ owner 26 adv 25 for SE Asia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Early Pallava charters in Prakrit; later in Sanskrit with Tamil documentary parts (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4002, ANC-4005, ANC-4026, ANC-4090 (Southeast Asian scripts).
**Trap:** Pallava Grantha is a script tradition, not a language. Script diffusion is not proof of direct political rule.
**Mains hook:** Bilingual division of political work is evidence of elite integration and regional administration (⚠️ owner 23 §7).
**Back:** *"Sanskrit praises, Tamil records, Grantha writes."*

### Flashcard ANC-4026 — Bhakti: the Alvars and the Nayanars in the Pallava–Chalukya milieu
**Front:** Who were the Alvars and Nayanars, and why must their social role not be called an anti-caste revolution?
**Definition (learn):** Tamil devotional poets: Vaishnava Alvars and Shaiva Nayanars. Their poetry, reconstructed from poems, compilations and hagiographies, spread affective devotion to a chosen deity and sacred place. (✅ owner 23 §14)
**Breakdown:**
- Tamil composition widened religious expression; Sanskrit ritual and temples continued to matter.
- Some poems criticise Jains, Buddhists, ritual pride and social hierarchy.
- Poets came from varied locations; later temple orders reproduced rank.
- Chronology: exact dates and biographies are not equally secure (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Dates not fixed; compilation date ≠ composition date (✅ owner 23 §16).
**Links:** ANC-4022, ANC-4025, ANC-4028, ANC-4036.
**Trap:** Bhakti was not entirely anti-temple, and it was not uniformly anti-caste. Hagiographic persecution stories need corroboration.
**Mains hook:** Bhakti challenged hierarchy rhetorically, yet operated within an emerging temple-centred society. Avoid simple binaries.
**Back:** *"Alvars for Vishnu, Nayanars for Shiva, the temple as stage."*

### Flashcard ANC-4027 — Pandyas and neighbours: the third power and the Gangas, Kadambas and Alupas
**Front:** Who were the Pandyas and the other regional actors that shaped Pallava–Chalukya rivalry?
**Definition (learn):** The Pandyas (Madurai and Tirunelveli) were a weaker third participant in the Pallava–Chalukya struggle. The Western Gangas, Kadambas and Alupas were regional allies, rivals or intermediaries. (✅ owner 23 §§2, 10.4)
**Breakdown:**
- Pandyas: competitors in the far south; recovered importance alongside Pallava expansion.
- Western Gangas: Karnataka-region allies and rivals.
- Kadambas: earlier western-Deccan power whose territories entered Chalukya expansion.
- Alupas and local chiefs: dynastic power worked through regional intermediaries.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner gives no fixed dates for these neighbours (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4002, ANC-4003, ANC-4005, ANC-4028.
**Trap:** The Pandyas are not the Pallavas. The Pallava–Chalukya contest is the central axis; the Pandyas are a third force.
**Mains hook:** Political geography was shaped by intermediaries and routes, not only by two capitals.
**Back:** *"Pandyas third, Gangas and Kadambas allied or fought."*

### Flashcard ANC-4028 — Sri Lanka and the Manavamma succession: Bay of Bengal diplomacy
**Front:** What was the Pallava role in Sri Lanka, and why is it not Pallava rule?
**Definition (learn):** Pallava involvement in Sri Lankan succession struggles, especially the Manavamma tradition, shows Bay of Bengal diplomacy. It does not make Sri Lanka a Pallava province. (✅ owner 23 §10.4)
**Breakdown:**
- Mechanism: succession struggles and diplomatic intervention.
- Limit: no permanent Pallava administration of Sri Lanka.
- Context: Bay of Bengal networks (cross-link to Topic 25 and Topic 27).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner gives no exact years (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4027, ANC-4131, ANC-4036.
**Trap:** Sri Lanka involvement ≠ Sri Lanka as a province. Diplomatic intervention ≠ annexation.
**Mains hook:** Use Sri Lanka to show Pallava influence through alliance and succession politics, not territorial control.
**Back:** *"Pallava hand in Lanka's succession, not Pallava rule."*

### Flashcard ANC-4029 — Layered sovereignty: king, chief, locality
**Front:** Who held power in the Pallava–Chalukya state, and how was it layered?
**Definition (learn):** Neither polity was a uniformly bureaucratic territorial state. Royal households worked through princes, officers, tributary rulers, military retainers, local chiefs, assemblies and landed or religious beneficiaries. (✅ owner 23 §11.1)
**Breakdown:**
- King and court: war, alliance, ritual, appointments and grants.
- Province or district: rashtra, visaya, kottam, nadu (terms vary by dynasty, date and region).
- Subordinate chiefs and feudatories: tribute, troops, mediation.
- Village and town bodies: revenue, irrigation, transactions where attested.
- Brahmana and temple beneficiaries: managed endowments and dues.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner gives no fixed date (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4030, ANC-4021, ANC-4031.
**Trap:** Do not impose one fixed hierarchy of territorial units everywhere. A grant did not make every beneficiary an autonomous sovereign.
**Mains hook:** Administration depended on access to agrarian revenue, tolls, tribute and labour. A victorious raid did not equal continuous occupation.
**Back:** *"King, chief, locality: sovereignty in layers."*

### Flashcard ANC-4030 — State-model debate: feudal, segmentary, integrative, peasantization
**Front:** Which four models explain Pallava–Chalukya state formation, and what does each miss?
**Definition (learn):** Competing explanations for early medieval regional states. None alone exhausts the evidence. (✅ owner 23 §11.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Indian-feudalism: grants, fiscal immunities, intermediaries, peasant dependence. Limit: outcomes varied.
- Segmentary-state heuristic: ritual sovereignty and uneven control. Limit: developed mainly for later Chola evidence.
- Integrative/processual: alliances, cults, grants, local elites. Limit: integration can also produce hierarchy and extraction.
- Peasantization/agrarian expansion: settlement and incorporation of forest and pastoral groups. Limit: "tribe to peasant" was not one-way.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Balanced judgement: coercion, redistribution, negotiated intermediary power and ritual integration coexisted (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4001, ANC-4029, ANC-4031, ANC-4102 (Topic 26 models).
**Trap:** A segmentary model cannot simply be pasted onto every Pallava or Chalukya phase.
**Mains hook:** Use one model as the frame and at least one other as the counterpoint. Name the evidence that supports each.
**Back:** *"Feudal, segmentary, integrative, peasantised: none alone."*

### Flashcard ANC-4031 — Source method: what each evidence type can and cannot prove
**Front:** What can inscriptions, copper plates, coins, archaeology, temples and literature establish, and what can they not?
**Definition (learn):** A source hierarchy for early medieval peninsular history. Each evidence type answers different questions. (✅ owner 23 §9.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Stone inscriptions and praśastis: rulers, titles, donors, campaigns; not frontiers or neutral victory accounts.
- Copper-plate grants: donor, beneficiary, locality, fiscal concessions; not uniform enforcement.
- Coins and seals: royal names and circulation zones; not complete territorial control.
- Archaeology: production, irrigation, occupation; not ethnicity or dynastic rule alone.
- Temples and sculpture: patronage, workshops, ritual landscape; not rigid "Pallava style".
- Literature and hagiography: court culture, religious argument; not transparent reportage.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Method rule: triangulate claim, genre, date and location (✅ owner 23 §9.2).
**Links:** ANC-4005, ANC-4021, ANC-4023, ANC-4030.
**Trap:** An inscription proves that a court wanted a victory remembered. Duration and depth of control need archaeology and rival records.
**Mains hook:** This is the "source caveat" paragraph for any Pallava or Chalukya answer (⚠️ owner 23 §7).
**Back:** *"Eulogy claims, archaeology confirms, triangulate."*

### Flashcard ANC-4032 — Eastern Chalukyas of Vengi and Kubja Vishnuvardhana
**Front:** What was the Eastern Chalukya branch, and how does it relate to Pulakeshin II and Badami?
**Definition (learn):** A branch at Vengi that emerged from Pulakeshin II's eastern expansion, with Kubja Vishnuvardhana as the founding figure, from the early 7th century. (✅ owner 23 table; ⚠️ owner 23 adv on Vengi)
**Breakdown:**
- Origin: Pulakeshin II's eastern expansion.
- Ruler: Kubja Vishnuvardhana, the Vengi branch from the early 7th century.
- Later: entangled with Chola politics; later history belongs to Topic 27 (✅ owner 23 §18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Early 7th century for the branch (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4003, ANC-4004, ANC-4013, ANC-4036.
**Trap:** Eastern Chalukya ≠ Badami Chalukya. The Vengi branch survived the Rashtrakuta takeover.
**Mains hook:** Use Vengi to show regional continuity and dynastic branching after Badami's fall.
**Back:** *"Vengi outlives Badami: Kubja's branch, later Chola-tied."*

### Flashcard ANC-4033 — PYQ 2024 GS-I Q2: Pallava contribution to South Indian art and literature
**Front:** How should the 2024 GS-I question on the Pallavas of Kanchi (contribution to art and literature) be answered?
**Definition (learn):** A 10-mark estimate question (150 words) asking for the contribution of the Pallavas of Kanchi to South Indian art and literature. (✅ owner 23 §19.1; owner 23 PYQ integration)
**Breakdown:**
- Thesis: Pallava patronage created a template where royal power, sacred geography, Tamil-Sanskrit bilingualism and craft innovation intersected.
- Route: rock-cut beginnings → monolithic rathas and reliefs → structural temples → Sanskrit court writing → Tamil Bhakti and bilingual epigraphy → evidence limit → graded verdict (✅ owner 23).
- Named units: Mandagapattu; Mamallapuram rathas and relief; Shore Temple and Kailasanatha; Mattavilasa-prahasana and Tamil-Sanskrit epigraphy (✅ owner 23 §20).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2024 GS-I Q2, estimate, 10 marks, 150 words (✅ owner 23 PYQ integration).
**Links:** ANC-4007, ANC-4008, ANC-4015, ANC-4016, ANC-4017, ANC-4025, ANC-4026.
**Trap:** An answer limited to temples is incomplete; the directive combines art and literature (✅ owner 23 adv §4).
**Mains hook:** The contribution is real but mainly monumental and epigraphic. Limit: survival biases attribution toward kings.
**Back:** *"Caves, rathas, temples, Sanskrit farce, Tamil charters."*

### Flashcard ANC-4034 — PYQ 2020 Prelims Q24: chronology of Pratihara, Pallava, Chola and Pala
**Front:** How should the 2020 Prelims chronology question on Pratihara, Pallava, Chola and Pala dynasties be routed?
**Definition (learn):** An objective question asking for the chronological order of these dynasties. The owner's executable route is an inferred order; the official key is not held locally. (✅ owner 23 §19.1; ⚠️ key unavailable)
**Breakdown:**
- Owner route: Mahendravarman I (Pallava, early 7th century) → Gopala (Pala) → Bhoja (Pratihara) → Parantaka I (Chola).
- Status: the owner labels this order "inferred" because the local official key is unavailable.
- Cross-routed to the Pratihara-Pala, Pallava and imperial Chola owners.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mahendravarman I early 7th century; Parantaka I c. 907–955 (✅ owner 23; ✅ owner 27).
**Links:** ANC-4007, ANC-4124, ANC-4127, ANC-4035.
**Trap:** Do not state the answer as official. The owner marks it as an inferred route.
**Mains hook:** Not a Mains question; use this as a chronology ladder test.
**Back:** *"Pallava first, Pala and Pratihara next, Chola later; key not held."*

### Flashcard ANC-4035 — Chronology ladder: the Pallava–Chalukya war sequence
**Front:** What is the sequence of conflict between the Pulakeshin II, Pallava and later Rashtrakuta actors?
**Definition (learn):** A conflict ladder showing how the rivalry moved through attacks, counter-attacks and succession changes. It is a sequence, not a two-state final. (✅ owner 23 §10.3)
**Breakdown:**
- Pulakeshin II expands across the Deccan.
- Pallava pressure under Mahendravarman I.
- Narasimhavarman I counter-campaign; Vatapi captured (c. 642).
- Chalukya recovery under Vikramaditya I.
- Renewed attacks; Vikramaditya II at Kanchi.
- Rashtrakutas displace the Badami Chalukyas c. 753; Pallavas face new rivals.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Pulakeshin II c. 609–642; Vatapi c. 642; Badami end c. 753 (✅ owner 23; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** ANC-4004, ANC-4006, ANC-4011, ANC-4012, ANC-4013.
**Trap:** A campaign success is not evidence of permanent administration. Rivalry was not a timeless ethnic struggle.
**Mains hook:** Use the ladder to show how access to routes, fertile zones, allies and capitals changed with each phase.
**Back:** *"Pulakeshin, Mahendra, Narasimha, Vikramaditya I and II, Rashtrakutas."*

### Flashcard ANC-4036 — Trap pack: site–dynasty pairings and grant myths
**Front:** Which high-yield statement traps in this owner most often confuse the site, dynasty or social meaning?
**Definition (learn):** The owner's named traps, collected so that each pair is recalled together. (✅ owner 23 §5, §19.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Aihole praśasti belongs to the Chalukyas (Pulakeshin II/Ravikirti), not the Pallavas.
- Mahabalipuram is Pallava, not Chalukya.
- Virupaksha at Pattadakal is Chalukya and queen-associated; it is not Hampi's Virupaksha.
- Land grants were not harmless charity; they changed rural power and peasant obligations.
- The Kalabhra "peasant revolution" is a debated reading of late evidence.
- Pallava Grantha is a script, not a spoken language.
- Mamalla = Narasimhavarman I; Rajasimha = Narasimhavarman II.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4002, ANC-4005, ANC-4014, ANC-4018, ANC-4023, ANC-4016, ANC-4025.
**Trap:** Mixing site and dynasty is the commonest error. Use the rule: Chalukya = Aihole, Badami, Pattadakal; Pallava = Kanchi, Mahabalipuram.
**Mains hook:** Use source and site precision to show command of the subject.
**Back:** *"Aihole Chalukya, Mamalla Pallava; Virupaksha not Hampi."*

### Flashcard ANC-4037 — PYQ 2025 Prelims Q14: titles Mattavilasa, Vichitrachitta, Gunabhara
**Front:** Which ruler bore the titles Mattavilasa, Vichitrachitta and Gunabhara, and how should the option set be routed?
**Definition (learn):** An objective question on a Pallava ruler's titles. The owner's answer is Mahendravarman I. (✅ owner 23 §19.1; ✅ owner 23 PYQ integration)
**Breakdown:**
- Titles: Mattavilasa, Vichitrachitta, Gunabhara (✅ owner 23).
- Ruler: Mahendravarman I (ANC-4007).
- Distinguish from: Simhavishnu, Narasimhavarman I Mamalla / Vatapikonda, and the earlier Simhavarman (ANC-4006, ANC-4009).
- Key status: official Set-A key available locally; the owner does not record the answer letter (✅ owner 23 PYQ integration).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Early 7th century (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** ANC-4007, ANC-4006, ANC-4008, ANC-4009.
**Trap:** Mamalla (Narasimhavarman I) is not the holder of Mattavilasa. Vatapikonda is a Narasimhavarman I title.
**Mains hook:** Not a Mains item; the route tests title-to-ruler recall.
**Back:** *"Mattavilasa, Vichitrachitta, Gunabhara: Mahendra, not Mamalla."*

### GROUP — Developments in Philosophy (basic 24; advanced 24)

### Flashcard ANC-4038 — Philosophy grows out of society: the RS Sharma frame
**Front:** How does R.S. Sharma link the development of philosophy to society, and what caution does Upinder Singh add?
**Definition (learn):** Philosophical development is read as a product of a society where state and varna order were established and thinkers debated the four goals of life. (✅ owner 24 §1 citing R.S. Sharma)
**Breakdown:**
- Society: state and varna order.
- Goals: dharma, artha, kama, moksha.
- Materialist currents: link to expanding economy, rational inquiry and metal money (⚠️ owner 24 causal link is interpretive).
- Caution: the six systems were presented together by the early Christian era in Sharma, but Upinder Singh says the grouped list crystallised later (⚠️ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Materialist currents 500 BC–AD 300 (✅ owner 24 mini-timeline).
**Links:** ANC-4039, ANC-4050, ANC-4064.
**Trap:** Philosophy was not detached from material conditions, but the exact causal link is interpretive, not proven.
**Mains hook:** Discuss philosophical plurality, then qualify with the source dating problems.
**Back:** *"Society made the questions; debate made the schools."*

### Flashcard ANC-4039 — The four purusharthas: dharma, artha, kama, moksha
**Front:** What are the four goals of human life, and how did their status change over time?
**Definition (learn):** The purusharthas are dharma (social order and righteous conduct), artha (economic resources), kama (physical or sensual pleasure) and moksha (deliverance from the cycle of rebirth). (✅ owner 24)
**Breakdown:**
- Dharma: regulation of social order.
- Artha: material well-being.
- Kama: sensual pleasure, legitimate when governed by dharma.
- Moksha: liberation; added to an earlier three-goal formulation (✅ owner 24 adv, Upinder Singh).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Four-goal synthesis is a mature, not original, formulation (✅ owner 24 §9.1).
**Links:** ANC-4040, ANC-4049, ANC-4052.
**Trap:** Moksha was not the only goal; dharma, artha and kama were also recognised.
**Mains hook:** Purusharthas offer a moderated social ethic: neither pure renunciation nor unrestrained materialism.
**Back:** *"Dharma keeps order, artha feeds, kama delights, moksha frees."*

### Flashcard ANC-4040 — Worldly-goal texts vs moksha-centred darshanas
**Front:** Which texts dealt with worldly problems, and which made moksha central?
**Definition (learn):** Arthashastra, Dharmashastra and Kamasutra addressed mainly worldly problems. Darshana texts made moksha central. (✅ owner 24 §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Worldly: Arthashastra (artha), Dharmashastra (dharma), Kamasutra (kama).
- Liberation-centred: darshana traditions (Samkhya, Yoga, Nyaya, Vaisheshika, Mimamsa, Vedanta).
- Overlap: moderated pursuit of worldly goals was legitimate under dharma.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner gives no dates for these texts.
**Links:** ANC-4039, ANC-4041.
**Trap:** Do not claim that artha and kama were rejected in Indian thought. They were legitimate under dharma (✅ owner 24 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Worldly and liberation goals coexisted in one intellectual field.
**Back:** *"Artha-kama-dharma texts for the world; darshanas for release."*

### Flashcard ANC-4041 — Samkhya: count, prakriti and purusha
**Front:** What is Samkhya, what do "count" and "prakriti/purusha" mean, and is Samkhya theistic?
**Definition (learn):** A dualist system whose name means "count" or "enumeration". It distinguishes prakriti (primordial nature) from purusha (consciousness). Early Samkhya did not require divine agency for creation. (✅ owner 24 §§1–2)
**Breakdown:**
- Metaphysics: prakriti evolves; purusha is distinct.
- Early form: no creator God needed.
- Textual layers: old strands precede Ishvarakrishna's Samkhya Karika, broadly fourth century AD (✅ owner 24 adv §13).
- Relation to Yoga: Samkhya supplied metaphysical vocabulary to Yoga.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Samkhya Karika broadly fourth century AD (✅ owner 24 adv).
**Links:** ANC-4042, ANC-4049, ANC-4061.
**Trap:** Samkhya is not necessarily theistic. Astika does not equal theist (see ANC-4049).
**Mains hook:** Samkhya's dualism and its non-theistic early form show that orthodox labels hide variety.
**Back:** *"Samkhya counts two: prakriti acts, purusha watches."*

### Flashcard ANC-4042 — Yoga: disciplined practice and the Yoga Sutra
**Front:** What does Yoga mean in the six systems, and what is the status of Patanjali's Yoga Sutra?
